Mahāyāna Scriptures › Treatise on the Stages of Yogic Practice › Fascicle 47
Fascicle 47 · 瑜伽師地論卷第四十七 · True Marks, the Four Dharmas, Excellent Aspiration, and the Abode of Utmost Joy

The Five Marks of a True Bodhisattva, and the First Ground

With chapter eighteen closed, the Bodhisattva Stage opens its second major section — the yoga foundation of sustained practice. This fascicle carries that section from its very beginning through a bodhisattva's five true marks (compassion, loving speech, valor, open-handed giving, and understanding the teaching's own hidden meaning), the four dharmas for householder and renunciate alike, the seven aspects of compassion and fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration, and on into the chapter on the bodhisattva's thirteen abodes — closing the abode of resolute practice entirely and giving the abode of utmost joy, the first ground itself, a complete eightfold account: its well-determined vow, its arising bodhicitta, its ten great vows, its ten purifying dharmas, its guidance toward the grounds still to come, its cultivation of wholesome roots, its rebirth as a wheel-turning king, and its majestic power.

Translator: Ru-Yi Meditation Center Status: complete Source: Taishō T30, no. 1579, fascicle 47 Commentary guide: Master Cháng Bǎi's lecture notes

1. Section: The Bodhisattva Stage's Second Section Opens — Five True Marks of a Bodhisattva, Each to Be Examined Five Ways

With chapter eighteen now closed, the treatise opens the Bodhisattva Stage's second of four major yoga sections — the yoga foundation concerned with sustained practice in accordance with the teaching, comprising four chapters: the chapter on a bodhisattva's true marks, the chapter on the two divisions of householder and renunciate bodhisattva, the chapter on excellent aspiration, and the chapter on the bodhisattva abodes, describing the thirteen stages of bodhisattva practice — this fascicle covering the first three chapters in full and opening the fourth as far as its first abode, the abode of excellent aspiration. The first chapter, on true marks, opens by asking what genuinely constitutes a true bodhisattva's mark, then answers in summary verse: five marks — compassion, loving speech, valor, open-handed giving, and understanding the teaching's own profound hidden meaning — each of which is, in turn, to be examined from five further angles: its own intrinsic nature, the basis it depends on, the beneficial fruit it produces, the sequence in which the five marks are cultivated, and how each mark relates to and is included within the six perfections.

Chinese Source

本地分中菩薩地第二持隨法瑜伽處菩薩相品第一

云何真實諸菩薩相？嗢柁南曰：真實諸菩薩　五種相應知　自性依處果　次第攝五轉

謂諸菩薩有五真實菩薩之相，若成就者墮菩薩數。何等為五？一者、哀愍，二者、愛語，三者、勇猛，四者、舒手惠施，五者、能解甚深義理密意。

如是五法，當知一一各有五轉。一者、自性，二者、依處，三者、果利，四者、次第，五者、相攝。

Ru-Yi Translation

Within the Basic Section, the Bodhisattva Stage, the second yoga foundation of sustained practice, chapter one: the marks of a bodhisattva.

What are the true marks of a bodhisattva? A summary verse states: "Bodhisattvas' true marks; one should know they are five kinds; intrinsic nature, basis, and fruit; sequence, and inclusion — five turnings."

This means that bodhisattvas have five true marks; anyone who has accomplished them falls within the number of bodhisattvas. What are these five? First, compassion; second, loving speech; third, valor; fourth, open-handed giving; fifth, understanding profound meaning and hidden intent.

One should understand that each of these five factors, in turn, has five further turnings: first, intrinsic nature; second, basis; third, beneficial fruit; fourth, sequence; fifth, mutual inclusion.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi opens by orienting the reader to a genuine structural transition: the Bodhisattva Stage's first major section, the initial yoga foundation just completed across fascicles 44 through 46, comprised eighteen chapters covering everything from the bodhisattva lineage through the qualities that define a bodhisattva's character. Now begins the second of four such sections, this one addressing the yoga foundation of sustained, ongoing practice in accordance with the teaching, and comprising four further chapters: the marks of a bodhisattva (this chapter), the divisions between householder and renunciate bodhisattvas, excellent aspiration, and the bodhisattva abodes — the last of which unfolds the full thirteen stages of bodhisattva practice, though this fascicle will only reach its very first stage before concluding. He notes in passing that the treatise's overall architecture continues beyond even this second section into two further major divisions, covering birth, being gathered in by a teacher, the bodhisattva grounds in their fullest technical detail, and finally bodhisattva conduct itself — a vast structure of which this fascicle opens only the next modest portion.

He then previews the chapter's own inner logic: five marks make a bodhisattva genuinely a bodhisattva rather than merely someone who has taken the name — compassion, meaning not merely an inward feeling of sympathy but a disposition that actually issues in concrete benefit and ease for beings; loving speech, meaning language that goes beyond simply comforting words to include teaching the profound, genuinely beneficial truths beings actually need to hear; valor, meaning vigorous, undaunted diligence in bringing both oneself and others to spiritual maturity; open-handed giving, meaning a readiness to give material wealth, the teaching itself, and freedom from fear alike, without restriction; and understanding profound meaning and hidden intent, meaning the four kinds of unobstructed understanding that let a bodhisattva penetrate the deepest teachings of emptiness and dependent origination in the scriptures, the subtleties of what may be transgressed and restored in the monastic code, and the specific technical vocabulary the treatises use to remove beings' doubts about the profound intentions underlying the Buddha's own words. He stresses that a bodhisattva lacking any one of these five falls short of being a bodhisattva in truth, however sincerely the aspiration for awakening may have been aroused — the same insistence on substance over mere title that closed chapter eighteen now becomes, in this new chapter, the entire organizing question. The treatise turns next to examining each of these five marks in turn, beginning with the first of the five angles, intrinsic nature, applied first to compassion.

2. Section: The Intrinsic Nature of Compassion — Both an Inward Wish for Beings' Benefit and Its Actual Expression in Conduct

The treatise begins examining the first of the five true marks, compassion, from the angle of its own intrinsic nature, dividing it in brief into two components: one residing in aspiration, and one residing in actual conduct. The component residing in aspiration is that bodhisattvas hold, toward all sentient beings, both the aspiration to bring benefit and the aspiration to bring ease — sincerely wishing, in their own hearts, that beings' thinking would improve for the better and that beings' bodies and minds would find rest from suffering; this inward wish alone is already called compassion. The component residing in actual conduct is that bodhisattvas, in accordance with that very aspiration, according to whatever strength and capacity they actually possess, bring concrete benefit to beings through body and speech — not merely wishing beings well but actually acting, through physical deeds and spoken words, to bring about the benefit and ease wished for; this too is called compassion.

Chinese Source

應知此中，哀愍自性略有二種。一、在意樂，二、在正行。

在意樂者，謂諸菩薩，於諸有情利益意樂、安樂意樂，是名哀愍。

在正行者，謂諸菩薩，於諸有情如所意樂，隨力隨能身語饒益，是名哀愍。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should understand that, here, compassion's own intrinsic nature is, in brief, of two kinds: first, residing in aspiration; second, residing in actual conduct.

Residing in aspiration means that bodhisattvas hold, toward all sentient beings, the aspiration to benefit them and the aspiration to bring them ease. This is called compassion.

Residing in actual conduct means that bodhisattvas, in accordance with that very aspiration, according to their own strength and capacity, bring concrete benefit to beings through body and speech. This too is called compassion.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi stresses that this twofold division of compassion's own intrinsic nature — aspiration and conduct together — is not incidental but central to the entire chapter's insistence on substance over mere sentiment: a bodhisattva's compassion must first exist as a genuine inward wish that beings' own thinking improve and that their bodies and minds find rest, but a wish alone, however sincere, remains incomplete without the second component, actual conduct that puts that wish into practice through real bodily and verbal action.

He explains why the aspiration itself is framed specifically around beings' thinking rather than only their circumstances: a bodhisattva rescuing beings leads always with wisdom, recognizing that most of the poverty, disaster, and suffering beings encounter traces back, at root, to the karma generated by their own mistaken thinking — so genuine compassion does not stop at solving a being's immediate material problem but reaches further, guiding that being's own thinking and outlook toward abandoning the unwholesome and cultivating the wholesome, thereby resolving the problem at its actual root rather than merely its surface symptom. He notes that these two components, the aspiration to benefit thinking and the aspiration to bring bodily and mental ease, together constitute what the treatise means by a bodhisattva's compassionate mind, while the conduct-component simply describes how that same compassionate aspiration, once genuinely held, naturally and necessarily expresses itself outwardly whenever a bodhisattva's own wisdom and merit provide the actual capacity to help. The treatise turns next to the intrinsic nature of the second mark, loving speech.

3. Section: The Intrinsic Nature of Loving Speech, of Valor, and of Open-Handed Giving

The second mark, loving speech, has, as its own intrinsic nature, the three kinds already explained earlier in the treatise's own chapter on the means of gathering beings: comforting speech, joyfully congratulatory speech, and beneficial speech. Comforting speech follows ordinary worldly courtesy — greeting others, inquiring after their well-being, offering words of consolation whenever something unwelcome has occurred, so that a being's spirits are lifted. Joyfully congratulatory speech offers genuine words of celebration whenever something wonderful or fortunate has happened to a being, bringing forth genuine joy. And beneficial speech actually teaches the profoundly beneficial truths of the teaching itself, so that a being who hears it gains real, lasting benefit — a gift of the teaching bringing benefit and ease to all beings. The third mark, valor, has as its own intrinsic nature a resolute, unshakeable firmness, entirely without timidity or weakness, possessed of tremendous inner strength — a bodhisattva who, faced with whatever difficulty arises in bringing both self and others to spiritual maturity, remains utterly undaunted, never retreating into cowardice or settling for the lesser vehicles, firmly resolved on nothing less than buddhahood itself. And the fourth mark, open-handed giving, has as its own intrinsic nature both a vast, expansive disposition toward giving and a disposition toward giving entirely free of any taint — giving broadly to every being without distinguishing friend from foe from stranger, and giving with a mind untroubled by affliction, unattached to either love or hatred, giving with the threefold emptiness of giver, gift, and recipient equally in mind.

Chinese Source

愛語自性，謂如前說若慰喻語、若慶悅語、若勝益語，當知如前攝事品說。

勇猛自性，謂諸菩薩剛決堅固，無所怯劣，有大勢力。

若諸菩薩廣大施性、無染施性，是名舒手惠施自性。

Ru-Yi Translation

Loving speech's own intrinsic nature is, as already explained earlier, comforting speech, joyfully congratulatory speech, and beneficial speech; one should understand this exactly as explained earlier in the Chapter on the Means of Gathering Beings.

Valor's own intrinsic nature is that bodhisattvas are resolute and firm, entirely without timidity or weakness, possessed of tremendous strength.

If bodhisattvas possess both a vast, expansive disposition toward giving and a disposition toward giving free of any taint, this is called open-handed giving's own intrinsic nature.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi refers the loving-speech mark directly back to the treatise's own fuller treatment in fascicle 43's chapter on the means of gathering beings, reprising the threefold distinction briefly: comforting speech as ordinary, courteous inquiry after another's well-being and comfort in adversity; joyfully congratulatory speech as genuine celebration of another's good fortune; and beneficial speech as the gift of the teaching itself, the most valuable of the three since it alone offers lasting rather than merely momentary benefit.

He explains valor as combining wisdom with an unbending resolve toward the six perfections and toward the twin tasks of maturing both oneself and others — such a bodhisattva, encountering hardship, neither wavers nor shrinks back into timidity, and above all never settles for the lesser attainment of the disciple's or solitary awakener's own more limited vehicles, holding fast instead to nothing short of complete buddhahood; this unshakeable inner strength is precisely what "valor" names. He explains open-handed giving's two components as, respectively, breadth and purity: a truly vast giving disposition draws no distinction between friend, enemy, or stranger, extending generosity to every being alike, while an untainted giving disposition gives with a mind free of the afflictions of attachment and aversion, ideally accomplished with the classical threefold emptiness — no fixed notion of a giver, no fixed notion of a gift, and no fixed notion of a recipient — in mind throughout the act of giving itself. The treatise turns next to the fifth and most technically demanding mark, understanding profound meaning and hidden intent, unpacked through the classical four kinds of unobstructed understanding.

4. Section: Understanding Profound Meaning and Hidden Intent — the Four Kinds of Unobstructed Understanding in Full

The fifth mark, understanding profound meaning and hidden intent, has as its own intrinsic nature the bodhisattva's own four kinds of unobstructed understanding, together with the correct, applied wisdom that these four undistortedly bring forth. Unobstructed understanding of the teaching means that bodhisattvas, with respect to every teaching and every one of its varied gateways, possess an unobstructed, irreversible wisdom born of cultivation regarding both its full range of particular characteristics and its underlying universal nature — equally described as the undeluded insight, arising from correctly realizing the differing names given to every kind of phenomenon, together with the meditative states of mind joined with it. Unobstructed understanding of meaning means the same unobstructed, irreversible cultivated wisdom applied instead to every phenomenon's varied particular marks, rather than to its names — the undeluded insight arising from correctly realizing the many differing characteristics found within each and every phenomenon. Unobstructed understanding of language means this same wisdom applied to the etymological explanation of every term used for every phenomenon — the undeluded insight arising from correctly realizing the explanatory derivation of technical vocabulary itself. And unobstructed understanding of eloquent exposition means this same wisdom applied to distinguishing and expounding every category of phenomena without exception — the undeluded insight, joined with meditative states of mind, that arises from correctly realizing how thoroughly to penetrate all phenomena, engaged in extensively for the sake of benefiting others through actual teaching.

Chinese Source

若諸菩薩四無礙解，及即於彼無倒引發正加行智，是名能解甚深義理密意自性。

《披》若諸菩薩四無礙解等者：菩薩所修菩薩四無礙解，菩提分品已說其相。（陵本四十五卷二頁3617）由此善能宣說正法，於諸法中起智加行，名即於彼無倒引發正加行智。

Ru-Yi Translation

If bodhisattvas possess the four kinds of unobstructed understanding, together with the correct, applied wisdom undistortedly brought forth by them, this is called understanding profound meaning and hidden intent's own intrinsic nature.

Commentary: Regarding "if bodhisattvas possess the four kinds of unobstructed understanding" and the like — the bodhisattva's own cultivation of the four kinds of unobstructed understanding has already had its characteristics explained in the chapter on the factors of awakening. Because of this, a bodhisattva is skillfully able to expound the true teaching, giving rise to applied wisdom with respect to all phenomena — this is called the correct, applied wisdom undistortedly brought forth by them.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi cross-references the treatise's own two earlier discussions of this same correct, applied wisdom — in fascicle 45's chapter on the factors of awakening and again in fascicle 48 — as the wisdom a bodhisattva brings forth specifically after having already accomplished the four kinds of unobstructed understanding, skillfully able to expound phenomena as a result, while noting that the Abhidharma-mahāvibhāṣā-śāstra, fascicle 180, frames the same relationship in the opposite causal direction: a practitioner's own sustained habituation with the Buddha's words — their names, their meaning, their etymological explanation, and their exposition — is precisely what gives rise to the four kinds of unobstructed understanding as applied wisdom's own result. He treats both framings as simply describing the same tight, mutually reinforcing relationship between the four kinds of understanding and their fruit in actual skillful teaching, rather than a genuine disagreement.

He then draws out, following the treatise's own fuller explanation in fascicle 38, two complementary formulations the treatise gives for each of the four unobstructed understandings. Unobstructed understanding of the teaching, in its first formulation, is the cultivated wisdom, unobstructed and never regressing, that bodhisattvas bring to every teaching and its varied approaches, with respect to both a phenomenon's full range of particular qualities and its shared universal nature; in its second formulation, it is simply the undeluded insight born of correctly realizing the differing names attached to every category of phenomenon, together with the corresponding states of concentrated mind. Unobstructed understanding of meaning, in its first formulation, is this same unobstructed cultivated wisdom applied to every phenomenon's varied particular marks rather than to its name; in its second, it is the undeluded insight born of correctly realizing the many different characteristics found within each phenomenon.

Unobstructed understanding of language, in its first formulation, is this same cultivated wisdom applied to the etymological derivation of every term describing every phenomenon; in its second, it is simply the undeluded insight born of correctly realizing how technical vocabulary itself is derived and explained. And unobstructed understanding of eloquent exposition, in its first formulation, is this same cultivated wisdom applied to every category and classification of phenomena without exception; in its second, it is the undeluded insight, joined with meditative absorption, born of correctly realizing how to penetrate all phenomena thoroughly, and is specifically exercised at length whenever a bodhisattva teaches for the direct benefit of others. He notes that a bodhisattva who has genuinely cultivated all four — through the four investigations, the four kinds of genuine wisdom, sustained correct reflection, and the calm-abiding and insight already explained at length in earlier fascicles regarding the profound emptiness and dependent origination of phenomena, the subtleties of what constitutes transgression and restoration within the monastic precepts, and the specific technical vocabulary and interpretive method the treatises use to resolve beings' doubts about the Buddha's own deepest intentions, exactly as the Compendium on Textual Exposition explains in fascicles 81 and 82 — gradually perfects all four kinds of unobstructed understanding together, and this fully accomplished capacity is precisely what the fifth mark, understanding profound meaning and hidden intent, describes. With this, the intrinsic nature of all five true marks of a bodhisattva has now been explained in full; the treatise turns next to the second of the five angles, the basis each of these five marks depends upon.

5. Section: The Basis of Compassion Opens — Beings Who Suffer, and Beings Who Delight in Wrongdoing

The treatise turns to the second of the five angles, the basis each mark depends on, beginning with compassion: one should understand that a bodhisattva's compassion rests, in brief, on five kinds of beings — beings who suffer, beings of wrong conduct, heedless beings, wrongly-practicing beings, and beings still latent with affliction, the first four common to ordinary beings and non-Buddhist practitioners alike, the fifth spanning both ordinary and noble beings within the Buddhist teaching itself. The first basis, beings who suffer, names those in the hells, among the hungry ghosts, and among animals, who are ceaselessly, continuously oppressed by unrelenting painful sensation, their bodies and minds harmed without any respite — such suffering beings are the very first object of a bodhisattva's compassion. The second basis, beings of wrong conduct, names beings whose present circumstances may not be fixed toward suffering but who habitually engage in unwholesome conduct of body, speech, and mind, delighting and dwelling contentedly in wrongdoing — exemplified by those who raise and then slaughter sheep, pigs, and chickens as their very livelihood, undisciplined in conduct, whose determined commitment to such unwholesome deeds guarantees a future fall into the lower realms.

Chinese Source

當知菩薩哀愍依處，略有五種。何等為五？一、有苦有情，二、惡行有情，三、放逸有情，四、邪行有情，五、煩惱隨眠有情。

那落迦等所有有情，皆為苦受連綿相續逼切而轉，如是名為有苦有情。

或復有情雖非定苦，而多現行諸身惡行、諸語惡行、諸意惡行，於諸惡中喜樂安住。所謂屠養羊豬雞等不律儀輩。如是名為惡行有情。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should understand that a bodhisattva's basis for compassion is, in brief, five kinds. What are these five? First, beings who suffer; second, beings of wrong conduct; third, heedless beings; fourth, wrongly-practicing beings; fifth, beings still latent with affliction.

All beings in the hells and the like are ceaselessly, continuously oppressed and turned about by painful sensation. Such beings are called beings who suffer.

There are also beings who, though not fixed toward suffering, frequently engage in unwholesome conduct of body, unwholesome conduct of speech, and unwholesome conduct of mind, delighting and dwelling contentedly in wrongdoing — such as those undisciplined in conduct who raise and slaughter sheep, pigs, chickens, and the like. Such beings are called beings of wrong conduct.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi opens by observing that beings and buddhas exist only in mutual dependence — without beings, there could be no bodhisattva at all, and without a bodhisattva's path, no buddha; a bodhisattva's compassion itself only ever arises in dependence on some actual being toward whom it is directed, which is precisely why this second angle asks specifically what beings serve as that necessary basis. He notes the treatise's own observation that the first four kinds of beings named here are found equally among ordinary worldly people and non-Buddhist practitioners, while the fifth kind spans both ordinary and noble beings specifically within the Buddhist teaching — a structure he will return to once all five have been explained.

He identifies the first basis, beings who suffer, as specifically the inhabitants of the three lower realms — hell-beings, hungry ghosts, and animals — whose suffering is not occasional but genuinely unrelenting, one moment of pain flowing into the next without any actual relief, and it is precisely this unbroken intensity of suffering that makes such beings the most immediate object of a bodhisattva's compassion. He then illustrates the second basis, beings of wrong conduct, with the treatise's own example of professional butchers, and draws out the Decisions section's own threefold grading of undisciplined conduct from fascicle 53: the lowest grade describes someone simply born into a family whose livelihood happens to be butchery, without any strong personal inclination toward such unwholesome work; the middling grade describes someone who has come to personally endorse taking up such a livelihood but has not yet actually begun; and the highest grade describes someone who, purely to sustain their own life, actively delights in, approves of, and commits to killing as a means of livelihood through deliberately harsh methods, continuing this practice for as long as they live. Beings of any of these three grades, having sown the causes of future suffering right now, remain, he stresses, no less an object of a bodhisattva's compassion than those already suffering the results of past unwholesome karma.

6. Section: Heedless Beings, Wrongly-Practicing Beings, and Beings Still Latent With Affliction — Closing the Basis of Compassion

The third basis, heedless beings, names those who, though neither fixed toward suffering nor engaged in unwholesome conduct, remain infatuated with and absorbed in sensory pleasure, forever delighting in dwelling amid entertainers, song and dance, and playful glances to amuse themselves — those given over to the pleasures of the senses, easily mistaking such entertainment for genuine art while it in fact stirs up craving and becomes the very root of continued samsaric wandering. The fourth basis, wrongly-practicing beings, names those who, neither suffering, unwholesome, nor heedless, nonetheless rely on false views to practice all manner of painful, misguided paths toward what they mistakenly believe is liberation — renouncing sensory desire and taking ordination within a wrongly-taught doctrine and discipline, holding one or another of the sixty-two false views concerning self and person. And the fifth basis, beings still latent with affliction, names those who, though free of the first four conditions altogether, remain, whether fully bound or not fully bound, still followed by the latent seeds of affliction — specifically genuinely practicing, wholesome ordinary beings and noble ones still in training within the Buddhist teaching itself. With this fifth basis, the treatise concludes: these five kinds of beings together comprise the entire basis and object upon which a bodhisattva's compassion depends and arises, with nothing beyond these five to be added or found lacking.

Chinese Source

或復有情雖非定苦及行惡行，而於諸欲耽著受用，常樂安住種種俳優、歌舞、笑睇，以自娛樂。所謂一類受欲塵者。如是名為放逸有情。

或復有情雖非定苦、行惡、放逸，而依妄見修行種種苦解脫行。謂捨諸欲，於惡說法毗奈耶中而出家者。如是名為邪行有情。

或復有情雖非定苦，廣說乃至非修邪行，而或具縛、或不具縛，為諸煩惱之所隨眠。謂正修行賢善異生及諸有學。是名煩惱隨眠有情。

《披》謂正修行賢善異生等者：謂隨信行、或隨法行補特伽羅，趣向諦觀，而未見諦，名正修行賢善異生。從預流果乃至阿羅漢向，名諸有學。如次釋前具縛、或不具縛應知。

是名菩薩所有哀愍五種依處，由此依處、由此所緣，哀愍而轉。除此無有若過若增。

Ru-Yi Translation

There are also beings who, though not fixed toward suffering nor engaged in unwholesome conduct, are infatuated with and given over to sensory desires, forever delighting in dwelling amid entertainers, song and dance, and playful glances, amusing themselves. These are beings absorbed in the pleasures of the senses. Such beings are called heedless beings.

There are also beings who, though not fixed toward suffering, unwholesome conduct, or heedlessness, rely on false views to practice all manner of painful paths toward what they take to be liberation — renouncing sensory desires and taking ordination within a wrongly-taught doctrine and discipline. Such beings are called wrongly-practicing beings.

There are also beings who, though not fixed toward suffering, and so on up through not practicing wrong paths, are nevertheless, whether fully bound or not fully bound, followed by the latent seeds of affliction. These are genuinely practicing, wholesome ordinary beings and noble ones still in training. These are called beings still latent with affliction.

Commentary: Regarding "genuinely practicing, wholesome ordinary beings" and the like — those who follow through faith or follow through the teaching, heading toward direct contemplation of the truths but not yet having seen the truths, are called genuinely practicing, wholesome ordinary beings. Those from the fruit of stream-entry through the path toward arhatship are called noble ones still in training. "Fully bound" and "not fully bound" should be understood as explaining these two in turn, respectively.

These, then, are the bodhisattva's five bases for compassion. It is by depending on these bases, by taking these as objects, that compassion turns and functions. Beyond these five, there is nothing to be added and nothing lacking.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes how easily the third basis, heedless beings, can be mistaken for something harmless or even admirable by worldly standards — ordinary people often regard the entertainments named here, performance, song, dance, and playful glances, as legitimate art, yet examined through the lens of the teaching, such entertainments readily stir up craving in the mind, leading beings to become lost in and infatuated with sensory pleasure precisely because they seem so innocuous; since craving is itself the root of continued wandering through birth-and-death, a bodhisattva's compassion extends with equal seriousness to beings absorbed in such heedless pleasure-seeking, not only to those obviously suffering or doing evident harm.

He explains the fourth basis, wrongly-practicing beings, as specifically non-Buddhist renunciates who have genuinely given up worldly desire and even taken up an ascetic discipline, yet do so grounded in one or another of the classical sixty-two false views regarding self and person — such beings' very sincerity and discipline make them no less deserving of a bodhisattva's compassionate outreach, since sincere effort misdirected by wrong view still leads only to further suffering rather than genuine liberation. For the fifth basis, he unpacks the commentary's own careful distinction between two kinds of practitioner following two different temperaments — those who follow primarily through faith, whose strong confidence lets them take up practice simply on a good teacher's word, and those who follow primarily through the teaching itself, whose stronger wisdom-faculty leads them to study the scriptures directly and only commit to practice after their own careful reflection — both alike still heading toward, but not yet having attained, direct realization of the four noble truths, and both alike called genuinely practicing, wholesome ordinary beings; while noble ones still in training span every stage from stream-entry through the very threshold of arhatship, the fifteen mind-moments preceding stream-entry itself already counted as heading toward that first fruit. He notes that "fully bound," used earlier of ordinary beings generally, here refers specifically to these genuinely practicing, wholesome ordinary beings, still bound by every affliction of the three realms, while "not fully bound" refers to the noble ones still in training, who have already begun severing them.

Surveying the completed fivefold scheme, he observes that these five bases move in a certain natural progression from the most obviously suffering to the most subtly still-imperfect: from beings openly suffering in the lower realms, through beings actively creating future suffering, through beings dulled by pleasure, through beings sincerely but wrongly seeking liberation, to beings genuinely on the Buddhist path yet still carrying affliction's own latent seeds — a bodhisattva's compassion, properly cultivated, must extend to every one of these five without exception, since no being, however apparently advanced or apparently comfortable, is yet entirely free of some condition calling for compassionate concern. The treatise turns next to the basis of the second mark, loving speech.

7. Section: The Basis of Loving Speech — Five Kinds of Speech Grounded in the Four Unobstructed Understandings, Closing the Second Mark's Basis in Full

The treatise turns to the basis of the second mark, loving speech, which likewise rests on five kinds: correct doctrinal speech, correct congratulatory speech, correct comforting speech, correct speech of open, unrestricted generosity, and speech that accords with reason — all five already explained at length in the treatise's own earlier chapter on the means of gathering beings. Correct doctrinal speech comprises four aspects grounded directly in the four kinds of unobstructed understanding: disclosing the various gateways of the teaching itself, through unobstructed understanding of the teaching; disclosing the undistorted characteristics of phenomena, through unobstructed understanding of meaning; disclosing the undistorted etymological explanation of every term, through unobstructed understanding of language; and disclosing the undistorted classification of every category of teaching, through unobstructed understanding of eloquent exposition. Correct congratulatory speech, in turn, has two aspects: celebrating a being's own flourishing worldly circumstances — a good family, thriving wealth — of which that being may not even be fully aware, and celebrating a being's own flourishing transcendent qualities, an increase in faith, moral discipline, learning, generosity, or wisdom.

Chinese Source

當知菩薩愛語依處，亦有五種。何等為五？一、正言論語，二、正慶悅語，三、正安慰語，四、正廣恣語，五、如理說語。如是廣辯，應知如前攝事品說。

《披》正言論語等者：開示諸法法門愛語，開示無倒法相愛語，開示無倒訓釋諸法言辭愛語，開示無倒法句品類差別愛語，是名正言論語。菩薩若見有情妻子、眷屬、財穀，其所昌盛而不自知，如應覺悟，以申慶悅；或知信、戒、聞、捨、慧增，亦復慶悅；是名正慶悅語。

是名菩薩所有愛語五種依處，由此依處、由此所緣，愛語而轉。除此無有若過若增。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should understand that a bodhisattva's basis for loving speech is likewise five kinds. What are these five? First, correct doctrinal speech; second, correct congratulatory speech; third, correct comforting speech; fourth, correct speech of open, unrestricted generosity; fifth, speech that accords with reason. Their extensive analysis should be understood exactly as explained earlier in the Chapter on the Means of Gathering Beings.

Commentary: Regarding "correct doctrinal speech" and the like — loving speech that discloses the various gateways of the teaching, loving speech that discloses the undistorted characteristics of phenomena, loving speech that discloses the undistorted etymological explanation of the teaching's own language, and loving speech that discloses the undistorted classification of the teaching's own categories, together are called correct doctrinal speech. If a bodhisattva sees that a being's spouse, family, or wealth and grain are flourishing, yet that being remains unaware of it, the bodhisattva, as appropriate, awakens that being to it and expresses congratulations; or, knowing that a being's faith, moral discipline, learning, generosity, or wisdom has increased, likewise offers congratulations. This is called correct congratulatory speech.

These, then, are the bodhisattva's five bases for loving speech. It is by depending on these bases, by taking these as objects, that loving speech turns and functions. Beyond these five, there is nothing to be added and nothing lacking.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi maps correct doctrinal speech's four aspects directly onto the four kinds of unobstructed understanding already explained in detail for the fifth mark: disclosing the teaching's own gateways — the Prajñāpāramitā approach, the Avataṃsaka approach, the consciousness-only approach, the Madhyamaka approach, and so on — relies on unobstructed understanding of the teaching, leading beings toward the ultimate truth; disclosing the undistorted characteristics and principles phenomena actually possess relies on unobstructed understanding of meaning; disclosing the correct explanation of the teaching's own terms in whatever language a listener speaks — Chinese, English, French, German — relies on unobstructed understanding of language; and disclosing the undistorted classification of phenomena into their many categories, wholesome, unwholesome, and neutral, past, present, and future, dark and bright, relies on unobstructed understanding of eloquent exposition, also called unobstructed understanding of joyful exposition.

He explains correct congratulatory speech's two aspects as covering both worldly and transcendent flourishing: a bodhisattva who notices that a being enjoys a good spouse, thriving children, and flourishing wealth, yet remains unaware enough of this good fortune to feel genuine gratitude, awakens that being to appreciate it and expresses heartfelt congratulations accordingly; and a bodhisattva who notices growth in a being's faith in the three jewels, moral discipline, conscience, shame, learning, generosity, or wisdom likewise offers encouragement and celebration, sustaining that being's continued spiritual progress. He then briefly covers the remaining three: correct comforting speech offers fitting words of consolation whenever a being faces terror or distress, whether from natural disaster, epidemic, or ordinary grief, dispelling fear through speech alone; correct speech of open, unrestricted generosity addresses beings, whether ordinary or noble, who desire little and are easily content, inviting them freely to take whatever provisions they need from an unrestricted offering made without regard to status or relation; and speech that accords with reason expounds the four noble truths and other teachings precisely in keeping with the four kinds of correct reasoning, matching the teaching exactly to the capacity of the listener. With all five bases of loving speech now closed in full, the treatise turns to the basis of the third mark, valor.

8. Section: The Basis of Valor — Five Grounds Already Named in the Chapter on the Factors of Awakening, Three for Others and Two for Oneself

The treatise turns to the basis of the third mark, valor, referring directly back to the five bases of the bodhisattva's own firm, sustaining resolve already explained in the earlier chapter on the factors of awakening — these same five, the treatise says, should be understood as valor's own five bases here as well. The first is encountering, amid the endless turning of birth-and-death, the many kinds of great suffering and wrongful conduct displayed by the beings a bodhisattva sets out to transform, without ever losing the resolve to benefit and bring ease to them regardless of how much such beings' own wrongdoing may cause the bodhisattva further hardship. The second is vowing to undergo a long span of continued birth-and-death specifically for the sake of benefiting beings, returning again and again to the defiled desire realm to carry out that work. The third is enduring the challenge of debating rival factions who dispute and press difficult questions, and teaching the meaning of the true teaching before a large assembly, without fear or timidity in either circumstance. The fourth is vowing to undertake every subject a bodhisattva should study — the five classical sciences of inner wisdom, medicine, logic, linguistics, and the arts and crafts — however long the accumulation of merit and wisdom such study demands. And the fifth is hearing and taking up the great vehicle's own vast, profound, difficult-to-fathom teachings — the Avataṃsaka's own fourfold dharma-realm and ten mysterious gates, the Lotus Sutra, the great Prajñāpāramitā — however extensive and difficult such teachings may be.

Chinese Source

當知菩薩勇猛依處，亦有五種。何等為五？謂即如前菩提分品所說菩薩堅力持性五種依處，當知此是菩薩勇猛五種依處。

由此依處、由此所緣，勇猛而轉。除此無有若過若增。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should understand that a bodhisattva's basis for valor is likewise five kinds. What are these five? These are exactly the five bases of the bodhisattva's own firm, sustaining resolve already explained earlier in the chapter on the factors of awakening; one should understand that these are valor's own five bases here as well.

It is by depending on these bases, by taking these as objects, that valor turns and functions. Beyond these five, there is nothing to be added and nothing lacking.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes that the treatise, rather than restating this fivefold list in full, simply refers back to its own earlier and fuller treatment in the chapter on the factors of awakening, fascicle 44 — a courtesy to the reader who has already followed the treatise continuously this far — and reprises the five briefly here. The first three, he observes, concern benefiting others: enduring beings' own wrongdoing and even outright harm without ever losing the resolve to help them, since a bodhisattva persists until such beings' wholesome roots finally ripen; vowing to remain within birth-and-death for however long is needed, returning to this very desire realm again and again for the sake of beings rather than seeking premature escape; and withstanding intellectual challenge from rival factions and teaching confidently before large assemblies, neither shrinking from difficult debate nor losing composure when addressing many people at once.

The remaining two, he continues, concern a bodhisattva's own self-cultivation: committing to master the five classical sciences regardless of how long such mastery takes to accumulate, and committing to study the great vehicle's own most extensive and demanding teachings — the Avataṃsaka Sutra's elaborate fourfold dharma-realm and ten mysterious gates, the Lotus Sutra, the vast Prajñāpāramitā literature — regardless of their sheer scope and difficulty. He sums up the whole fivefold structure as inseparably serving both self-benefit and the benefit of others, precisely the same twin commitment that has run through the entire chapter so far, now specifically identified as what actually grounds and sustains a bodhisattva's own unshakeable valor. The treatise turns next to the basis of the fourth mark, open-handed giving.

9. Section: The Basis of Open-Handed Giving — Five Qualities of Genuine Generosity, From Frequent Giving to Giving Grounded in Nothing at All

The treatise turns to the basis of the fourth mark, open-handed giving, which likewise rests on five qualities already explained in the treatise's own earlier chapter on the perfection of giving: frequent giving, joyful giving, earnest giving, untainted giving, and ungrounded giving. Frequent giving means that a bodhisattva gives away whatever is received the moment the opportunity arises, without stinginess, rather than waiting to accumulate a large sum before giving all at once. Joyful giving means delight before giving, a pure mind while giving, and no regret afterward. Earnest giving means giving at the fitting time rather than an unfitting one, with both giver and gift genuinely pure, in a manner appropriate to the recipient's own station, and with an undistracted, wholly attentive mind throughout. Untainted giving means giving without anger or agitation even toward a recipient of poor conduct, and without any superstitious clinging to false omens about when giving is or is not auspicious. And ungrounded giving means giving that seeks neither worldly reputation nor a recipient's future repayment nor any exalted future rebirth as a heavenly king or universal monarch, but is grounded in nothing beyond the giving itself, dedicated entirely toward awakening.

Chinese Source

當知菩薩舒手惠施，亦五依處。何等為五？一、數數惠施，二、歡喜惠施，三、殷重惠施，四、無染惠施，五、無依惠施。如是五種，如前施品廣辯應知。

《披》數數惠施等者：隨有所得即隨惠施，無所吝惜，不於長時漸漸積集眾多財物，然後頓施，是名數數惠施。施前意悅，施時心淨，施後無悔，是名歡喜惠施。應時而施，不以非時；自他淨施，非不清淨；合儀而施，不以非儀；無亂心施，不以散亂；是名殷重惠施。不以異意、不以憤怒撓濁之心，而行惠施；又復不由惡見妄有執取而行惠施；是名無染惠施。不依世間名聲讚頌而行布施，不依於他反報恩德而行布施，不依帝釋、魔王、輪王、自在等果而行布施，是名無依惠施。如是諸義，如施品說應知。

由此依處、由此所緣，菩薩舒手惠施而轉。除此無有若過若增。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should understand that a bodhisattva's open-handed giving likewise has five bases. What are these five? First, frequent giving; second, joyful giving; third, earnest giving; fourth, untainted giving; fifth, ungrounded giving. These five, one should understand, are explained extensively as before in the Chapter on the Perfection of Giving.

Commentary: Regarding "frequent giving" and the like — giving away whatever is obtained the moment it is received, without stinginess, rather than gradually accumulating much wealth over a long time and only then giving all at once, is called frequent giving. Delight before giving, a pure mind while giving, and no regret afterward is called joyful giving. Giving at the fitting time, not an unfitting one; giving with both giver and recipient genuinely pure, not impure; giving in a manner fitting to the occasion, not unfitting; giving with an undistracted mind, not a scattered one — this is called earnest giving. Giving without a different intention, without a mind agitated and clouded by anger, and giving without clinging to false views and mistaken grasping, is called untainted giving. Giving that does not depend on worldly reputation and praise, does not depend on the recipient's future repayment of kindness, and does not depend on the fruit of becoming Indra, a demon king, a wheel-turning monarch, or one with mastery, is called ungrounded giving. These meanings, one should understand, are as explained in the Chapter on the Perfection of Giving.

It is by depending on these bases, by taking these as objects, that a bodhisattva's open-handed giving turns and functions. Beyond these five, there is nothing to be added and nothing lacking.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi illustrates frequent giving with the example of Master Yin Guang, who, upon receiving especially fine offerings, would immediately pass them along for other venerable teachers to use rather than keeping or hoarding them — precisely the spirit of seizing every opportunity to give as it arises rather than waiting to accumulate a large store first. He explains joyful giving's threefold timing — genuine delight anticipating the act of giving, a mind free of attachment informed by the wisdom of emptiness while actually giving, and complete freedom from regret afterward — and unpacks earnest giving's four dimensions in turn: giving exactly what a recipient actually needs at the moment of genuine need, rather than at some arbitrary time; ensuring both the giver's own intention and the gift's own source are genuinely pure, ideally joined with wisdom recognizing the threefold emptiness of giver, gift, and recipient; matching the manner of giving to the recipient's own proper station — never, for instance, offering food containing garlic or onion to a monastic, or inviting a monastic to a wedding banquet, since such offerings would violate the recipient's own appropriate conduct; and giving with careful, undistracted attention rather than careless haste, since carelessness easily produces gifts that are mismatched, unequal, or simply inadequate.

He explains untainted giving's two components as freedom from both anger and superstition: a bodhisattva remains unmoved by anger even when a recipient behaves with agitation, disrespect, or open verbal abuse toward the giver, and refrains equally from any superstitious practice tied to giving, whether extravagant, harmful sacrificial rites or the mistaken belief that giving is only auspicious on certain calendar days, such as the first and fifteenth of the lunar month, and inauspicious on all others. He explains ungrounded giving's three components as freedom from seeking worldly fame and praise through giving, freedom from expecting the recipient to repay the kindness shown, and freedom from giving merely to secure some exalted future rebirth as Indra, a heavenly demon-king, a wheel-turning monarch, or a god with great mastery — a bodhisattva's giving, rightly understood, seeks nothing beyond the giving itself and its dedication toward awakening, exactly as the treatise's own earlier chapter on the perfection of giving, fascicle 39, already explains at length. With all five bases of open-handed giving now closed in full, the treatise turns to the basis of the fifth and final mark, understanding profound meaning and hidden intent.

10. Section: The Basis of Understanding Profound Meaning Opens — the Discourses, and the Twin Depths of Emptiness and Dependent Origination

The treatise turns to the basis of the fifth and final mark, understanding profound meaning and hidden intent, which likewise rests on five bases. The first is the Buddha's own discourses, which accord with and disclose the profound truths of emptiness and dependent origination alike — the Buddha's teaching being called a discourse precisely because it accords with both the true nature of reality and the capacity of its listeners, and its content is profound in two distinct ways: profound because emptiness's own particular characteristic is neither definitely existent nor definitely nonexistent, and profound because the links of dependent origination, though genuinely impermanent, suffering, empty, and without self, nonetheless present an appearance closely resembling permanence and self, easily misleading beings who have not examined it with care.

Chinese Source

當知菩薩能解甚深義理密意，亦五依處。何等為五？謂於如來所說契經，隨順甚深甚深顯現空性相應、緣性緣起，應知是名第一依處。

《披》隨順甚深甚深顯現等者：甚深有二，一、空性甚深，二、緣起甚深。空性甚深者，謂空自相非定有無，是名甚深空性相應。緣起甚深者，謂緣起支雖實無常、苦、空、無我，而有常、我等相相似顯現。有尋有伺地廣辯應知。（陵本十卷十六頁812）是名甚深顯現緣性緣起。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should understand that a bodhisattva's capacity to understand profound meaning and hidden intent likewise has five bases. What are these five? First, the discourses spoken by the tathagata, which accord with and disclose the profound truths of emptiness and of dependent origination. One should understand this is the first basis.

Commentary: Regarding "accord with and disclose the profound" and the like — depth is of two kinds: first, the depth of emptiness; second, the depth of dependent origination. The depth of emptiness means that emptiness's own particular characteristic is neither definitely existent nor definitely nonexistent — this is called according with the profound truth of emptiness. The depth of dependent origination means that, although the links of dependent origination are genuinely impermanent, suffering, empty, and without self, they nonetheless present an appearance resembling permanence and self. This is explained at length in the Stage With Both Investigation and Analysis, and should be understood accordingly. This is called disclosing the profound truth of dependent origination.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains why this first basis is called profound in two distinct ways. Emptiness's own depth, he says, lies in how difficult it is to state correctly: since the true nature of self and phenomena is neither definitely existent nor definitely nonexistent, only direct realization — not mere verbal description — can actually grasp it, precisely the difficulty the Compendium on Mutual Association, fascicle 93, points to when it says that dependent origination's own intrinsic nature is utterly profound, and only one who has already been shown it can make it accessible to others. He cites the Compendium of Establishing Illustrious Teaching's own verse — "if this is absent, and this other remains present, according to these two principles, emptiness's characteristic is spoken of as non-dual" — unpacking each line in turn: "if this is absent" means that no true self-nature of person or phenomena exists within dependently-arisen phenomena; "and this other remains present" means that, beyond that absence, dependently-arisen phenomena themselves and the suchness of both personal and phenomenal emptiness genuinely exist and cannot simply be denied; "according to these two principles" means following both non-definite-existence and non-definite-nonexistence together; and "emptiness's characteristic is spoken of as non-dual" means that emptiness thereby avoids both the exaggeration of imagining something to be there that is not, and the diminishment of denying dependently-arisen phenomena that genuinely do appear.

He works through the treatise's own gloss on "non-definite existence" and "non-definite nonexistence": ordinary beings cling, within the five aggregates and other conditioned phenomena, to the belief that beings and phenomena possess some genuinely real, fixed nature — yet ultimately, neither a being's own nature nor a phenomenon's own nature can actually be found there, which is why this is called non-definite existence; yet within these same conditioned phenomena, personal selflessness and phenomenal selflessness themselves genuinely exist as true realities, which is why complete denial is likewise impossible, called non-definite nonexistence. He adds Elder Miao Jing's own complementary framing: the truly, perfectly accomplished nature is precisely the realization, within dependently-arisen phenomena, that the purely imagined nature is empty — recognizing that whatever appears is simply what consciousness itself manifests, so that both the object known and the mind knowing it are alike ungraspable; once both subject and object are thus seen as empty and the dependently-arisen nature itself settles into a profound, six-fold non-appearing, this stillness itself is what "profound" names — such stillness appearing as non-definite existence to an ordinary being's own deluded discrimination, which still produces a defiled dependently-arisen appearance, and appearing instead as non-definite nonexistence once a noble one's own non-discriminating wisdom sees that dependent arising itself continues to function even amid this very stillness.

Turning to dependent origination's own depth, he explains that the links of dependent origination, however genuinely impermanent, suffering, empty, and without self they actually are, nonetheless present an appearance closely resembling permanence, ease, self, and purity — a resemblance that easily deceives an unexamined mind into mistaking passing appearance for lasting substance, exactly as detailed in the Stage With Both Investigation and Analysis, fascicle 10. He closes by noting that only a bodhisattva who has actually cultivated calm-abiding and insight can genuinely realize personal and phenomenal emptiness in this full sense, reaching the fourth of the classical four kinds of genuine reality — the reality realized by wisdom purified of the cognitive obscuration — beyond the first three lesser kinds of reality: what is agreed upon by ordinary worldly convention, what is agreed upon through reasoned inference, and the reality realized merely by wisdom purified of the afflictive obscuration alone, the domain of the disciple's own more limited realization. The treatise turns next to the second basis, the monastic code.

11. Section: The Monastic Code, the Treatises, Hidden Intent, and Unobstructed Understanding — Closing the Basis of All Five Marks Entirely

The second basis is skill regarding transgression and skill regarding restoration to purity within the monastic code — a bodhisattva who correctly understands both what constitutes a violation of the precepts and how genuine confession restores purity afterward, since the Buddha's own precepts exist not to punish beings but to guide them toward genuine freedom from suffering, and therefore necessarily allow for restoration. The third basis is using language to establish, without any distortion, the very characteristics of phenomena found within the treatises — the great bodhisattva authors from Nagarjuna through Maitreya and Asanga having themselves skillfully composed such treatises precisely through this same capacity. The fourth basis is correctly disclosing and dispelling doubt about phenomena whose underlying intent is difficult to grasp — every tathagata's own hidden intent sometimes remaining concealed within scripture rather than stated outright, as when the scriptures speak of an arhat who has "killed father and mother," a riddle resolved only by understanding that the true referents are ignorance, the father, and craving, the mother. And the fifth basis is unobstructed understanding itself, applied to every phenomenon's own teaching, meaning, explanatory term, and classification without exception. With this fifth basis, the treatise concludes: these five — the discourses, the monastic code, the treatises, hidden intent, and unobstructed understanding — together comprise the entire basis on which a bodhisattva's capacity to understand profound meaning and hidden intent depends, with nothing beyond these five to add or find lacking — closing the second of the five angles, the basis of every one of the five true marks, entirely in full.

Chinese Source

於毗奈耶毀犯善巧、還淨善巧，當知是名第二依處。

於摩怛理迦，施設、建立無倒法相，當知是名第三依處。

能正顯除意趣難解諸法相義，當知是名第四依處。

《披》能正顯除意趣難解諸法相義者：一切如來密意言義，是名意趣難解諸法相義。即於此義能正顯了，能正除遣所化有情隨所生起一切疑惑。義如真實義品中說。（陵本三十六卷二十五頁3014）

於一切法，法、義、釋辭、品類差別，當知是名第五依處。

由此依處、由此所緣，菩薩能解甚深義理密意而轉。除此無有若過若增。

Ru-Yi Translation

Skill regarding transgression within the monastic code and skill regarding restoration to purity, one should understand, is the second basis.

Using language to establish, without distortion, the characteristics of phenomena within the treatises, one should understand, is the third basis.

Correctly disclosing and dispelling doubt about phenomena whose underlying intent is difficult to grasp, one should understand, is the fourth basis.

Commentary: Regarding "correctly disclosing and dispelling doubt about phenomena whose underlying intent is difficult to grasp" — every tathagata's own hidden intent and meaning is called phenomena whose underlying intent is difficult to grasp. Correctly disclosing this very meaning, and correctly dispelling whatever doubts arise for the beings being taught regarding it, is meant here. Its meaning is as explained in the Chapter on Genuine Reality.

Regarding all phenomena, their teaching, their meaning, their explanatory terms, and their varied classifications, one should understand, is the fifth basis.

It is by depending on these bases, by taking these as objects, that a bodhisattva's capacity to understand profound meaning and hidden intent turns and functions. Beyond these five, there is nothing to be added and nothing lacking.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the second basis, the monastic code, as resting on the simple but crucial recognition that a transgression, once it has occurred, whether through a lapse of mindfulness or through being overwhelmed by affliction, is not an irredeemable stain — since a transgression, like every other phenomenon, has no fixed, unchangeable nature of its own, it can genuinely be purified through sincere confession. He stresses that the Buddha's own purpose in establishing the precepts was never to burden beings with guilt but to help them subdue their coarser afflictions and progress toward genuine freedom from suffering, which is precisely why restoration must always remain possible — a bodhisattva who is skilled in both what constitutes a violation and how confession restores purity can not only keep the precepts purely themselves but help guide other practitioners through the same process, cultivating the concentration and wisdom that pure discipline makes possible.

For the third basis, he highlights the great bodhisattva authors of the tradition as living proof of this same capacity in action — Nagarjuna composing the Great Treatise on the Perfection of Wisdom, Maitreya himself the very source of this treatise, and Asanga composing the Compendium of the Great Vehicle and the Compendium of Establishing Illustrious Teaching — each skillfully using language to establish undistorted accounts of the true characteristics of phenomena, demonstrating just how vast and profound this third basis's own wisdom can become. For the fourth basis, hidden intent, he unpacks the classic riddle the commentary alludes to: scripture sometimes speaks of an arhat who has "killed father and mother," language that would ordinarily describe one of the five gravest transgressions utterly incompatible with arhatship — until a bodhisattva's own correct disclosure reveals that "father" here secretly refers to ignorance and "mother" to craving, the two roots an arhat must sever to attain the fruit at all; since the Buddha adapted his teaching to the specific capacities of specific listeners, sometimes speaking plainly and sometimes concealing meaning within figurative language, a bodhisattva's task is precisely to correctly draw out such hidden intent and thereby dispel whatever doubt it would otherwise leave in a listener's mind.

He closes with the fifth basis, unobstructed understanding itself, noting that without genuine mastery of the teaching, its meaning, its explanatory terminology, and its many classifications — encompassing both a phenomenon's own particular characteristic and its characteristic shared with all others — none of the four kinds of unobstructed understanding could actually be attained, and without them, understanding profound meaning and hidden intent in the fullest sense would remain permanently out of reach. With all five bases of the fifth mark now closed, he observes, the entire second of the five angles — the basis on which every one of the five true marks depends, examined mark by mark across compassion, loving speech, valor, giving, and understanding — reaches its own complete conclusion. The treatise turns next to the third of the five angles, the beneficial fruit each of these five marks actually produces.

12. Section: The Beneficial Fruit of Compassion — Breaking Resentment, Boundless Abiding, and Immunity Even From Poison and Weapons

The treatise turns to the third of the five angles, the beneficial fruit each mark produces, beginning with compassion. A bodhisattva's compassion, most immediately, breaks resentment and hostility toward beings at the very root; it extends broadly to every task that benefits beings, undertaken without any timidity or weakness of mind; a bodhisattva applying this effort never grows weary of it, dwelling continually in compassion in a way that includes the untainted meditative absorptions of present-life ease — the four form-realm dhyanas — while also genuinely benefiting others. The treatise further cites the Buddha's own teaching on the benefit of cultivating loving-kindness specifically: one who has genuinely cultivated it cannot, in this very body, be harmed by poison or by blade — and every kind of bodhisattva compassion, the treatise adds, should be understood to yield this same benefit.

Chinese Source

菩薩哀愍，於諸有情，最初能斷怨害嫌恨。菩薩哀愍，普於一切利有情事皆能修作，心無怯劣。於此加行嘗無厭倦，多住哀愍，能攝無罪現法樂住及饒益他。

又如世尊所說修慈所得勝利，謂於現身毒藥、刀杖不加害等。如是一切菩薩哀愍，皆當了知。

是名菩薩哀愍果利。

Ru-Yi Translation

A bodhisattva's compassion, toward sentient beings, most immediately breaks resentment, hostility, and hatred. A bodhisattva's compassion is able to undertake every task that benefits beings broadly, the mind never timid or weak. Applying this effort, a bodhisattva never grows weary, dwelling extensively in compassion, thereby including untainted present-life dwelling in ease, and genuinely benefiting others.

Furthermore, exactly as the world-honored one taught regarding the excellent benefit gained from cultivating loving-kindness — namely, that in this very body, poison and blade cannot cause harm — one should understand that every kind of bodhisattva compassion yields this same benefit.

This is called the beneficial fruit of a bodhisattva's compassion.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains that a bodhisattva's compassion first and most directly dissolves whatever resentment, hostility, or hatred that bodhisattva might otherwise hold toward beings, since genuine compassion and lingering ill-will toward the very beings compassion is directed at cannot coexist. From that root, compassion extends outward to encompass every undertaking that benefits beings, sustained without timidity, and — most significantly — sustained without ever growing weary, such continuous, tireless dwelling in compassion itself constituting what the commentary calls the four immeasurable meditative absorptions, cultivated specifically within the form realm's four dhyanas for the sake of benefiting beings.

He explains the citation of the Buddha's own teaching on loving-kindness as extending the same principle to actual physical protection: someone who has genuinely cultivated boundless loving-kindness becomes, in this very body, immune to harm from poison or weapon — not through any external protection but through the sheer transformative power of a mind thoroughly saturated with goodwill, extended here to compassion, grief, joy, and equanimity alike as the four immeasurables. He then draws in the treatise's own fuller account from fascicle 44 of the four specific fruits compassion yields: first, attaining the most excellent present-life dwelling in ease; second, gathering and increasing boundless, most excellent provisions of merit; third, holding an unshakeably firm aspiration toward unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening; and fourth, being able to endure every great suffering encountered within birth-and-death for the sake of benefiting beings — with the commentary clarifying that where the source text illustrates this fourfold fruit using loving-kindness specifically, the same fruit equally applies when a bodhisattva cultivates compassion, sympathetic joy, and equanimity in turn. The treatise turns next to the beneficial fruit of the second mark, loving speech.

13. Section: The Beneficial Fruit of Loving Speech — Breaking Four Verbal Faults Now, and Commanding Trust and Authority in Every Future Life

The treatise turns to the beneficial fruit of the second mark, loving speech, dividing it into what is gained in this present life and what is gained in future lives. In this very life, a bodhisattva's loving speech breaks the four verbal faults of false speech, divisive speech, harsh speech, and frivolous speech — since a bodhisattva who delights in speaking words that genuinely benefit and bring ease to beings naturally has no room left for these four faults to arise; through this same loving speech, a bodhisattva is able to protect both self and other, dwelling in security and ease. And in future lives, a bodhisattva's loving speech ripens into speech that is dignified and composed, speech that others will inevitably trust and act upon — the natural fruit, sustained across many lifetimes, of habitually speaking with genuine care for beings' own benefit.

Chinese Source

菩薩愛語，於現法中斷語四過。所謂妄語、離間、麤惡，及以綺語。

由此愛語，於現法中能自攝受、能攝受他，安隱而轉。

菩薩愛語，於當來世其言敦肅，言必信用。

是名菩薩愛語果利。

《披》能自攝受等者：不為自害，攝受饒益，是名能自攝受。不為他害，而為饒益，是名能攝受他。不受彼不善業所生身心憂苦，是名安隱而轉。

Ru-Yi Translation

A bodhisattva's loving speech, in this very life, breaks the four verbal faults — namely, false speech, divisive speech, harsh speech, and frivolous speech.

Through this loving speech, in this very life, a bodhisattva is able to protect both self and other, dwelling in security and ease.

A bodhisattva's loving speech, in future lives, becomes speech that is dignified and composed, speech that will always be trusted and acted upon.

This is called the beneficial fruit of a bodhisattva's loving speech.

Commentary: Regarding "able to protect both self and other" and the like — not harming oneself, but gathering in and benefiting oneself, is called being able to protect oneself. Not harming another, but bringing benefit instead, is called being able to protect another. Not undergoing the bodily and mental distress produced by unwholesome karma is called dwelling in security and ease.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi cites the treatise's own fuller account of loving speech's three kinds from the Stage Attained Through Hearing, fascicle 14: comforting speech, greeting beings warmly with a relaxed, smiling face rather than a furrowed brow, inquiring after their well-being, their comfort with the seasons, or their ease day and night, according to worldly custom; congratulatory speech, celebrating a being's flourishing family, wealth, or growth in faith, discipline, learning, generosity, and wisdom; and beneficial speech, disclosing wholesome teachings both worldly and transcendent — together, these three kinds of loving speech cause beings to feel genuine affection toward a bodhisattva, drawing near and accepting instruction, settling into truth, precisely the gathering-in function loving speech serves.

He unpacks the commentary's own gloss on protecting self and other: a bodhisattva who speaks lovingly avoids the four verbal faults and therefore causes no harm to their own future welfare, actively gathering in benefit for themselves instead; the same speech causes no harm to others but instead brings them benefit; and because such speech generates none of the four unwholesome verbal karmas, a bodhisattva sustained by it never has to undergo the bodily and mental suffering that false, divisive, harsh, or frivolous speech would otherwise eventually produce — this freedom from self-inflicted suffering is precisely what "dwelling in security and ease" describes. He closes by noting that the future-life fruit, speech others will trust and act upon without hesitation, is simply the natural, cumulative result of a lifetime — indeed many lifetimes — of consistently speaking in this loving, beneficial way, a bodhisattva's own word eventually carrying weight precisely because it has so reliably served beings' genuine good. The treatise turns next to the beneficial fruit of the third mark, valor.

14. Section: The Beneficial Fruit of Valor — Freedom From Laziness Now, and Unwavering Resolve Toward Every Future Undertaking

The treatise turns to the beneficial fruit of the third mark, valor, likewise dividing it into present and future benefit. In this very life, a bodhisattva's valor yields three benefits: freedom from every trace of laziness and idleness, the mind constantly joyful; the capacity to receive the bodhisattva's own pure precepts and, having received them, never violate or retreat from them; and genuine capacity for patient endurance, gathering in benefit for both self and other. And in future lives, whatever bodhisattva undertaking such valor sets in motion, that resolve remains steadfast — whatever merit has been planned but not yet completed will never be abandoned partway through out of laziness or discouragement.

Chinese Source

菩薩勇猛，於現法中能離一切懶惰懈怠，心常歡喜；能受菩薩淨戒律儀，受已終無毀犯、退屈；能正堪忍，攝受自他。

於當來世一切菩薩所起事業，稟性堅固，凡所造修若未成辦，終無懈退。

是名菩薩勇猛果利。

Ru-Yi Translation

A bodhisattva's valor, in this very life, is able to leave behind every kind of laziness and idleness, the mind constantly joyful; is able to receive the bodhisattva's own pure precepts and, having received them, never violate them or retreat from them; and is able to genuinely endure with patience, gathering in benefit for both self and other.

In future lives, whatever undertaking a bodhisattva sets in motion, that bodhisattva's own disposition remains firm — whatever has been planned and cultivated, if not yet accomplished, will never be abandoned through laziness.

This is called the beneficial fruit of a bodhisattva's valor.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through the three present-life benefits of valor in turn: first, a bodhisattva's resolute, unshakeable diligence naturally leaves no room for laziness or idleness to take hold, the mind remaining constantly, genuinely joyful rather than dragged down by reluctance; second, this same firm resolve allows a bodhisattva to actually receive the threefold pure precepts — restraining precepts, precepts gathering wholesome teachings, and precepts benefiting sentient beings — and, having received them, never violate or timidly retreat from them, protecting the precept-essence itself and allowing wholesome roots to grow accordingly; and third, this resolve produces genuine capacity for patient endurance while carrying out the bodhisattva path, gathering in and benefiting both oneself and others along the way.

He explains the future-life benefit as the natural extension of this same steadfastness across lifetimes: having received the bodhisattva precepts, a bodhisattva's disposition toward every subsequent self-benefiting or other-benefiting undertaking remains firm and unwavering, so that whatever merit has been planned but not yet brought to completion is never simply abandoned midway through out of laziness or discouragement — valor cultivated now becomes a disposition that itself endures and compounds across future lives. The treatise turns next to the combined beneficial fruit of the fourth and fifth marks, open-handed giving and understanding profound meaning, treated together rather than separately.

15. Section: The Beneficial Fruit of Giving and of Understanding, Treated Together by Cross-Reference — Closing the Beneficial Fruit of All Five Marks in Full

The treatise closes this third angle by treating the fourth and fifth marks together, referring the reader directly to the treatise's own earlier chapter on power for the differing fourfold powers of giving and of wisdom, rather than repeating that fuller treatment here. Giving's own four powers are severing what is to be countered — dissolving stinginess toward wealth, teaching, and fearlessness alike; maturing the provisions — dwelling in the threefold emptiness of giver, gift, and recipient while both completing one's own provisions toward awakening and maturing beings through the four means of gathering; benefiting both self and other — a mind filled with joy before, during, and after giving, while relieving a recipient's hunger, cold, poverty, fear, and sickness; and yielding future results — five kinds of resulting benefit including constant wealth and ease, high position, great riches, capable associates, and an accomplished retinue. Wisdom's own four powers parallel this structure exactly: severing the ignorance underlying attachment to self and phenomena; maturing the provisions of awakening for oneself while maturing beings through the four means of gathering; benefiting both self and other through genuinely realizing the true nature of every object of knowledge, producing vast, pure joy while teaching beings the truth for their own present and future benefit; and yielding, in time, the complete severing of both obscurations and the full accomplishment of unsurpassed awakening itself. With this, the treatise concludes: these are the beneficial fruits of a bodhisattva's giving and of understanding profound meaning — and together, the beneficial fruits of all five true marks of a bodhisattva now close in full, from compassion's boundless abiding through giving and wisdom's fourfold powers.

Chinese Source

當知菩薩舒手惠施、能解甚深義理密意所得果利，如威力品惠施威力、般若威力差別應知。

是名菩薩舒手惠施、能解甚深義理密意二種果利。

是名菩薩五相果利。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should understand that the beneficial fruit a bodhisattva attains through open-handed giving and through understanding profound meaning and hidden intent should be understood exactly as the differing powers of giving and of wisdom explained in the Chapter on Power.

These are called the two beneficial fruits of a bodhisattva's open-handed giving and understanding of profound meaning and hidden intent.

These, then, are called the beneficial fruits of a bodhisattva's five true marks.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes the treatise's own economical choice here — rather than repeating material already given at length, it simply directs the reader back to fascicle 37's chapter on power, and reprises the parallel fourfold structure shared by giving's power and wisdom's power. Giving's four powers, he explains, are severing the stinginess that would otherwise block generosity toward wealth, the teaching, and freedom from fear alike; maturing one's own provisions toward awakening while, through the threefold emptiness of giver, gift, and recipient, simultaneously maturing beings through the four means of gathering; benefiting both self and other, a bodhisattva's own mind filled with joy throughout the entire act of giving while the recipient's immediate hunger, cold, poverty, fear, or illness is genuinely relieved; and yielding, in the future, five concrete kinds of resulting benefit — constant wealth and ease wherever rebirth occurs, high official position, abundant wealth, capable and accomplished associates, and an equally accomplished circle of dependents.

He explains wisdom's four powers as tracing the identical structure at a deeper level: severing the very ignorance that produces attachment to a fixed self and fixed phenomena; maturing one's own provisions toward awakening through the wisdom of emptiness, unattached to any object, while likewise maturing beings through the four means of gathering; benefiting both self and other by genuinely, truthfully realizing the nature of every object of knowledge — the aggregates, the realm of beings, the container-world, personal and phenomenal emptiness — producing vast, pure joy for oneself while broadly teaching beings the corresponding truth for their own present and future benefit; and yielding, in time, the complete severing of both the afflictive and the cognitive obscurations, culminating in unsurpassed awakening's full accomplishment. With this cross-referenced treatment, he notes, the beneficial fruit of all five true marks — compassion's boundless present-life ease, loving speech's present and future benefit, valor's present steadiness and future resolve, and giving and wisdom's shared fourfold power — now closes completely in full. The treatise turns next to the fourth of the five angles, the sequence in which a bodhisattva actually cultivates these five marks.

16. Section: The Sequence Opens — Compassion First to Gather Beings In, Then Loving Speech to Guide Them Toward the Good

The treatise turns to the fourth of the five angles, the sequence in which a bodhisattva actually cultivates the five true marks, opening with the question: why do the five marks unfold in exactly this order? Bodhisattvas, the treatise answers, first cultivate compassion in order to gather beings in — attending to them, caring for them, wishing to bring them genuine benefit. Only next do bodhisattvas cultivate loving speech, so that beings already gathered in through compassion can actually be led out of unwholesome ground and established on wholesome ground, expounding correct principle to instruct and guide them once that initial bond of care has been formed.

Chinese Source

云何五相如是次第？謂諸菩薩，先修哀愍攝受有情，於彼顧念，欲作饒益。

次修愛語，為彼有情出不善處安立善處，宣說正理，攝受教誨。

Ru-Yi Translation

Why do the five marks unfold in this particular sequence? Bodhisattvas first cultivate compassion in order to gather beings in, attending to and caring for them, wishing to bring them benefit.

Next, bodhisattvas cultivate loving speech, so that those beings may be led out of unwholesome ground and established on wholesome ground, expounding correct principle to gather them in through instruction and guidance.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi
