Master Cháng Bǎi treats this fourth angle as answering a genuinely natural question: given that the five marks have now been examined from every other angle, why does this particular order — compassion, then loving speech, then valor, then giving, then understanding — actually make sense as a developmental sequence rather than an arbitrary list? He explains that compassion must come first because it is compassion alone that first draws a bodhisattva's attention toward a given being at all, caring for that being and forming the wish to help — without this initial bond of genuine concern, no bodhisattva would ever have occasion to speak to, teach, or give to that being in the first place.

Loving speech follows naturally as the second step, he explains, because it is specifically through speech — comforting, congratulatory, and especially beneficial speech disclosing the teaching itself — that a bodhisattva actually begins guiding a being already gathered in by compassion out of the ten unwholesome courses of action and into a foundation of wholesome conduct, cultivating the seeds of discipline, concentration, and wisdom. Compassion opens the relationship; loving speech is the first concrete instrument through which that relationship becomes genuinely instructive. The treatise turns next to the third mark in this sequence, valor.

17. Section: Valor Comes Third to Sustain the Relationship Through Setbacks, Giving and Understanding Last to Match Each Being's Own Need — Closing the Sequence in Full

After loving speech, bodhisattvas next cultivate valor, so as never to abandon beings already gathered in through the first two marks even when such beings, swayed by some passing affliction, momentarily lapse into wrong conduct — enduring every such vicissitude precisely in order not to give up on beings who remain, at bottom, still established in correct practice. And only after valor do bodhisattvas cultivate the final two marks together: for beings who respond to material generosity, gathering them in and maturing them through giving; for beings who respond to the teaching, gathering them in and maturing them through the wisdom that understands profound meaning; and for beings who respond to both, gathering them in through both together — which is precisely why open-handed giving and understanding profound meaning are cultivated last, following upon valor. With this, the treatise concludes: this is the bodhisattva's own sequence for cultivating the five true marks, from first to last.

Chinese Source

次修勇猛，於已趣入諸有情類，若諸有情起諸邪行，種種煩惱變異事中皆能堪忍，為不棄捨安住種種正行、邪行諸有情故。

《披》起諸邪行等者：謂如暴惡毀犯淨戒，是名起諸邪行。如是邪行，當知種種煩惱變異所作，令改本性，由是菩薩深生憐愍，故能堪忍，為不棄捨彼有情故。由彼有情於佛聖教已正趣入，說名安住種種正行，然於一時煩惱所媚，暫起毀犯，復名邪行。

是諸菩薩修勇猛已，一類有情以財攝受，能令成熟；一類有情以法攝受，能令成熟；一類有情以財、以法二種攝受，能令成熟。是故菩薩次後修習舒手惠施、能解甚深義理密意。

當知是名菩薩五相前後次第。

Ru-Yi Translation

Next, bodhisattvas cultivate valor, so that, toward beings already brought into the teaching, whenever such beings give rise to wrong conduct or undergo various afflictive changes, they are able to endure all of it, so as not to abandon beings who remain established in correct practice even when they also, at times, exhibit wrong conduct.

Commentary: Regarding "giving rise to wrong conduct" — this refers to violently violating the pure precepts. Such wrong conduct, one should understand, arises from various afflictive changes that alter a being's own originally wholesome disposition. Because of this, a bodhisattva generates deep compassion and is thereby able to endure it, in order not to abandon that being. Since such a being has already correctly entered the Buddha's noble teaching, this is called abiding in correct practice; yet at a given moment, deceived by affliction, that being temporarily gives rise to a violation, which is why it is also called wrong conduct.

Once bodhisattvas have cultivated valor, for one class of being, gathering in through material wealth is able to bring maturity; for one class of being, gathering in through the teaching is able to bring maturity; and for one class of being, gathering in through both wealth and teaching together is able to bring maturity. This is why bodhisattvas next cultivate open-handed giving and understanding profound meaning and hidden intent.

One should understand that this is called the bodhisattva's own sequence for the five marks, from first to last.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi unpacks the commentary's own compassionate reading of why valor is needed specifically at this third stage: a being who has already genuinely entered the teaching through compassion and loving speech may nonetheless, swayed by some passing surge of affliction, momentarily violate the very precepts that being has sincerely undertaken — the commentary insists this temporary lapse does not erase that being's underlying, genuine commitment to correct practice, since the lapse arises from affliction distorting an otherwise wholesome disposition rather than from any change in that being's fundamental direction. A bodhisattva's valor, precisely because it generates deep compassion in response to such lapses rather than judgment or abandonment, is what allows the relationship established through compassion and loving speech to actually survive the inevitable setbacks that occur along any being's own path — without this steadfastness, a bodhisattva might otherwise give up on beings exactly when they most need continued support.

He explains the final two marks' shared placement as reflecting simple practical wisdom: some beings respond more readily to material generosity, some to the teaching's own wisdom, and some to both together, so a bodhisattva who has already sustained the relationship through valor's patient endurance is now positioned to discern which kind of gathering-in each particular being actually needs and to supply it accordingly — giving material support to those who need it, profound teaching to those who need that, and both to those who need both. He closes by observing the elegant logic binding the whole sequence together: compassion opens the relationship, loving speech begins actively guiding it, valor sustains it through inevitable difficulty, and giving and understanding finally supply whatever specific support brings a given being all the way to genuine spiritual maturity — a natural, developmental order rather than an arbitrary listing. The treatise turns next to the fifth and final angle, how the five marks relate to and are included within the six perfections.

18. Section: Mutual Inclusion — Mapping All Five True Marks Onto the Six Perfections, Closing the Fifth and Final Angle

The treatise turns to the fifth and final angle, mutual inclusion, opening with the question: how do the five true marks of a bodhisattva relate to the six perfections that carry a bodhisattva across to the far shore? Compassion, the treatise answers, is included within the perfection of meditative concentration, since it is specifically within the undistracted absorption of the four immeasurables that a bodhisattva actually cultivates boundless compassion toward beings. Loving speech is included within both the perfection of moral discipline and the perfection of wisdom — discipline accounting for comforting and congratulatory speech, since a bodhisattva who keeps the precepts purely naturally avoids the four verbal faults and speaks in accord with beings' own needs, and wisdom accounting for beneficial speech, since teaching the profound truths that genuinely benefit beings requires wisdom's own discernment.

Chinese Source

問：菩薩五相，六到彼岸，何到彼岸攝何等相？答：菩薩哀愍，當知靜慮到彼岸攝；

《披》菩薩哀愍當知靜慮到彼岸攝者：由四無量名為哀愍，故說靜慮到彼岸攝。

菩薩愛語，尸羅、般若到彼岸攝；

《披》菩薩愛語尸羅般若到彼岸攝者：依慰喻語、若慶悅語，故說尸羅到彼岸攝。依勝益語，故說般若到彼岸攝。

Ru-Yi Translation

Question: Among the bodhisattva's five marks and the six perfections that carry one to the far shore, which perfection includes which mark? Answer: A bodhisattva's compassion, one should understand, is included within the perfection of meditative concentration.

Commentary: Regarding "a bodhisattva's compassion is included within the perfection of meditative concentration" — because the four immeasurables are called compassion, this is why it is said to be included within the perfection of meditative concentration.

A bodhisattva's loving speech is included within the perfections of moral discipline and of wisdom.

Commentary: Regarding "a bodhisattva's loving speech is included within the perfections of moral discipline and of wisdom" — based on comforting speech and congratulatory speech, it is said to be included within the perfection of moral discipline. Based on beneficial speech, it is said to be included within the perfection of wisdom.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi opens by explaining the Sanskrit term paramita itself — reaching the far shore, transcending, or bringing a task to its final completion — and notes that the treatise's own fuller account, in the Decisions section, fascicle 78, specifies that genuinely reaching the far shore through any of the six perfections requires five further conditions: freedom from defiling attachment, freedom from clinging concern, freedom from fault, freedom from discriminating thought, and correct dedication toward awakening. He then walks through the mapping itself: compassion belongs to meditative concentration specifically because the four immeasurables — loving-kindness, compassion, sympathetic joy, and equanimity — are themselves a form of concentrated absorption, cultivated in an undistracted mind within the first or fourth dhyana, and it is precisely this concentrated cultivation that allows a bodhisattva's compassion to reach the depth and breadth already described toward all five kinds of beings who serve as compassion's own basis.

He explains loving speech's dual inclusion by distinguishing its three components: comforting speech and congratulatory speech, responding naturally and appropriately to whatever a being brings, belong to moral discipline, since a bodhisattva who keeps the precept of benefiting sentient beings purely will, as the treatise's own chapter on that precept states in fascicle 40, proactively offer such conversational warmth even when a being does not initiate it, let alone fail to reciprocate when a being does; beneficial speech, by contrast, belongs to wisdom, since actually teaching the profound truths that genuinely benefit beings requires the discerning wisdom capable of correctly perceiving what a given being actually needs to hear. The treatise turns next to mapping the remaining three marks — valor, giving, and understanding — onto their own corresponding perfections.

19. Section: Valor, Giving, and Understanding Complete the Mapping — Chapter One Closes in Full, and Chapter Two Opens on the Householder and Renunciate Divisions

Valor, the treatise continues, is included within the three perfections of diligence, patience, and wisdom — diligence, since a bodhisattva never abandons the constant work of benefiting beings; patience, since a bodhisattva endures every vicissitude such beings undergo; and wisdom, since only the discerning power of wisdom allows a bodhisattva to actually penetrate and apply the teaching amid such difficulty. Open-handed giving is included, straightforwardly, within the perfection of giving itself. And understanding profound meaning and hidden intent is included within both meditative concentration and wisdom, since it is specifically through concentration's own basis that a bodhisattva cultivates the wisdom realizing personal and phenomenal emptiness, penetrating the profound meaning of the teaching through the four kinds of unobstructed understanding. With all five marks now mapped, the treatise draws the entire first chapter to its full close: each of the five true marks — compassion, loving speech, valor, giving, and understanding — has now been examined through all five angles: intrinsic nature, basis, beneficial fruit, sequence, and mutual inclusion, fully and accurately explained. Chapter two, on the two divisions of householder and renunciate bodhisattva, then opens, its very name — "division" — carrying three traditional senses: positional division, marking how identical bodhisattva precepts manifest differently in outward form between householder and renunciate; functional division, marking how the same precepts express themselves differently in actual conduct between the two; and the sense of wings, since the six perfections, comprising both merit and wisdom together, are like a bird's own two wings carrying a bodhisattva swiftly to the far shore regardless of which of the two divisions one belongs to.

Chinese Source

菩薩勇猛，進、忍、般若到彼岸攝；菩薩所有舒手惠施，當知即施到彼岸攝；菩薩所有能解甚深義理密意，靜慮、般若到彼岸攝。

如是真實菩薩五相，當知一一皆有五轉。所謂自性、依處、果利、次第、相攝。已廣分別，應如實知。

本地分中菩薩地第二持隨法瑜伽處分品第二

Ru-Yi Translation

A bodhisattva's valor is included within the perfections of diligence, patience, and wisdom. A bodhisattva's open-handed giving, one should understand, is included within the perfection of giving. A bodhisattva's understanding of profound meaning and hidden intent is included within the perfections of meditative concentration and of wisdom.

One should understand that each of these true marks of a bodhisattva, in this way, has all five turnings — namely, intrinsic nature, basis, beneficial fruit, sequence, and mutual inclusion. Having now been extensively analyzed, this should be genuinely understood.

Within the Basic Section, the Bodhisattva Stage, the second yoga foundation of sustained practice, chapter two: the divisions of householder and renunciate.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi completes the mapping by noting that valor's inclusion within three separate perfections — diligence, patience, and wisdom — reflects how demanding this particular mark actually is: sustaining tireless effort on beings' behalf requires diligence, enduring whatever difficulty such beings present requires patience, and correctly discerning how to actually help amid that difficulty requires wisdom, so that no single perfection alone could account for everything valor accomplishes. Giving's inclusion within the perfection of giving needs no further comment, being simply direct correspondence. And understanding profound meaning's inclusion within both concentration and wisdom mirrors the very structure already explained for the fifth mark's own intrinsic nature: the four kinds of unobstructed understanding are themselves the fruit of sustained calm-abiding serving as the basis for insight into personal and phenomenal emptiness, concentration and wisdom working together rather than either alone.

Surveying the whole of chapter one now closed, he observes that the treatise's own five-angle method — examining the intrinsic nature, basis, beneficial fruit, sequence, and mutual inclusion of each of the five true marks in turn — has produced a genuinely thorough account, leaving no dimension of what makes a bodhisattva's character authentic unaddressed. He then introduces chapter two by way of the Treatise Commentary's own etymological note on the Sanskrit term underlying "division," carrying three senses at once: positional division, since a householder bodhisattva and a renunciate bodhisattva, though sharing an identical underlying precept-essence, differ in outward appearance and dress according to their respective circumstances; functional division, since the same shared precepts express themselves differently in actual practice between the two; and the sense of wings, since the six perfections, comprising the two wings of merit and wisdom together, carry any bodhisattva, householder or renunciate alike, swiftly to the far shore of liberation — the ancient alternate name for this same chapter, "the chapter of wings," reflecting this third sense directly. He notes that chapter two follows naturally on chapter one precisely because the five true marks just explained apply equally to bodhisattvas of both divisions, making the divisions themselves the fitting next topic.

20. Section: Four Dharmas Let Both Householder and Renunciate Bodhisattvas Swiftly Attain Awakening

Chapter two opens by asking: through how many dharmas, correctly cultivated, can householder and renunciate bodhisattvas alike swiftly realize unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening? A summary verse answers: bodhisattvas of both divisions swiftly attain awakening through four dharmas — skillfully cultivating one's own undertaking as the first, skillful means and benefiting others following, and undistorted dedication placed last. The prose explanation confirms: whether abiding as a householder or as a renunciate, bodhisattvas share, in brief, these same four dharmas that let both divisions correctly and diligently train toward swift, unsurpassed awakening — skillfully cultivating one's own undertaking, skillful means, benefiting others, and undistorted dedication.

Chinese Source

在家、出家二分菩薩，有幾種法正修學時，速證無上正等菩提？嗢柁南曰：二分諸菩薩　初事業善修　善巧饒益他　迴向最為後

謂諸菩薩，或在家分、或出家分差別轉時，略有四法，當知令此在家、出家二分菩薩正勤修學，速證無上正等菩提。何等為四？一者、善修事業，二者、方便善巧，三者、饒益於他，四者、無倒迴向。

Ru-Yi Translation

Through how many dharmas, correctly cultivated, do householder and renunciate bodhisattvas swiftly realize unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening? A summary verse states: "Bodhisattvas of both divisions; first, skillfully cultivating one's undertaking; skillful means, benefiting others; dedication placed last of all."

This means that bodhisattvas, whether functioning as a householder or as a renunciate, share, in brief, four dharmas. One should understand that these let both householder and renunciate bodhisattvas correctly and diligently train, swiftly realizing unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening. What are these four? First, skillfully cultivating one's own undertaking; second, skillful means; third, benefiting others; fourth, undistorted dedication.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi frames chapter two's opening question as a natural continuation of everything chapter one has already established: having explained the five true marks that make any bodhisattva genuinely a bodhisattva regardless of outward circumstance, the treatise now turns to the concrete methods by which bodhisattvas of both outward forms, householder and renunciate alike, actually accelerate their own progress toward awakening — a question of practical method rather than of identity.

He previews the four dharmas briefly: skillfully cultivating one's own undertaking, meaning the disciplined, thorough, constant, and faultless practice of the six perfections themselves, about to be explained in full through the example of giving; skillful means, the resourceful methods by which a bodhisattva's practice actually reaches and benefits beings effectively; benefiting others, the direct fruit that skillful means is meant to produce; and undistorted dedication, ensuring that whatever merit accrues through the first three is correctly directed toward unsurpassed awakening rather than allowed to dissipate into lesser aims. He notes that these four dharmas apply with equal force to both divisions of bodhisattva named in this chapter's own title — a signal that whatever outward differences separate a householder's life from a renunciate's, the substance of genuine bodhisattva practice remains identical between them. The treatise turns next to explaining the first of these four dharmas, skillfully cultivating one's own undertaking, in full detail.

21. Section: Skillfully Cultivating One's Undertaking Opens — the Six Perfections Practiced With Resolve, Illustrated First Through Giving

The treatise opens the detailed explanation of the first dharma, skillfully cultivating one's own undertaking: bodhisattvas take up the six perfections as their very undertaking, cultivating them with resolute determination, cultivating them thoroughly and completely, cultivating them constantly without interruption, and cultivating them free of any afflictive fault. Taking the perfection of giving as the illustrative example, resolute cultivation means that when a bodhisattva possesses various forms of wealth or teaching that could be given, and someone comes forward genuinely seeking it, that bodhisattva gives without any distinction based on whether the recipient has shown gratitude or ingratitude, virtue or fault — and no human being, non-human being, renunciate, brahmin, or any other party in the world, however they might try to obstruct it through improper means, can ever shake that resolve to give.

Chinese Source

云何菩薩善修事業？謂諸菩薩，於六波羅蜜多決定修作、委悉修作、恆常修作、無罪修作。

云何菩薩於施波羅蜜多決定修作？謂諸菩薩，現有種種可施財法，諸乞求者正現在前，有恩、無恩、有德、有失無有差別，要當施與。若人非人、若諸沙門、若婆羅門及餘世間，無有如法能令施心有所傾動。

《披》若人非人等者：謂生人趣，得人種類，是名為人。若生餘趣，那落迦、傍生，及與鬼趣、天、龍、藥叉、阿素洛、揭路荼、緊捺洛、牟呼洛迦等，是名非人。如顯揚論說。（三卷十四頁）生在人中，希求魔梵而修行者，是名沙門、若婆羅門。若魔、若梵，名餘世間。如攝異門分說。（陵本八十三卷十七頁6327）

Ru-Yi Translation

What is a bodhisattva's skillful cultivation of one's own undertaking? It means that bodhisattvas, with respect to the six perfections, cultivate them with resolute determination, cultivate them thoroughly and completely, cultivate them constantly, and cultivate them free of fault.

What is a bodhisattva's resolute cultivation of the perfection of giving? It means that bodhisattvas, presently possessing various forms of giving-worthy wealth or teaching, when a seeker genuinely appears before them, give without any distinction based on whether the recipient has shown gratitude or ingratitude, virtue or fault. No human or non-human, no renunciate or brahmin, and no other party in the world can, through any improper means, cause that giving mind to waver.

Commentary: Regarding "human or non-human" and the like — being born into the human realm, obtaining a human form, is called human. Being born into other realms — the hells, animals, the realm of hungry ghosts, and the eightfold host of gods, dragons, yakshas, asuras, garudas, kimnaras, and mahoragas — is called non-human. This is as explained in the Compendium of Establishing Illustrious Teaching. Being born human yet practicing while aspiring toward becoming a demon-king or a Brahma is called a renunciate or a brahmin. A demon or a Brahma-heaven god themselves are called other parties in the world. This is as explained in the Compendium on Mutual Association.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the treatise structures the first dharma around four qualities of practice — resolute, thorough, constant, and faultless — applied across all six perfections, and chooses giving as the illustrative example through which to demonstrate all four in turn, trusting the reader to extend the same fourfold pattern to the remaining five perfections by analogy. Resolute cultivation, he explains, describes an unshakeable commitment to give whenever wealth or teaching is available and a genuine seeker appears, entirely regardless of that seeker's own moral standing or prior relationship to the bodhisattva — no distinction drawn between someone who has shown gratitude and someone who has shown none, someone of evident virtue and someone of evident fault, since the giving itself, not the recipient's worthiness, is what a bodhisattva's resolve actually concerns.

He unpacks the commentary's own classification of who might attempt, without success, to obstruct this resolve: human beings, meaning any being born with a human body in the human realm; non-human beings, comprising every other realm of rebirth — the hells, animals, hungry ghosts, and the eightfold host of divine and semi-divine beings including gods, dragons, yakshas, asuras, garudas, kimnaras, and mahoragas; renunciates and brahmins, meaning human practitioners whose aspiration, however sincere their outward renunciation, is actually directed toward becoming a demon-king or a Brahma-heaven deity rather than toward genuine liberation; and other parties in the world, referring to demons and Brahma-heaven gods themselves. He notes that even beings as powerful as demon-kings or Brahma-heaven deities, despite whatever obstruction they might attempt, cannot shake a bodhisattva's resolute commitment to give once that commitment has genuinely taken root — a striking testament to just how unshakeable "resolute cultivation" actually means. The treatise turns next to explaining the second quality, thorough cultivation, applied likewise to the perfection of giving.

22. Section: Thorough, Constant, and Faultless Giving — Withholding Nothing, Never Growing Weary, and Giving Only What Genuinely Benefits

The second quality, thorough cultivation, means that when a bodhisattva possesses various forms of giving-worthy wealth or teaching and a seeker appears, that bodhisattva gives everything without exception — nothing at all is withheld from any being; a bodhisattva capable of giving away even the very life within this body can hardly hesitate over mere external possessions. The third quality, constant cultivation, means that a bodhisattva never grows weary of giving, sustaining it continuously at every moment without interruption, giving away whatever is received the instant it is received, entirely without stinginess — never waiting to accumulate before giving. The fourth quality, faultless cultivation, means that a bodhisattva stays entirely free of the various tainted forms of giving already explained in the earlier chapter on the perfection of giving, cultivating instead only giving free of such taint — since wherever affliction is present, fault is present, and a bodhisattva who gives without affliction is precisely what "faultless cultivation" describes.

Chinese Source

云何菩薩於施波羅蜜多委悉修作？謂諸菩薩，現有種種可施財法，諸乞求者正現在前，一切施與，無有少物於諸有情而不能捨。於內身命尚能惠施，何況外物。

云何菩薩於施波羅蜜多恆常修作？謂諸菩薩，於修惠施無有厭倦，恆常無間，於一切時，隨有所得即隨惠施，無所吝惜。

云何菩薩於施波羅蜜多無罪修作？謂諸菩薩遠離如前施品所說諸雜染施，修行所餘無雜染施。

《披》遠離如前施品所說諸雜染施等者：施品中說：菩薩若知種種內外施物，於彼眾生唯令安樂、不作利益，或復於彼不作安樂、不作利益，便不施與。若知種種內外施物，於彼眾生定作利益、不定安樂，或復於彼定作利益、定作安樂，即便施與。（陵本三十九卷二頁3185）是名略說菩薩應施、不應施義。此說雜染及無雜染，如應當知。

Ru-Yi Translation

What is a bodhisattva's thorough cultivation of the perfection of giving? It means that bodhisattvas, presently possessing various forms of giving-worthy wealth or teaching, when a seeker appears, give everything, with no small thing at all that cannot be relinquished to sentient beings. Since a bodhisattva can give even their own inner body and life, how much more so external things.

What is a bodhisattva's constant cultivation of the perfection of giving? It means that bodhisattvas, in cultivating giving, are never weary, sustaining it constantly and without interruption, giving away at every moment whatever has just been received, without any stinginess.

What is a bodhisattva's faultless cultivation of the perfection of giving? It means that bodhisattvas stay far from the various tainted forms of giving already explained in the earlier chapter on the perfection of giving, cultivating instead the remaining, untainted giving.

Commentary: Regarding "staying far from the various tainted forms of giving already explained in the earlier chapter on the perfection of giving" — the chapter on the perfection of giving explains: if a bodhisattva knows that various internal or external items of giving would, for a given being, bring only ease without genuine benefit, or would bring neither ease nor benefit, that bodhisattva does not give them. If a bodhisattva knows that various internal or external items of giving would, for a given being, certainly bring benefit though not necessarily ease, or would certainly bring both benefit and ease, that bodhisattva does give them. This is, in brief, what should and should not be given. What is meant here by tainted and untainted giving should be understood accordingly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains thorough cultivation as giving that holds absolutely nothing back — a bodhisattva willing, in the most extreme cases recorded in the tradition, to give away head, eyes, marrow, flesh, and limbs for the sake of beings, making the withholding of mere external wealth almost unthinkable by comparison; this thoroughness distinguishes genuine giving from a merely partial or calculated generosity that keeps something valuable in reserve. He explains constant cultivation as giving unbroken by fatigue or hesitation, seizing the opportunity to give the very moment resources become available rather than accumulating a reserve first — the same principle already introduced under open-handed giving's own basis several milestones earlier, now applied specifically within the fourfold structure of skillfully cultivating the perfection of giving as an undertaking.

He unpacks faultless cultivation through the commentary's own citation of the treatise's earlier chapter on giving, fascicle 39, which lays out a genuinely discerning standard for what a bodhisattva actually gives: a bodhisattva declines to give something that would bring a recipient only fleeting ease without any real, lasting benefit, or something that would bring neither ease nor benefit at all, but does give whatever would bring certain benefit, whether or not it also brings ease, and certainly gives whatever brings both. He stresses the underlying principle this standard reveals: a bodhisattva's giving is never governed by simple sentiment or by whatever a recipient happens to want in the moment, but by genuine wisdom discerning what will actually help that recipient's own spiritual progress — tainted giving is giving that fails this test, contributing nothing to a recipient's genuine wholesome development, while untainted giving passes it, and it is precisely this discernment, free of any accompanying affliction, that makes giving faultless in the fullest sense.

23. Section: The Same Fourfold Structure Extends to All Six Perfections — Closing Skillfully Cultivating One's Undertaking, the First of the Four Dharmas, in Full

The treatise concludes its treatment of giving: a bodhisattva who cultivates giving in this way — resolutely, thoroughly, constantly, and faultlessly — is thereby said to skillfully cultivate the perfection of giving as an undertaking. It then extends this same fourfold structure to the remaining five perfections by direct analogy: exactly as with the perfection of giving, one should understand that moral discipline, patience, diligence, meditative concentration, and wisdom are likewise each to be cultivated resolutely, thoroughly, constantly, and faultlessly, as befits each. With this, the treatise concludes: this is what it means for a bodhisattva, through these four modes of cultivation, to skillfully cultivate all six perfections resolutely, thoroughly, constantly, and without fault — closing skillfully cultivating one's own undertaking, the first of the chapter's four dharmas, entirely in full.

Chinese Source

如是菩薩於施波羅蜜多能善修作。

如於施波羅蜜多能善修作，如是於戒、忍、精進、靜慮、慧波羅蜜多，如其所應，當知亦爾。

是名菩薩由四行相，於其六種波羅蜜多決定修作、委悉修作、恆常修作、無罪修作。

Ru-Yi Translation

In this way, a bodhisattva is able to skillfully cultivate the perfection of giving.

Just as one is able to skillfully cultivate the perfection of giving, one should understand that moral discipline, patience, diligence, meditative concentration, and the perfection of wisdom are, as befits each, likewise cultivated in the same way.

This is called a bodhisattva's cultivation, through these four modes, of resolute cultivation, thorough cultivation, constant cultivation, and faultless cultivation with respect to the six perfections.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes that the treatise, having established all four qualities in full detail through the single example of giving, simply extends the identical fourfold structure to the remaining five perfections by direct analogy rather than repeating the same explanation five more times — a bodhisattva applies the same resolute, thorough, constant, and faultless cultivation to moral discipline, patience, diligence, meditative concentration, and wisdom exactly as already shown for giving, trusting the reader to make the natural substitution in each case: resolute discipline unshaken by any temptation to break the precepts, thorough patience that endures every insult without exception, constant diligence sustained without a single day of laziness, and so forth across all six.

He treats this economical presentation as itself instructive: rather than needing six separate, elaborate explanations, a single worked example combined with a general principle of extension covers the entire domain, exactly the same rhetorical strategy the treatise has used at several earlier points across this fascicle. With this extension complete, he notes, the treatise's closing summary — that a bodhisattva cultivates all six perfections through these same four modes — brings the entire first of chapter two's four dharmas, skillfully cultivating one's own undertaking, to its full and complete close. The treatise turns next to the second dharma, skillful means, which the summary verse names immediately following.

24. Section: Skillful Means Opens — Ten Kinds, From Dispelling Hostility Toward the Teaching to Mastery of All Three Vehicles

The treatise turns to the second of chapter two's four dharmas: skillful means. One should understand that this skillful means comprises, in brief, ten kinds. The first is skillful means for dispelling the hostility of beings who resent and turn against the noble teaching. The second is skillful means for drawing neutral beings, neither hostile nor yet believing, into the teaching. The third is skillful means for maturing those who have already entered. The fourth is skillful means for bringing those already matured to liberation. The fifth is skillful means regarding every worldly discipline and treatise outside the teaching itself. The sixth is skillful means for correctly discerning the keeping and violation of the pure precept-discipline bodhisattvas uphold. The seventh is skillful means regarding a bodhisattva's own correct vows. The eighth is skillful means regarding the disciple's vehicle. The ninth is skillful means regarding the solitary awakener's vehicle. And the tenth is skillful means regarding the great vehicle itself.

Chinese Source

云何菩薩方便善巧？當知如是方便善巧，略有十種。何等為十？一者、憎背聖教有情，除其恚惱方便善巧；二者、處中有情，令其趣入方便善巧；三者、已趣入者，令其成熟方便善巧；四者、已成熟者，令得解脫方便善巧；五者、於諸世間一切異論方便善巧；六者、於諸菩薩淨戒律儀受持、毀犯，能正觀察方便善巧；七者、於諸正願方便善巧；八者、於聲聞乘方便善巧；九者、於獨覺乘方便善巧；十者、於其大乘方便善巧。

Ru-Yi Translation

What is a bodhisattva's skillful means? One should understand that this skillful means comprises, in brief, ten kinds. What are these ten? First, skillful means for dispelling the hostility of beings who resent and turn against the noble teaching. Second, skillful means for causing neutral beings to enter the teaching. Third, skillful means for maturing those who have already entered. Fourth, skillful means for bringing those already matured to liberation. Fifth, skillful means regarding every worldly discipline outside the teaching. Sixth, skillful means for correctly discerning the keeping and violation of the pure precept-discipline bodhisattvas receive and uphold. Seventh, skillful means regarding correct vows. Eighth, skillful means regarding the disciple's vehicle. Ninth, skillful means regarding the solitary awakener's vehicle. Tenth, skillful means regarding the great vehicle.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through all ten kinds of skillful means in turn. The first four trace a natural progression along which any being might travel toward the teaching: dispelling the hostility of beings who actively resent and oppose the noble teaching, so that hatred gives way to trust; drawing beings who are simply neutral — neither hostile nor yet believing — into the teaching for the first time, cultivating the seeds of faith, diligence, mindfulness, concentration, and wisdom; maturing beings who have already entered but whose wholesome roots are not yet ripe; and finally bringing beings whose faculties, wholesome roots, and wisdom have already ripened all the way to genuine liberation.

The fifth kind, he explains, concerns everything outside the teaching's own inner wisdom — logic, medicine, linguistics, and the arts and crafts, along with the full range of worldly learning and technical skill — mastery a bodhisattva needs specifically to subdue and convert practitioners of non-Buddhist paths, who would otherwise remain unreachable through purely doctrinal argument alone. The sixth concerns thorough mastery of what is permitted and prohibited across the three groups of bodhisattva precepts — restraining precepts, precepts gathering wholesome teachings, and precepts benefiting sentient beings — enabling a bodhisattva to keep the precepts purely and, should any violation occur, immediately confess and restore that purity, while also guiding other practitioners through the same process.

The seventh concerns a bodhisattva's own correct vows — the treatise's own chapter on the factors of awakening, fascicle 45, listing ten such vows: venerating the buddhas, upholding the true teaching, manifesting appearance in the world, gathering in wholesome teachings, maturing beings, universally carrying out buddha-work, purifying one's own buddha-land, sharing the same aspiration toward the great vehicle with fellow bodhisattvas, ensuring one's own effort is never wasted, and swiftly realizing awakening — skillful means here means knowing how to actually cultivate and fulfill these vows in practice. The eighth, ninth, and tenth concern mastery of the disciple's vehicle, the solitary awakener's vehicle, and the great vehicle respectively, since a bodhisattva who understands the specific paths and practices proper to each of the three vehicles is thereby equipped to guide beings of any of the three corresponding lineages toward their own fitting liberation. The treatise turns next to noting that all ten of these kinds of skillful means have already been explained at length elsewhere in the treatise.

25. Section: All Ten Kinds of Skillful Means Cross-Referenced to Their Own Fuller Treatment Elsewhere in the Treatise

The treatise closes its enumeration of the ten kinds of skillful means with a cross-reference: all of this skillful means, it notes, has already been explained extensively at its own appropriate place elsewhere within this very Bodhisattva Stage, and should be understood accordingly rather than repeated here in full. The commentary specifies exactly where: the first four kinds of skillful means correspond to the six kinds of skillful means for maturing beings already explained in the chapter on the factors of awakening; the fifth corresponds to skillfully knowing the various treatises, already explained in that same chapter; the sixth corresponds to the bodhisattva's own perfection of moral discipline, already explained in the chapter on discipline; the seventh corresponds to a bodhisattva's own correct vows, already explained in the chapter on the factors of awakening; and the eighth, ninth, and tenth together correspond to establishing through vehicles, already explained in the chapter on the qualities of a bodhisattva.

Chinese Source

如是一切方便善巧，如前即此菩薩地中，隨彼彼處已廣分別，如應當知。

《披》隨彼彼處已廣分別如應當知者：此中前四方便善巧，如前菩提分品，說有六種成熟有情方便應知。（陵本四十五卷五頁3626）又說善知諸論。（陵本四十四卷十七頁3597）即此第五方便善巧。又戒品說菩薩戒波羅蜜多。（陵本四十卷一頁3235）即此第六方便善巧。又菩提分品中說菩薩所修正願。（陵本四十五卷十六頁3657）即此第七方便善巧。功德品說乘施設建立。（陵本四十六卷十四頁3712）即此後三方便善巧。

Ru-Yi Translation

All of this skillful means, exactly as already explained at its appropriate place elsewhere within this very Bodhisattva Stage, has been explained at length; it should be understood accordingly.

Commentary: Regarding "explained at length at its appropriate place elsewhere, understood accordingly" — among these, the first four kinds of skillful means correspond to the six kinds of skillful means for maturing beings already explained in the chapter on the factors of awakening. That same chapter also explains skillfully knowing the various treatises — this is the fifth skillful means here. The chapter on moral discipline explains the bodhisattva's own perfection of moral discipline — this is the sixth skillful means here. The chapter on the factors of awakening also explains a bodhisattva's own cultivation of correct vows — this is the seventh skillful means here. The chapter on the qualities of a bodhisattva explains establishing through vehicles — these are the final three kinds of skillful means here.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this cross-referencing as further evidence of just how tightly interwoven the entire Bodhisattva Stage actually is — nearly every one of these ten kinds of skillful means already appeared, in fuller form, in material this same reader has already worked through across earlier fascicles. He recalls the six kinds of skillful means for maturing beings from the chapter on the factors of awakening (fascicle 45) as covering the first four kinds here in full; skillfully knowing the various treatises from that same chapter's own earlier material (fascicle 44) as covering the fifth, a bodhisattva's own thorough, well-received, well-retained mastery of the five classical sciences, sustained through ongoing diligence and refined through continued reflection; the bodhisattva's own perfection of moral discipline, treated across the chapter on discipline (fascicles 40 through 42), as covering the sixth; and the ten correct vows already discussed just above (fascicle 45's own account of the five deeper categories of vow: the vow to arouse the aspiration, the vow of favorable rebirth, the vow of conduct, correct vows themselves, and the great vow) as covering the seventh.

He notes that the eighth, ninth, and tenth kinds — skillful means regarding the disciple's, the solitary awakener's, and the great vehicle respectively — correspond directly to establishing through vehicles, already translated at considerable length in fascicle 46's own chapter on the qualities of a bodhisattva, where each of the three vehicles was examined through its own sevenfold structure of wisdom, basis, object, companions, function, provisions, and fruit. He observes that this deliberate, repeated cross-referencing rather than fresh repetition reflects the treatise's own consistent economy of presentation — trusting a reader who has followed the text continuously from the beginning to recall and apply material already thoroughly explained, rather than restating it identically each time it becomes relevant again in a new context. The treatise turns next to explaining the concrete functions these ten kinds of skillful means together actually accomplish.

26. Section: Five Functions of Skillful Means Open — Establishing Beings in Their Own Good, Subduing Rival Views, and Keeping the Precepts Radiantly Pure

The treatise turns to explaining what these ten kinds of skillful means actually accomplish: together, they perform five functions. First, correctly establishing the beings being taught in their own genuine good. Second, being able to overcome every rival doctrine. Third, enabling bodhisattvas not to commit what should not be committed, and, should a violation occur, swiftly and properly confessing and removing it, so that the pure precept-discipline a bodhisattva has received is thereby kept skillfully polished to a genuine brightness. Fourth, enabling bodhisattvas to bring every cherished future aim to its own complete fulfillment. And fifth, enabling bodhisattvas, according to each being's own lineage and resolute understanding, to teach a fitting teaching that accords with correct principle. The first four kinds of skillful means together produce the first function: correctly establishing the beings being taught in their own genuine good, guiding them toward the aims and benefits proper to whichever of the three vehicles suits them. The fifth kind of skillful means, mastery of every worldly discipline outside the teaching, produces the second function: enabling a bodhisattva to skillfully overcome every rival, non-Buddhist doctrine. And the sixth kind of skillful means, correct discernment regarding the bodhisattva precepts, produces the third function: enabling a bodhisattva to avoid committing what should not be committed, and, should any violation occur, to swiftly and properly confess and restore it, keeping the received precept-discipline genuinely, radiantly pure.

Chinese Source

如是十種菩薩所有方便善巧，能作五事。一、能正安立所化有情於自義利；二、能摧伏一切異論；三、能令諸菩薩不犯所犯，犯已速疾如法悔除，於善清淨菩薩所受淨戒律儀，能善修瑩；四、能令諸菩薩證當來一切所愛事義圓滿；五、能令諸菩薩於諸有情，隨其種性及勝解，說相稱法、說順正理。

謂由前四種方便善巧，令諸菩薩能正安立所化有情於自義利。

由於世間一切異論方便善巧，令諸菩薩善能摧伏一切異論。

由於菩薩淨戒律儀受持、毀犯，能正觀察方便善巧，令諸菩薩不犯所犯，犯已速疾如法悔除，於善清淨菩薩所受淨戒律儀，能善修瑩。

《披》於善清淨等者：善淨意樂，名善清淨。此說菩薩堪能受戒自性功德，由此堪受菩薩所受淨戒律儀。今說由彼方便善巧，令戒清淨，名善修瑩。

Ru-Yi Translation

These ten kinds of skillful means a bodhisattva possesses are able to perform five functions. First, correctly establishing the beings being taught in their own genuine good. Second, being able to overcome every rival doctrine. Third, enabling bodhisattvas not to commit what should not be committed, and, having committed it, swiftly and properly confessing and removing it, so that the pure precept-discipline received is skillfully kept polished bright. Fourth, enabling bodhisattvas to bring to complete fulfillment every cherished aim in the future. Fifth, enabling bodhisattvas, according to each being's own lineage and resolute understanding, to speak a fitting teaching, speaking in accordance with correct principle.

This means that, through the first four kinds of skillful means, bodhisattvas are able to correctly establish the beings being taught in their own genuine good.

Through skillful means regarding every worldly discipline, bodhisattvas are able to skillfully overcome every rival doctrine.

Through skillful means for correctly discerning the keeping and violation of the pure precept-discipline bodhisattvas receive, bodhisattvas do not commit what should not be committed, and, having committed it, swiftly and properly confess and remove it, keeping the pure precept-discipline received skillfully polished bright.

Commentary: Regarding "polished bright" and the like — a wholesome, pure aspiration is called genuinely pure. This refers to a bodhisattva's own capacity, an intrinsic quality that makes one fit to receive the precepts, through which one is able to receive the pure precept-discipline bodhisattvas receive. Here it is explained that, through that skillful means, the precepts are kept pure — this is called being skillfully polished bright.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains that this fivefold function moves from the most foundational task — simply establishing beings in their own genuine good, whichever of the three vehicles that good actually consists in — through progressively more specific accomplishments: overcoming rivals, maintaining one's own precept purity, fulfilling future aims, and finally teaching precisely what each being needs to hear. He notes that the treatise structures its explanation by mapping each function directly back to the specific kinds of skillful means responsible for producing it, beginning here with the first three functions: the first four kinds of skillful means (dispelling hostility, drawing in the neutral, maturing the entered, liberating the matured) together accomplish the fundamental work of establishing beings in their own good; the fifth kind (mastery of worldly disciplines) equips a bodhisattva to out-argue and overcome rival, non-Buddhist doctrines that might otherwise draw beings away from the true teaching; and the sixth kind (discernment regarding the bodhisattva precepts) keeps a bodhisattva's own precept-discipline continuously bright and pure, correcting any lapse the moment it occurs rather than letting it linger.

He unpacks the commentary's own gloss on "polished bright" as referring to the wholesome, pure aspiration that itself constitutes a bodhisattva's inherent capacity to receive the precepts in the first place — the sixth skillful means does not create this capacity from nothing but rather sustains and keeps luminous the purity already inherent in a bodhisattva who is genuinely fit to hold the precepts, much as regular polishing keeps an already-fine jewel radiant rather than manufacturing its brilliance anew each time. The treatise turns next to explaining the fourth and fifth functions, produced respectively by the seventh kind of skillful means and by the eighth, ninth, and tenth together.

27. Section: The Fourth and Fifth Functions Close — Fulfilling Every Cherished Future Aim, and Teaching Each Being According to Its Own Lineage, Closing Skillful Means Entirely

The fourth function, the treatise explains, comes through skillful means regarding correct vows: since a bodhisattva's correct vows are joined with the Buddha's own unsurpassed awakening, this skillful means enables a bodhisattva both to personally realize unsurpassed awakening and to bring beings to their own genuine benefit — the complete fulfillment of every cherished future aim, self-benefit and other-benefit alike. And the fifth function comes through skillful means regarding the three vehicles together: because a bodhisattva possesses skillful means regarding the disciple's, the solitary awakener's, and the great vehicle all three, that bodhisattva is able to teach each being a fitting teaching, matched precisely to that being's own lineage, faculty, and resolute understanding, and always in accordance with correct principle. With this, the treatise concludes: these ten kinds of skillful means together enable a bodhisattva to perform these five functions, and through these five functions, every undertaking — present and future alike — reaches its own ultimate completion, closing skillful means, the second of chapter two's four dharmas, entirely in full.

Chinese Source

由於正願方便善巧，令諸菩薩能證當來一切所愛事義圓滿。

由於三乘方便善巧，令諸菩薩於諸有情，隨其種性、根及勝解，說相稱法、說順正理。

是名十種方便善巧，令諸菩薩能作五事。由此五事，能令菩薩現法當來一切事義皆得究竟。

Ru-Yi Translation

Through skillful means regarding correct vows, bodhisattvas are able to realize the complete fulfillment of every cherished aim in the future.

Through skillful means regarding the three vehicles, bodhisattvas, according to each being's own lineage, faculty, and resolute understanding, are able to speak a fitting teaching, speaking in accordance with correct principle.

These ten kinds of skillful means, then, enable bodhisattvas to perform these five functions. Through these five functions, bodhisattvas bring every undertaking, present and future alike, to its own ultimate completion.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the fourth function as the direct fruit of a bodhisattva's correct vows, since a vow genuinely joined with the aspiration for unsurpassed awakening naturally propels both a bodhisattva's own eventual realization and that bodhisattva's capacity to bring beings to their own genuine liberation — self-benefit and benefiting others converging in this single fulfillment. He explains the fifth function as the natural payoff of mastering all three vehicles together: a bodhisattva who genuinely understands the disciple's path, the solitary awakener's path, and the great vehicle's own path can meet any being exactly where that being actually stands, matching the teaching offered to whatever lineage, faculty, and resolute understanding that particular being already possesses, rather than offering a single, undifferentiated teaching regardless of audience.

Surveying all five functions together, he observes a natural inner order: establishing beings in their own good is the most fundamental function, overcoming rivals and maintaining precept purity protect and sustain a bodhisattva's own capacity to keep performing that fundamental work, fulfilling future aims extends this work across time, and teaching fittingly according to each being's own capacity ensures the work is actually effective rather than generic. With this fivefold structure fully explained and each function traced back to its producing skillful means, he notes, skillful means — the second of chapter two's four dharmas — closes completely in full, its ten kinds and their five resulting functions together forming a genuinely comprehensive account of how a bodhisattva's wisdom translates into concrete, effective benefit for beings. The treatise turns next to the third dharma, benefiting others.

28. Section: Benefiting Others Through the Four Means of Gathering — Three Classes of Beings, Closing the Third Dharma in Full

The treatise turns to the third dharma, benefiting others, opening with the question: how does a bodhisattva actually benefit beings? Bodhisattvas, relying on the four means of gathering — giving, loving speech, beneficial conduct, and cooperation — bring benefit to one portion of beings, ease to another portion, and both benefit and ease together to a further portion of the beings being taught; this is, in brief, what a bodhisattva's benefiting of others consists of. Beings capable of receiving genuine intellectual and spiritual benefit accept the bodhisattva's own teaching directly, their thinking transformed and their wisdom sharpened enough to distinguish wholesome from unwholesome; beings not yet ready for such teaching accept only material ease, the practical resolution of some worldly difficulty, without yet being able to receive the deeper teaching itself; and beings already established within one of the three vehicles' own lineages receive both — genuine spiritual benefit together with whatever material support their circumstances require. This account is given only in brief here, the treatise notes, and should be understood in its fuller form exactly as already explained in the earlier chapter on benefiting self and others.

Chinese Source

云何菩薩饒益於他？謂諸菩薩依四攝事，即布施、愛語、利行、同事，能與一分有情利益，能與一分有情安樂，能與一分所化有情利益安樂，是名略說菩薩所有饒益於他。廣說如前自他利品，應知其相。

《披》能與一分有情利益等者：此中初二有情，但言一分，不言所化，當知此說不住種姓有情，與下一分所化有情有別。前說所調伏界無量，當知此名所化有情。於彼一切授與善法，名與利益；授與饒益，名與安樂。

Ru-Yi Translation

How does a bodhisattva benefit others? It means that bodhisattvas, relying on the four means of gathering — namely, giving, loving speech, beneficial conduct, and cooperation — are able to give benefit to a portion of beings, are able to give ease to a portion of beings, and are able to give benefit and ease together to a portion of the beings being taught. This is, in brief, called a bodhisattva's benefiting of others. Its fuller explanation is exactly as given earlier in the Chapter on Benefiting Self and Others; one should understand its characteristics accordingly.

Commentary: Regarding "able to give benefit to a portion of beings" and the like — here, the first two kinds of beings are described only as "a portion," without the qualifier "being taught," and one should understand that this refers to beings not abiding in any lineage, distinct from the "portion of beings being taught" mentioned afterward. The boundless realm of what is to be tamed, already explained earlier, one should understand, is what "beings being taught" refers to here. Bestowing wholesome teaching upon all such beings is called giving benefit; bestowing material aid is called giving ease.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi reprises the four means of gathering briefly: giving, comprising material giving, giving of the teaching, and giving of fearlessness; loving speech, comprising congratulatory, comforting, and beneficial speech in teaching; beneficial conduct, encouraging beings to actually take up practice toward liberation rather than remaining merely theoretical about the teaching; and cooperation, a bodhisattva's own willingness to set an example by actually practicing alongside those being guided rather than merely directing others from a distance — together, these four methods draw beings toward the teaching and sustain their progress once gathered in.

He draws out the commentary's own careful distinction among the three classes of beings named here: beings capable of genuine intellectual benefit are those with sufficient wisdom to actually receive and apply the teaching, their thinking transformed enough to distinguish wholesome from unwholesome, worldly from transcendent good, and to cease harming others as a result; beings capable only of material ease lack this same receptivity to the teaching itself but will gladly accept practical help — resolving a concrete worldly difficulty — without yet being able to absorb any deeper doctrinal content; and beings already established within one of the three vehicles' own lineages, called specifically "beings being taught" as already explained under the boundless realm of what is to be tamed in the earlier chapter on the qualities of a bodhisattva, receive both, benefiting from a bodhisattva's own spiritual guidance while also receiving whatever material support their present circumstances call for. Since this account is given only briefly here, he notes, the treatise refers the reader to its own fuller treatment in the earlier chapter on benefiting self and others for the complete picture — with this cross-reference, benefiting others, the third of chapter two's four dharmas, closes in full. The treatise turns next to the fourth and final dharma, undistorted dedication.

29. Section: Undistorted Dedication — Every Accumulated Merit Directed Solely Toward Awakening, Never Toward Worldly Reward, Closing All Four Dharmas in Full

The treatise turns to the fourth and final dharma, undistorted dedication, opening with the question: what constitutes a bodhisattva's undistorted dedication? Bodhisattvas, having accumulated wholesome roots through the three doors just explained — skillfully cultivating one's own undertaking, skillful means, and benefiting others — take up all such wholesome roots gathered across the past, future, and present alike, and, with a single, wholehearted, sincere, pure faith, dedicate and direct them all toward unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening. Never do bodhisattvas use these accumulated wholesome roots to seek any other worldly resultant fruit — except unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening itself, nothing else is ever sought. This constant, lifelong habituation, the treatise's own commentary explains, means gathering and increasing these same wholesome roots at every single moment, without exception, until such dedication itself becomes second nature.

Chinese Source

云何菩薩無倒迴向？謂諸菩薩三門積集所有善根，即善修事業、方便善巧、饒益於他。去來今世一切攝取，以淳一味妙淨信心，迴求無上正等菩提。終不用此所集善根，希求世間餘果異熟，唯除無上正等菩提。

《披》去來今世一切攝取者：謂一切時，於彼善根攝受、增長、串習成性故。

Ru-Yi Translation

What is a bodhisattva's undistorted dedication? It means that bodhisattvas, having accumulated all wholesome roots through three doors — namely, skillfully cultivating one's own undertaking, skillful means, and benefiting others — take up all of these wholesome roots across the past, future, and present, and, with a single, wholehearted, sincere, pure faith, dedicate and turn them toward unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening. Never do bodhisattvas use these accumulated wholesome roots to seek any other worldly resultant fruit — except unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening.

Commentary: Regarding "across the past, future, and present, take up all" — this means that, at every single time without exception, one gathers in, increases, and habituates these same wholesome roots until they become one's own nature.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains that undistorted dedication functions as the crucial capstone binding together everything the first three dharmas have already accomplished: without this fourth step, all the merit gathered through disciplined practice of the six perfections, skillful teaching methods, and concrete benefit to beings could still, in principle, drift toward some lesser, merely worldly aim — a comfortable future rebirth, wealth, or status — rather than toward awakening itself. He stresses the treatise's own vivid phrase "single, wholehearted, sincere, pure faith" as describing a dedication that is genuinely undivided, not split between competing aims, and genuinely pure, untainted by any residual craving for worldly reward; a bodhisattva sustained by such dedication would, even faced with the prospect of great worldly wealth and status, never redirect accumulated merit toward securing it, since such worldly rewards, however pleasant, are ultimately impermanent and, without accompanying wisdom, can even become the occasion for further unwholesome action, whereas the wisdom gained through genuine spiritual attainment remains permanently secure and can never itself become a source of wrongdoing.

He notes, with evident reassurance, that a bodhisattva who dedicates all merit exclusively toward awakening in this way does not thereby forfeit worldly benefit altogether — such benefit tends to arise naturally as a byproduct regardless, precisely because genuinely great merit rarely fails to produce some measure of worldly flourishing along the way — but the bodhisattva's own primary orientation remains fixed on awakening throughout. He then unpacks the commentary's gloss on sustaining this dedication "across the past, future, and present": a bodhisattva does not dedicate merit toward awakening only once, at some single decisive moment, but continuously, at every single moment without exception, gathering in, increasing, and habituating this same wholesome intention until it becomes an ingrained, natural disposition — precisely how, he notes, some beings appear to possess an innately compassionate, bodhisattva-like temperament from a very young age, without ever having been taught it, a disposition that can only be explained by exactly this kind of sustained habituation carried forward from countless previous lives. He draws the practical lesson directly: any practitioner cultivating wholesome roots today should likewise gather, increase, and habituate them in this same continuous way, so that the resulting disposition carries forward naturally into future lives as well. With this, undistorted dedication, the fourth and final dharma, closes in full — and with it, all four of chapter two's dharmas that let both householder and renunciate bodhisattvas swiftly attain unsurpassed awakening now stand complete. The treatise turns next to a further topic within this same chapter, explaining how these four dharmas comprehensively encompass everything a bodhisattva needs to learn.

30. Section: These Four Dharmas Comprise Everything the Buddha Teaches Bodhisattvas, and Bring the Hard-to-Attain Fruit Genuinely Near

The treatise now explains how these four dharmas comprehensively encompass everything a bodhisattva must learn: whatever the world-honored one has taught, for householder or renunciate bodhisattvas alike, regarding what should be learned, one should understand that all of it is comprised within just these four — skillfully cultivating one's own undertaking, skillful means, benefiting others, and undistorted dedication. It then explains why mastering these four brings a bodhisattva genuinely close to the fruit: bodhisattvas who skillfully cultivate their own undertaking, apply skillful means, benefit others, and dedicate without distortion in this way draw near to, approach, and press right up against unsurpassed awakening — a fruit otherwise so difficult to attain and so difficult to realize.

Chinese Source

世尊所有為在家分、或出家分諸菩薩說所應學法，當知一切此四所攝。謂善修事業、方便善巧、饒益於他、無倒迴向。

是故如是善修事業、方便善巧、饒益於他、無倒迴向諸菩薩眾，親近、鄰逼難得難證無上菩提。

Ru-Yi Translation

Whatever the world-honored one has taught as what should be learned, for householder or renunciate bodhisattvas, one should understand, is all comprised within these four: skillfully cultivating one's own undertaking, skillful means, benefiting others, and undistorted dedication.

Therefore, bodhisattvas who skillfully cultivate their own undertaking, apply skillful means, benefit others, and dedicate without distortion draw near to, approach, and press right up against unsurpassed awakening, otherwise so hard to attain and so hard to realize.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this comprehensive claim as the natural capstone to everything chapter two has established: whatever the Buddha has ever taught bodhisattvas, whether directed at householders or renunciates, reduces without remainder to just these four dharmas — a genuinely sweeping claim about the completeness of this fourfold framework. He then explains why the treatise describes awakening as "hard to attain and hard to realize" by citing the Avatamsaka Sutra's own commentarial tradition on the fourfold difficulty involved: difficult because it is too subtle to be the object of mere learning-based wisdom; difficult because it cannot be seen even by the mind's eye of reflective wisdom; difficult because it lies beyond conceptual thought entirely, not even the object of worldly cultivated wisdom, since only the genuine wisdom belonging to the grounds themselves can reach it; and difficult because it surpasses the mind's own ordinary ground altogether, exceeding not only what lies before the grounds but even the wisdom naturally born to noble ones already upon the grounds, since only a buddha's own wisdom, seeing reality exactly as it is, can be called "well-attained" in the fullest sense.

He explains that a bodhisattva who genuinely masters all four dharmas — resolute, thorough, constant, faultless cultivation of the six perfections; the ten kinds of skillful means and their five functions; the four means of gathering beings; and single-minded dedication of all merit toward awakening — thereby draws remarkably close to this otherwise forbiddingly difficult fruit, both the subsequently-attained wisdom that discriminates and teaches and the fundamental non-discriminating wisdom that directly realizes suchness converging as that bodhisattva's own practice matures. The treatise turns next to a final, sweeping confirmation that no bodhisattva, past, present, or future, has ever attained awakening by any other route.

31. Section: No Bodhisattva of Any Time Attains Awakening by Any Other Route — Then Comparing Householder Against Renunciate Begins

The treatise closes this sub-topic with a sweeping confirmation: one should understand that every bodhisattva of the past, future, and present, whether householder or renunciate, who has diligently trained and thereby already realized, will realize, or is presently realizing unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening, does so entirely through these same four dharmas — beyond them, there is nothing to be added and nothing lacking. The treatise then opens a further sub-topic, comparing relative superiority: although householder and renunciate bodhisattvas alike diligently train in these same four dharmas, renunciate bodhisattvas are, in fact, remarkably distinct from and superior to householder bodhisattvas. Asked why, the treatise begins its fivefold explanation: first, every renunciate bodhisattva is entirely freed from the difficulties of being entangled with parents, spouse, children, and relatives, while a householder bodhisattva is not so freed — a renunciate having relinquished the emotional bonds of family life, while a householder must still care for parents and cherish spouse and children, emotional attachments that inevitably become a burden, making ease of body and mind harder to sustain. Second, every renunciate bodhisattva is entirely freed from the hardship, urgent business, and anxious suffering involved in supporting parents and relatives through farming, trade, or service to a king, while a householder bodhisattva, needing to provide for dependents through exactly such worldly livelihoods, is not so freed, bearing genuine hardship and worry that a renunciate has already left behind.

Chinese Source

當知過去、未來、現在所有菩薩，或在家分、或出家分，精勤修學，於其無上正等菩提曾當現證，一切皆由如是四法。除此無有若過若增。

又諸菩薩，或在家分、或出家分，雖復同於如是四法正勤修學，而出家者於在家者，甚大殊異、甚大高勝。所以者何？當知一切出家菩薩，於其父母、妻子、親屬攝受過患皆得解脫，在家菩薩則不如是。

又復一切出家菩薩，於為攝受父母、親屬，營農、商賈、事王業等種種艱辛遽務憂苦皆得解脫，在家菩薩則不如是。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should understand that every bodhisattva of the past, future, and present, whether householder or renunciate, who has diligently trained and thereby already realized, will realize, or presently realizes unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening — all of this is entirely through these four dharmas. Beyond these, there is nothing to be added and nothing lacking.

Furthermore, although bodhisattvas, whether householder or renunciate, equally and diligently train in these same four dharmas, renunciates are, compared to householders, remarkably distinct and remarkably superior. Why is this? One should understand that every renunciate bodhisattva is entirely freed from the difficulties of being entangled with parents, spouse, and relatives, while a householder bodhisattva is not so freed.

Furthermore, every renunciate bodhisattva is entirely freed from the various hardships, urgent business, and anxious suffering involved in supporting parents and relatives through farming, trade, or service to a king, while a householder bodhisattva is not so freed.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this closing confirmation as underscoring, one final time, the completeness of the fourfold framework just explained — every single bodhisattva across all of time, without exception, whatever their outward circumstance, reaches awakening through exactly these four dharmas and no other route. He then introduces the chapter's final sub-topic, a direct comparison between the two divisions, noting that the treatise here does something it rarely does elsewhere with such directness: openly ranking renunciate practice above householder practice, even though both alike genuinely train in the identical four dharmas.

He unpacks the first two of the five reasons given: a renunciate bodhisattva, having relinquished the emotional bonds of family life at the very outset, is freed from the entanglements of mutual attachment and affection that inevitably accompany caring for parents, spouse, and children — bonds that, however loving, still function as a kind of burden limiting a householder's freedom of body and mind. The second reason extends this same point to the practical demands of livelihood: a householder bodhisattva, needing to support family members, must engage in farming, commerce, or service to worldly authority, each involving genuine hardship and anxiety, while a renunciate, having set aside these very obligations, is simply freed from this entire category of worldly struggle — not through any lack of compassion for family, but through having structured life differently around the priorities the renunciate path demands. The treatise turns next to the remaining three reasons.

32. Section: Renunciates Outpace Householders in Celibate Practice, in the Factors of Awakening, and in the Authority Their Words Carry — Chapter Two Closes, Chapter Three Opens

Third, every renunciate bodhisattva is able to practice celibate conduct single-mindedly, as if bound by hook and chain, while a householder bodhisattva is not so able, still engaged in ordinary sexual conduct that a renunciate has entirely set aside. Fourth, every renunciate bodhisattva is able to swiftly realize penetrating wisdom with respect to the entire range of factors of awakening, bringing whatever wholesome practice is undertaken quickly to its own culmination, while a householder bodhisattva, burdened by household duties and worldly affairs, is not so able, progress necessarily slower given divided attention. And fifth, every renunciate bodhisattva abides in a settled, genuinely pure precept-discipline, such that whatever that bodhisattva says is trusted and followed by the assembly, while a householder bodhisattva is not so able — a householder still entangled in desire who teaches others to abandon desire simply carries less persuasive authority than a renunciate whose own life already visibly embodies the very freedom being taught. With these five reasons given, the treatise concludes: across these and countless further immeasurable wholesome qualities besides, one should understand that renunciate bodhisattvas are, compared to householder bodhisattvas, remarkably distinct and remarkably superior — closing chapter two, the divisions of householder and renunciate bodhisattva, entirely in full. Chapter three, on excellent aspiration, then opens — a bodhisattva's own vow toward unsurpassed awakening, aroused with exceptional vigor and force.

Chinese Source

又復一切出家菩薩，一向能行鉤鎖梵行，在家菩薩則不如是。

又復一切出家菩薩，普於一切菩提分法速證通慧，隨所造修彼彼善法，皆能疾疾到於究竟，在家菩薩則不如是。

又復一切出家菩薩，安住決定清淨律儀，凡所發言眾咸信奉，在家菩薩則不如是。

《披》安住決定清淨律儀者：於戒波羅蜜多決定修作、無罪修作，是名決定及與清淨。

如是等類無量善法，當知一切出家菩薩於在家者，甚大殊異、甚大高勝。

本地分中菩薩地第二持隨法瑜伽處增上意樂品第三

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, every renunciate bodhisattva is able to practice celibate conduct single-mindedly, as if bound by hook and chain, while a householder bodhisattva is not so able.

Furthermore, every renunciate bodhisattva is able to swiftly realize penetrating wisdom with respect to the entire range of factors of awakening, bringing whatever wholesome practice is undertaken quickly to its own culmination, while a householder bodhisattva is not so able.

Furthermore, every renunciate bodhisattva abides in settled, genuinely pure precept-discipline, such that whatever is spoken is trusted and followed by the assembly, while a householder bodhisattva is not so able.

Commentary: Regarding "abides in settled, genuinely pure precept-discipline" — resolutely and faultlessly cultivating the perfection of moral discipline is called settled and pure.

Across these and countless further immeasurable wholesome qualities of this kind, one should understand that every renunciate bodhisattva is, compared to a householder, remarkably distinct and remarkably superior.

Within the Basic Section, the Bodhisattva Stage, the second yoga foundation of sustained practice, chapter three: excellent aspiration.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the image of "hook and chain" celibate practice as describing a discipline that firmly restrains conduct exactly the way a hook and chain restrain movement — the precept of celibacy functioning as the hook binding unwholesome bodily and verbal action, and the true teaching itself functioning as the chain binding inner desire, a control a renunciate can sustain single-mindedly in a way a householder, still engaged in ordinary married life, simply cannot. He explains the fourth reason as following directly from the freedom already established by the first three: a renunciate whose time is not divided among family obligations, livelihood, and worldly affairs can devote that same freed time entirely to conscience, shame, and the full range of the thirty-seven factors of awakening already explained at length in the earlier chapter on that very subject, reaching completion far more swiftly than a householder whose attention remains necessarily divided.

He explains the fifth reason through a simple but telling example: a householder still entangled in desire who nonetheless urges others to abandon desire will always carry less persuasive force than a renunciate who has actually already left desire behind, since the renunciate's own visibly embodied freedom lends an authority mere verbal instruction alone cannot match — precisely why the commentary specifies that a renunciate's precept-discipline is both "settled," meaning resolutely and continuously cultivated, and "pure," meaning cultivated entirely free of any accompanying fault. He closes by noting that the treatise deliberately leaves the list open-ended — these five named differences merely illustrate a far larger set of advantages renunciate life confers, without exhausting them.

Introducing chapter three, he glosses "excellent aspiration" through its full technical meaning: the Sanskrit term underlying "yoga foundation" carries the sense of capacity, specifically the capacity to accord with ultimate truth, while "excellent aspiration" itself combines "excellent," meaning vigorous, powerful, and supreme, with "aspiration," meaning intention or vow — together naming the exceptionally forceful, unshakeable vow toward unsurpassed awakening that a bodhisattva at this stage of the path has come to hold. He notes that this third chapter follows naturally on the second: having explained the concrete dharmas and the comparative advantages of householder versus renunciate practice, the treatise now turns to the inner quality of aspiration itself that sustains a bodhisattva's practice regardless of outward circumstance — the treatise turns next to unpacking excellent aspiration's own sevenfold compassion as its first topic.

33. Section: Excellent Aspiration Opens — Seven Kinds of Compassion, Fifteen Kinds of Excellent Aspiration, and Ten Resulting Accomplishments

Chapter three opens by asking: what is a bodhisattva's excellent aspiration? A summary verse answers: the wise, toward sentient beings, hold seven aspects of compassion; fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration; and one should understand these accomplish ten undertakings. The prose explanation opens with the first of these three topics: bodhisattvas, from deep within their own hearts, give rise to seven aspects of compassion toward sentient beings — and precisely because bodhisattvas possess this sevenfold compassion, such aspiration is called wholesome aspiration, or, in its fullest form, utterly wholesome aspiration. Wholesome aspiration names the compassion an ordinary bodhisattva, still at the stage of resolute practice, gives rise to; utterly wholesome aspiration names the fully perfected compassion of a bodhisattva who has already entered the noble stage. What are these seven aspects of compassion? They are: fearless compassion, reasoned compassion, tireless compassion, unsolicited compassion, untainted compassion, vast compassion, and equal compassion.

Chinese Source

云何菩薩增上意樂？嗢柁南曰：智者於有情　有七相憐愍　十五勝意樂　作十事應知

謂諸菩薩，於諸有情深心發起七相憐愍。以諸菩薩具憐愍故，名善意樂、極善意樂。

《披》名善意樂極善意樂者：此中意樂，通說勝解行地，乃至到究竟地。由是差別，名善、極善。

何等名為七相憐愍？一者、無畏憐愍，二者、如理憐愍，三者、無倦憐愍，四者、無求憐愍，五者、無染憐愍，六者、廣大憐愍，七者、平等憐愍。

Ru-Yi Translation

What is a bodhisattva's excellent aspiration? A summary verse states: "The wise, toward sentient beings, hold seven aspects of compassion; fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration; one should know they accomplish ten undertakings."

This means that bodhisattvas, from deep within their own hearts, give rise to seven aspects of compassion toward sentient beings. Because bodhisattvas possess this compassion, it is called wholesome aspiration, or utterly wholesome aspiration.

Commentary: Regarding "called wholesome aspiration, or utterly wholesome aspiration" — this aspiration spoken of here spans from the stage of resolute practice all the way to the ultimate ground. Because of this distinction, it is called wholesome or utterly wholesome respectively.

What are these seven aspects of compassion? First, fearless compassion; second, reasoned compassion; third, tireless compassion; fourth, unsolicited compassion; fifth, untainted compassion; sixth, vast compassion; seventh, equal compassion.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi previews chapter three's own tripartite structure exactly as the summary verse lays it out: seven aspects of compassion, examined first; fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration, examined second; and ten resulting undertakings these fifteen kinds of aspiration accomplish, examined third.
