He notes with evident satisfaction that this same sevenfold compassion and fifteenfold aspiration were already referenced by name several times in the immediately preceding fascicle's own chapter on the qualities of a bodhisattva — under the title "compassionate one" and under the account of clean, excellent aspiration belonging to the first ground — so that a reader who has followed the treatise continuously now finally arrives at the primary passage those earlier cross-references were pointing toward all along.

He explains the distinction between wholesome and utterly wholesome aspiration as tracking the same basic divide between ordinary and noble practitioner already familiar from many earlier discussions: an ordinary bodhisattva, still within the provisioning and applied-effort stages culminating in the four stages of warmth, summit, forbearance, and supreme mundane dharma, has not yet entered the noble path, and the compassion such a bodhisattva cultivates, while genuine, is called simply wholesome aspiration; a noble bodhisattva, from the first ground of utmost joy through the tenth and final ground, has brought that same compassion to full, complete perfection, and it is this fully matured compassion that earns the name utterly wholesome aspiration. The treatise turns next to explaining each of the seven aspects of compassion in its own full detail, beginning with fearless compassion.

34. Section: Fearless Compassion, and Reasoned Compassion Grounded in Genuinely Wholesome Conduct

The first aspect, fearless compassion, describes a bodhisattva who gives rise to compassion toward beings not out of fear or dread but from genuine care, manifesting conduct of body, speech, and mind that accords with beings' own needs, fitting their hearts and bringing them benefit and ease. The second aspect, reasoned compassion, describes a bodhisattva whose compassion never operates in a way contrary to reason — never guiding beings by means of what is not the true teaching, not the true discipline, not genuinely wholesome conduct, or through improper occasion, but always in accord with correct principle. Genuinely wholesome conduct, the treatise's own commentary explains, means a bodhisattva's own disposition of never wronging or deceiving others — free of any betrayal born of distorted thinking, of misplaced trust, of neglected service, of broken agreement, or of exploiting another's own difficult circumstance — approaching every being with a relaxed, open countenance, meeting their gaze directly, greeting them first with a smile, habitually speaking words of genuine affection, gladly keeping the company of those pursuing the same wholesome path, and remaining, in body and mind alike, thoroughly clear and untroubled.

Chinese Source

謂諸菩薩，於諸有情非怖畏故而起憐愍，現行隨順身語意業，適可其心利益安樂，是名菩薩無畏憐愍。

又諸菩薩，於諸有情非不如理憐愍而轉，謂終不以非法、非律、非賢善行，及以非處勸授有情，是名菩薩如理憐愍。

《披》不以非法非律等者：惡說法毗奈耶，是名非法非律。遠離顰蹙，舒顏平視，含笑先言，常為愛語，性多攝受善法朋侶，身心澄淨，是名賢善。如聲聞地說。（陵本二十五卷二十三頁2161）與此相違，是名非賢善行。於彼彼事理有相違，是名非處。

Ru-Yi Translation

This means that bodhisattvas give rise to compassion toward sentient beings not out of fear, manifesting conduct of body, speech, and mind that accords with beings' own needs, fitting their hearts, bringing benefit and ease. This is called a bodhisattva's fearless compassion.

Furthermore, bodhisattvas' compassion toward sentient beings never operates contrary to reason — meaning that they never, in the end, guide beings by means of what is not the true teaching, not the true discipline, not genuinely wholesome conduct, or improper occasion. This is called a bodhisattva's reasoned compassion.

Commentary: Regarding "not by means of what is not the true teaching, not the true discipline" and the like — a wrongly-taught doctrine and discipline is called not the true teaching, not the true discipline. Keeping a relaxed countenance free of a furrowed brow, meeting another's gaze directly, greeting them first with a smile, habitually speaking words of affection, being disposed to keep company with companions in wholesome practice, and remaining clear in body and mind — this is called genuinely wholesome. This is as explained in the Stage of Disciples. Its opposite is called not genuinely wholesome conduct. Wherever a given matter contradicts correct principle, this is called improper occasion.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains fearless compassion as compassion grounded purely in genuine care for beings rather than in any anxious motive of self-protection or self-interest — a bodhisattva's outward conduct of body, speech, and mind naturally aligning with whatever a given being actually needs, precisely because the underlying motivation is untainted by fear in the first place. He then unpacks reasoned compassion's fourfold negative specification, each ruling out a particular way compassion could go astray: never guiding beings through a wrongly-taught doctrine and discipline, since compassion misapplied through false teaching would only mislead rather than genuinely help; and never guiding them through conduct that fails to be genuinely wholesome or through circumstances contrary to correct principle.

He draws out the commentary's own rich, concrete description of what genuinely wholesome conduct actually looks like in practice, citing the treatise's earlier Stage of Disciples at length: never betraying others' trust through distorted thinking, through misplaced confidence they have shown, through neglecting service owed, through breaking an agreement, or through exploiting some vulnerability in their circumstances — and, in positive terms, meeting others with a relaxed, open face rather than a scowl, looking them in the eye respectfully, smiling and greeting them first without any trace of arrogance, habitually speaking affectionately, gladly seeking out companions in wholesome practice, and remaining, inside and out, genuinely untroubled and clear. He notes this same standard of "genuinely wholesome conduct" applies equally across both the lesser and great vehicles, since it describes basic human decency and integrity rather than anything specific to Mahayana practice alone. The treatise turns next to the third aspect, tireless compassion.

35. Section: Tireless, Unsolicited, and Untainted Compassion — Compassion Sustained Without Fatigue, Without Waiting to Be Asked, and Without Seeking Any Return

The third aspect, tireless compassion, describes a bodhisattva who, having given rise to fearless and reasoned compassion, undertakes whatever benefiting activity suits a given being's own needs through the four means of gathering, without ever growing weary of it. The fourth aspect, unsolicited compassion, describes a bodhisattva who, without waiting for beings to actually request help, spontaneously gives rise to compassion and acts to benefit them. And the fifth aspect, untainted compassion, describes a bodhisattva whose compassion arises entirely free of any attached, craving mind — benefiting others while seeking neither their gratitude in return nor any pleasant future karmic result — also called compassion without a fixed object, since it extends without discrimination across the same five kinds of beings already identified as compassion's own basis: beings who suffer, beings of wrong conduct, heedless beings, wrongly-practicing beings, and beings still latent with affliction.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩，於諸有情如是憐愍，隨其所宜發起一切饒益事業，曾無厭倦，是名菩薩無倦憐愍。

又諸菩薩，於諸有情不待求請，自起憐愍，為作饒益，是名菩薩無求憐愍。

又諸菩薩，於諸有情無愛染心而起憐愍，謂饒益他，不祈恩報，亦不希望當來可愛諸果異熟，是名菩薩無染憐愍，亦名菩薩無緣憐愍。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, bodhisattvas, having given rise to this compassion toward sentient beings, undertake, as suited to that being, every benefiting activity, without ever growing weary of it. This is called a bodhisattva's tireless compassion.

Furthermore, bodhisattvas, without waiting for sentient beings to request it, spontaneously give rise to compassion and act to benefit them. This is called a bodhisattva's unsolicited compassion.

Furthermore, bodhisattvas give rise to compassion toward sentient beings free of any attached, craving mind — meaning that, in benefiting others, they seek neither gratitude in return nor hope for any pleasant future karmic result. This is called a bodhisattva's untainted compassion, also called a bodhisattva's compassion without a fixed object.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi cites Elder Miao Jing's own teaching on how tireless compassion is actually sustained across different stages of the path: an ordinary bodhisattva at the stage of resolute practice may indeed grow physically or mentally fatigued from sustained benefiting activity, but is able to enter meditative absorption to dissolve that fatigue and continue; a bodhisattva who has already entered the noble stage, possessing far greater capacity and wisdom, simply does not experience such fatigue at all — "tireless" therefore describes both a genuine achievement at the ordinary stage and an effortless natural condition at the noble stage.

He explains unsolicited compassion as compassion that takes initiative rather than merely responding when asked — a bodhisattva actively watches for opportunities to benefit beings rather than waiting passively to be approached, since many beings in genuine need may never think to ask at all. For untainted compassion, he distinguishes the two closely related names the treatise gives it: "untainted" specifically describes the absence of any hope for gratitude or future pleasant reward, no attachment or self-interest clinging to the act of benefiting others; "compassion without a fixed object" describes instead the breadth of untainted compassion's own reach, extending without discrimination across the same fivefold basis for compassion already explained in chapter one — beings who suffer, beings of wrong conduct, heedless beings, wrongly-practicing beings, and beings still latent with affliction — compassion given freely to all five without picking and choosing among them. The treatise turns next to the sixth and seventh aspects, vast compassion and equal compassion.

36. Section: Vast Compassion That Never Retaliates, and Equal Compassion Extending Without Limit — Closing All Seven Aspects in Full

The sixth aspect, vast compassion, describes a bodhisattva whose compassion toward beings is exclusively vast, never small or narrow — meaning that, even when subjected to every kind of harmful treatment at the hands of beings everywhere, a bodhisattva never abandons them; a bodhisattva would rather personally undergo something genuinely unwanted than ever wish harm upon another in return. And the seventh aspect, equal compassion, describes how a bodhisattva, possessing every one of these characteristics and qualities of compassion just explained, extends them equally and impartially across the entire realm of sentient beings, without any limit or boundary drawn anywhere within it. With this seventh aspect, the treatise concludes: a bodhisattva joined with these seven aspects of compassion is thereby called possessed of wholesome aspiration, or utterly wholesome aspiration — wholesome aspiration at the stage prior to the noble path, and utterly wholesome aspiration once the noble grounds, all the way through the tenth and most ultimate, have been reached — closing the first of chapter three's three topics, the seven aspects of compassion, entirely in full.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩，於諸有情所起憐愍，唯是廣大而非狹小。言廣大者，謂於一切諸有情所，雖遭一切不饒益事，而不棄捨；菩薩自身寧受非愛，終不以惡欲加於彼。是名菩薩廣大憐愍。

又諸菩薩，如是相狀、如是功德相應憐愍，普於一切諸有情類平等平等，於有情界無有分限，是名菩薩平等憐愍。

菩薩與此七種行相憐愍相應，名善意樂、極善意樂。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, the compassion bodhisattvas give rise to toward sentient beings is exclusively vast, never narrow. What is meant by vast is that, toward all sentient beings, even when subjected to every kind of harmful treatment, a bodhisattva never abandons them; a bodhisattva would rather personally undergo something unwanted than ever, in the end, wish to inflict harm upon another. This is called a bodhisattva's vast compassion.

Furthermore, bodhisattvas, possessing compassion joined with characteristics and qualities such as these, extend it equally and impartially across the entire class of sentient beings, without any limit or boundary within the realm of beings. This is called a bodhisattva's equal compassion.

A bodhisattva joined with these seven aspects of compassion is called possessed of wholesome aspiration, or utterly wholesome aspiration.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains vast compassion as the capacity to sustain genuine care for beings even under the most provocative circumstances — insulted, harmed, betrayed by the very beings that compassion is directed toward, a bodhisattva's underlying commitment to their welfare simply does not waver, and would sooner absorb unwanted harm personally than ever retaliate with ill will, however justified retaliation might seem by ordinary standards. He treats this as compassion's own test of genuineness: a compassion that evaporates the moment it meets resistance or ingratitude was never truly vast to begin with.

He explains equal compassion as the final, integrating aspect gathering everything the preceding six have already established — fearlessness, accordance with reason, tirelessness, spontaneity, freedom from taint, and vastness — and extending this entire complex of qualities without exception across the whole of the realm of beings, drawing no line anywhere that would exclude some class of being from its reach. Surveying all seven together, he notes that they build in a natural, cumulative order: fearless and reasoned compassion establish compassion's own proper foundation and direction; tireless and unsolicited compassion describe its sustained, proactive energy; untainted compassion describes its purity of motive; and vast and equal compassion describe its ultimate scope, extending without limit to every being regardless of how that being treats the bodhisattva in return. With all seven aspects now closed, the first of chapter three's three topics reaches its own complete conclusion, distinguishing wholesome aspiration at the ordinary stage from utterly wholesome aspiration once the noble grounds are reached. The treatise turns next to the second of the three topics, the fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration.

37. Section: Fifteen Kinds of Excellent Aspiration Opens — Defined as Pure Faith and Discerning Wisdom Converging Into Settled Resolve

The treatise turns to the second of chapter three's three topics: one should understand that here, taking pure faith as the guide and discernment of the teaching as the guide, arriving at settled, confirmed resolute understanding and approval regarding the buddha's own teachings, is what is called a bodhisattva's excellent aspiration. This same excellent aspiration, the treatise then announces, comprises, in brief, fifteen kinds: the most supreme aspiration, the restraining aspiration, the aspiration of the perfections, the aspiration of genuine reality, the aspiration of power, the aspiration of benefit, the aspiration of ease, the aspiration of liberation, the firm aspiration, the undistorted aspiration, the impure aspiration, the pure aspiration, the utterly pure aspiration, the aspiration for what is to be tamed, and the innate aspiration.

Chinese Source

當知此中，淨信為先、擇法為先，於諸佛法所有勝解印解決定，是名菩薩增上意樂。

《披》於諸佛法所有勝解等者：此中佛法，謂即無上菩提及與菩提方便。信他諸佛菩薩而行，是名印解。自內了知真實，是名決定。

如是菩薩增上意樂，當知略說有十五種。何等十五？一、最上意樂，二、遮止意樂，三、波羅蜜多意樂，四、真實義意樂，五、威力意樂，六、利益意樂，七、安樂意樂，八、解脫意樂，九、堅固意樂，十、無虛妄意樂，十一、不清淨意樂，十二、清淨意樂，十三、善清淨意樂，十四、應調伏意樂，十五、俱生意樂。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should understand that here, taking pure faith as the guide and taking discernment of the teaching as the guide, arriving at settled, confirmed resolute understanding and approval regarding all the buddha's own teachings, is called a bodhisattva's excellent aspiration.

Commentary: Regarding "resolute understanding regarding all the buddha's own teachings" — the teaching meant here is unsurpassed awakening itself together with the means to it. Trusting and following the buddhas and bodhisattvas is called approval. Personally, inwardly realizing the truth is called settled resolve.

One should understand that this excellent aspiration of a bodhisattva comprises, in brief, fifteen kinds. What are these fifteen? First, the most supreme aspiration; second, the restraining aspiration; third, the aspiration of the perfections; fourth, the aspiration of genuine reality; fifth, the aspiration of power; sixth, the aspiration of benefit; seventh, the aspiration of ease; eighth, the aspiration of liberation; ninth, the firm aspiration; tenth, the undistorted aspiration; eleventh, the impure aspiration; twelfth, the pure aspiration; thirteenth, the utterly pure aspiration; fourteenth, the aspiration for what is to be tamed; fifteenth, the innate aspiration.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the treatise's own opening definition as combining two distinct components into a single settled resolve: pure faith, a confidence toward the three jewels already purified of doubt through prior accumulated merit, and discerning wisdom, cultivated within calm-abiding and insight, capable of correctly determining the teaching's own truth — citing the Compendium on Mutual Association's own gloss that a disciple who hears the Buddha's teaching on discipline, concentration, wisdom, and causality, reflects on it correctly, and practices in accordance with it, is said to possess this same trusting accord. He explains the commentary's own careful distinction between "approval," meaning trust in the buddhas and bodhisattvas as one's guide, and "settled resolve," meaning a bodhisattva's own personal, inward realization of the three natures and three non-natures — approval alone, however sincere, is not yet the full accomplishment; only when it ripens into direct, personal realization does excellent aspiration reach its complete form.

He notes that this excellent aspiration spans the entire path, from an ordinary bodhisattva's own contaminated cultivation during the provisioning and applied-effort stages all the way through the uncontaminated realization of a noble bodhisattva from the first ground to the tenth, cultivated and habituated continuously rather than achieved once and left static. He previews the fifteen kinds only briefly here, noting that each will be explained in its own turn: reverence for the three jewels' own supreme excellence; joy specifically in the restraining function of the precepts; delight in cultivating the remaining five perfections; delight in realizing personal and phenomenal emptiness; delight in the buddhas' and bodhisattvas' own inconceivable power; the wish to give beings wholesome teaching; the wish to give beings material ease; freedom from attachment to either the beings helped or the results of helping them; unwavering resolve toward awakening itself; undistorted wisdom regarding the actual means of helping beings and attaining awakening; and finally four aspirations tracking degrees of purity and readiness across the path's later stages, closing with the aspiration innate to the very last stage before buddhahood. The treatise turns next to explaining each of these fifteen kinds in its own full detail, beginning with the most supreme aspiration.

38. Section: The Most Supreme Aspiration Toward the Three Jewels, and the Restraining Aspiration Delighting in the Precepts

The first of the fifteen, the most supreme aspiration, describes bodhisattvas who give rise to excellent aspiration toward the Buddha, the teaching, and the community as supreme and genuinely real — their radiant, vast qualities eclipsing every worldly and non-Buddhist alternative, which is precisely why they are called supreme. The second, the restraining aspiration, describes bodhisattvas who give rise to excellent aspiration specifically toward the pure precept-discipline they have received and uphold — precepts that restrain wrongdoing, delighted in not as mere obligation but as a genuine source of joy.

Chinese Source

謂諸菩薩，於佛法僧最上真實起勝意樂，是名菩薩最上意樂。

《披》於佛法僧最上真實者：謂佛法僧真實功德，映蔽一切世間外道，說名最上。

又諸菩薩，於所受持淨戒律儀起勝意樂，是名菩薩遮止意樂。

Ru-Yi Translation

This means that bodhisattvas give rise to excellent aspiration toward the Buddha, the teaching, and the community as supreme and genuinely real. This is called a bodhisattva's most supreme aspiration.

Commentary: Regarding "toward the Buddha, the teaching, and the community as supreme and genuinely real" — the genuine qualities of the Buddha, the teaching, and the community eclipse every worldly and non-Buddhist alternative; this is why they are called supreme.

Furthermore, bodhisattvas give rise to excellent aspiration toward the pure precept-discipline they have received and uphold. This is called a bodhisattva's restraining aspiration.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi briefly surveys the three jewels' own genuine qualities underlying the first aspiration: the Buddha, whether Shakyamuni or any buddha across the ten directions and three times, self-awakened and awakening others with perfectly complete conduct; the teaching, comprising the twelve divisions of scripture disclosing dependent origination and emptiness, itself unfolding into instructional teaching, cultivated practice, revealed principle, and realized fruit; and the community, comprising the noble ones of all three vehicles who have genuinely attained the path, a field of merit deserving the world's own reverence. A bodhisattva's delight in these qualities, so far beyond anything worldly or non-Buddhist teaching can offer, is precisely what the most supreme aspiration describes — he notes in passing that Avalokiteshvara's own crown, bearing an image of Amitabha Buddha, visually embodies exactly this constant, reverent aspiration toward the three jewels.

He explains the second aspiration as a bodhisattva's genuine delight — not mere dutiful compliance — in the precepts specifically for their restraining function, preventing unwholesome conduct of body and speech before it can occur; "pure precepts" refers to the inward discipline of mind, while "discipline" refers to the outward bodily and verbal conduct that precept-keeping actually produces in practice. The treatise turns next to the third aspiration, delight in the remaining five perfections.

39. Section: Delight in the Remaining Perfections, in Genuine Reality, and in the Buddhas' Own Inconceivable Power

The third aspiration, the aspiration of the perfections, describes bodhisattvas who give rise to excellent aspiration toward the cultivation and realization of giving, patience, diligence, meditative concentration, and wisdom. The fourth, the aspiration of genuine reality, describes bodhisattvas who give rise to excellent aspiration toward personal selflessness, phenomenal selflessness, and the profound, ultimate suchness of all phenomena — the very domain a noble one's own wisdom takes as its object. And the fifth, the aspiration of power, describes bodhisattvas who give rise to excellent aspiration toward the buddhas' and bodhisattvas' own inconceivable supernormal power, and toward their innate power alike — the noble power of the six supernormal knowledges, the power born from cultivating the six perfections, and the innate power of merit simply accompanying a buddha's own manifestation in the world.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩，於所修證施、忍、精進、靜慮、般若起勝意樂，是名菩薩波羅蜜多意樂。

又諸菩薩，於法無我、補特伽羅無我、甚深勝義諸法真如起勝意樂，是名菩薩真實義意樂。

又諸菩薩，於佛菩薩不可思議神通威力、或俱生威力起勝意樂，是名菩薩威力意樂。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, bodhisattvas give rise to excellent aspiration toward the cultivation and realization of giving, patience, diligence, meditative concentration, and wisdom. This is called a bodhisattva's aspiration of the perfections.

Furthermore, bodhisattvas give rise to excellent aspiration toward the selflessness of phenomena, the selflessness of the person, and the profound, ultimate suchness of all phenomena. This is called a bodhisattva's aspiration of genuine reality.

Furthermore, bodhisattvas give rise to excellent aspiration toward the buddhas' and bodhisattvas' own inconceivable supernormal power, and toward their innate power. This is called a bodhisattva's aspiration of power.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes that the third aspiration simply extends the restraining aspiration's own delight in the precepts (already the second perfection) to the remaining five, a bodhisattva finding genuine joy in cultivating giving, patience, diligence, meditative concentration, and wisdom rather than experiencing them as mere obligation. He illustrates the fourth aspiration with Elder Miao Jing's own teaching from the Diamond Sutra — that a listener who hears this scripture with a mind of pure faith gives rise directly to the reality it describes — noting that the deeper one's own understanding of personal and phenomenal emptiness genuinely grows, the more naturally joy arises alongside it, this joy itself being what the aspiration of genuine reality names.

He unpacks the fifth aspiration through the treatise's own threefold account of power already introduced in the earlier chapter on power, fascicle 37: noble power, the six supernormal knowledges attained through a buddha's or bodhisattva's own thoroughly pacified, well-tamed mind, allowing compassionate, wisdom-guided response to beings' every need — illustrated by Avalokiteshvara's own vow to appear wherever invoked amid flood, fire, or other calamity; the power born from cultivating the six perfections as the actual causes producing a buddha's own boundlessly excellent fruit; and innate power, the wondrous, spontaneous power simply accompanying a buddha's own manifestation as a direct result of immeasurable merit accumulated across countless past eons — illustrated by the newborn Buddha's own seven steps and declaration of supremacy, and by trees spontaneously providing shade wherever the Buddha later walked. A bodhisattva's genuine delight in all such inconceivable power, he notes, is precisely what the aspiration of power describes. The treatise turns next to the sixth and seventh aspirations, the wish to bring beings both benefit and ease.

40. Section: The Wish to Give Wholesome Teaching, the Wish to Relieve Suffering, and Freedom From Attachment to Either — Three More Aspirations

The sixth aspiration, the aspiration of benefit, describes bodhisattvas who wish to bestow wholesome teaching upon sentient beings. The seventh, the aspiration of ease, describes bodhisattvas who wish to bestow genuine, beneficial relief upon sentient beings. And the eighth, the aspiration of liberation, describes bodhisattvas whose mind, in bringing about this very benefit and ease for beings, remains entirely free of any attached, craving mind, and whose mind, regarding whatever pleasant future karmic result such action might produce, remains equally unbound — genuinely free, seeking nothing in return either from the beings helped or from the results of having helped them.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩，於諸有情欲以善法而授與之，是名菩薩利益意樂。

又諸菩薩，於諸有情欲以饒益而授與之，是名菩薩安樂意樂。

又諸菩薩，即於如是諸有情所無愛染心，又於當來可愛異熟其心無繫，是名菩薩解脫意樂。

《披》即於如是諸有情所無愛染心者：謂於有情欲與利益安樂，非由心有愛染故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, bodhisattvas wish to bestow wholesome teaching upon sentient beings. This is called a bodhisattva's aspiration of benefit.

Furthermore, bodhisattvas wish to bestow genuine relief upon sentient beings. This is called a bodhisattva's aspiration of ease.

Furthermore, bodhisattvas, toward these very same sentient beings, hold no attached, craving mind, and, toward whatever pleasant future karmic result might follow, their mind remains unbound. This is called a bodhisattva's aspiration of liberation.

Commentary: Regarding "toward these very same sentient beings, hold no attached, craving mind" — this means that the wish to give beings benefit and ease does not arise from any attachment or craving in the mind.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the sixth aspiration as a bodhisattva's wish to teach beings the classical formula "refrain from every evil, undertake every good, purify one's own mind — this is what every buddha teaches," guiding beings out of unwholesome ground and into wholesome ground through the refuges, the five precepts, and the eight precepts, gradually leading them toward the noble path itself. He explains the seventh as a bodhisattva's wish to relieve beings of poverty, want, and the helplessness of having no one to depend on, providing whatever material support actually eases their present suffering.

He explains the eighth aspiration as the crucial qualification binding the sixth and seventh together properly: a bodhisattva who gives beings both wholesome teaching and material ease does so without any attachment clinging to the beings themselves, and without regard for whether those beings ever cause the bodhisattva harm or hardship in return, and equally without any mind fixed on some pleasant karmic reward the giving itself might eventually produce — genuine liberation from both objects of attachment, the beings helped and the fruit of helping them. Without this eighth aspiration, he notes, even the sixth and seventh, however outwardly generous, could still remain subtly tainted by self-interest; only together do all three describe a bodhisattva's genuinely free and complete generosity. The treatise turns next to the ninth and tenth aspirations, firmness of resolve and freedom from distorted wisdom.

41. Section: Firm Resolve Toward Awakening, and Undistorted Wisdom Regarding the Actual Means of Helping Beings and Attaining It

The ninth aspiration, the firm aspiration, describes bodhisattvas whose mind, toward unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening, remains single-mindedly focused, never once wavering or changing. The tenth, the undistorted aspiration, describes bodhisattvas who hold undistorted wisdom joined with resolute understanding regarding both the means of benefiting sentient beings and the means of approaching and realizing great awakening — knowing not merely that beings should be helped and awakening sought, but genuinely, correctly how.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩，於其無上正等菩提，其心專注，曾無變易，是名菩薩堅固意樂。

又諸菩薩，於諸有情饒益方便，於大菩提趣證方便，無顛倒智俱行勝解，是名菩薩無虛妄意樂。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, bodhisattvas, toward unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening, hold a mind single-mindedly focused, never once wavering. This is called a bodhisattva's firm aspiration.

Furthermore, bodhisattvas hold undistorted wisdom joined with resolute understanding regarding the means of benefiting sentient beings, and regarding the means of approaching and realizing great awakening. This is called a bodhisattva's undistorted aspiration.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats the ninth aspiration as the settled counterpart to the eighth ground's own quality of certainty already discussed at length in the previous fascicle — a mind so thoroughly fixed on unsurpassed awakening that no circumstance, however difficult, could ever dislodge it, distinguishing genuine, mature resolve from the more fragile, easily-discouraged intention of a beginning practitioner. He explains the tenth aspiration as ensuring that a bodhisattva's compassionate intention toward beings and aspiration toward awakening are never merely well-meaning but actually effective — undistorted wisdom knowing precisely which methods genuinely benefit a given being and which practices genuinely lead toward awakening, since good intentions without correct method can still fail to accomplish what they set out to do. The treatise turns next to a cluster of four aspirations tracking degrees of purity across the later stages of the path.

42. Section: Impure, Pure, and Utterly Pure Aspiration — Tracking the Gradual Severing of the Two Obscurations Across the Grounds

The eleventh aspiration, the impure aspiration, names every excellent aspiration belonging to the stage of resolute practice — since bodhisattvas at this ordinary stage, prior to the noble grounds, have not yet severed the seeds of either the afflictive or the cognitive obscuration, whatever excellent aspiration such a bodhisattva holds remains, in this specific sense, impure. The twelfth, the pure aspiration, names every excellent aspiration belonging to bodhisattvas from the ground of pure, excellent aspiration through the ground of certain conduct — the first ground through the ninth — who have already severed some portion of the seeds of both obscurations, making their aspiration genuinely pure by comparison. And the thirteenth, the utterly pure aspiration, names every excellent aspiration belonging to bodhisattvas who have reached the ultimate ground, the tenth, where both obscurations' seeds have been severed almost entirely, leaving only the slightest residual trace of habitual tendency — an aspiration purer still.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩，勝解行地所有一切增上意樂，是名菩薩不清淨意樂。

又諸菩薩，從淨勝意樂地乃至決定行地所有一切增上意樂，是名菩薩清淨意樂。

又諸菩薩，到究竟地所有一切增上意樂，是名菩薩善清淨意樂。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, every excellent aspiration belonging to bodhisattvas at the stage of resolute practice is called a bodhisattva's impure aspiration.

Furthermore, every excellent aspiration belonging to bodhisattvas from the ground of pure, excellent aspiration through the ground of certain conduct is called a bodhisattva's pure aspiration.

Furthermore, every excellent aspiration belonging to bodhisattvas who have reached the ultimate ground is called a bodhisattva's utterly pure aspiration.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi maps these three aspirations directly onto the same threefold division of the path already familiar from many earlier discussions: the stage of resolute practice, comprising the provisioning and applied-effort stages prior to the grounds, where an ordinary bodhisattva's aspiration, however genuine, remains impure since neither obscuration's seeds have yet been touched; the first through ninth grounds, where a noble bodhisattva has already severed a substantial portion of both obscurations' seeds, making the resulting aspiration genuinely pure; and the tenth and final ground, where both obscurations have been severed almost to completion, with only the faintest habitual residue remaining, making the resulting aspiration utterly pure, the highest grade this progression describes short of buddhahood itself.

He notes that this threefold scheme gives concrete, technical precision to what "purity" actually means in the context of a bodhisattva's aspiration — not a vague spiritual quality but a specific function of how much of the afflictive and cognitive obscurations' underlying seeds have actually been eliminated at any given stage, a measure that increases steadily and irreversibly as a bodhisattva advances from resolute practice through the tenth ground. The treatise turns next to the fourteenth and fifteenth aspirations, closing the entire fifteenfold scheme.

43. Section: The Aspiration Still to Be Tamed, and the Innate Aspiration That Has Become Second Nature — Closing All Fifteen Kinds and Opening the Ten Resulting Accomplishments

The fourteenth aspiration, the aspiration for what is to be tamed, is simply the impure aspiration named again from a different angle: since a bodhisattva at this ordinary stage still holds an aspiration not yet purified, that very aspiration is precisely what still needs to be examined, discerned, and tamed through continued practice. The fifteenth and final aspiration, the innate aspiration, names both the pure aspiration and the utterly pure aspiration together, from the first ground through the tenth — called innate because these aspirations have become the bodhisattva's own settled nature, no longer requiring deliberate reflection to arise, and because they have become genuinely, firmly established within the very basis, consciousness itself, that supports them. With this fifteenth aspiration, all fifteen kinds close in full, and the treatise turns to its third and final topic: these fifteen wondrous, excellent kinds of excellent aspiration, spanning every stage of the path from resolute practice through the ultimate ground, together accomplish, in brief, ten undertakings.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩不清淨意樂，是則名為應調伏意樂，由此意樂應思擇故。

又諸菩薩清淨意樂、善清淨意樂，是則名為俱生意樂，由此意樂性成就故，於所依中善安立故。

《披》由此意樂性成就故等者：不由思擇，習所成性，名性成就。云何習所成性？謂由數習諸善法故，於自所依異熟識中，積集諸善，堅住、不忘、成滿究竟，即由此義，名善安立。

如是菩薩十五妙善增上意樂，隨一切地，以要言之，能作十事。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, a bodhisattva's impure aspiration is thereby called the aspiration for what is to be tamed, because this aspiration is precisely what must be discerned and examined.

Furthermore, a bodhisattva's pure aspiration and utterly pure aspiration are thereby called the innate aspiration, because this aspiration has become one's own nature, and because it is genuinely well-established within its own basis.

Commentary: Regarding "because this aspiration has become one's own nature" and the like — arising not through deliberate reflection but as a habituated nature is called becoming one's own nature. What is habituated nature? It means that, through repeated habituation in wholesome teachings, wholesome qualities accumulate within one's own supporting, ripening consciousness, remaining firm, unforgotten, and brought to their own ultimate completion — it is precisely by this meaning that it is called genuinely well-established.

These fifteen wondrous, excellent kinds of excellent aspiration a bodhisattva holds, spanning every stage, in brief, accomplish ten undertakings.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the fourteenth aspiration as simply a renaming of the eleventh, impure aspiration, viewed now from the standpoint of the work still required: an ordinary bodhisattva's aspiration, not yet purified of either obscuration's seeds, is precisely the raw material that continued discernment and practice must gradually tame and refine — the same reality described twice, once in terms of its present impurity and once in terms of the task it still poses.

He unpacks the fifteenth aspiration's rich technical gloss at some length: an aspiration becomes "innate" specifically once it no longer requires deliberate, effortful reflection to arise, having instead become a habituated disposition through repeated practice — the same principle already introduced under undistorted dedication several milestones earlier, where sustained habituation across the past, future, and present eventually produces a wholesome quality so natural it appears spontaneous, as though present from birth. He explains "well-established within its own basis" as describing how such habituated wholesome qualities accumulate specifically within the ripening consciousness that carries karmic seeds forward from life to life, becoming firmly rooted there, never forgotten, and eventually reaching their own complete fulfillment — precisely why bodhisattvas from the first ground through the tenth, whose pure and utterly pure aspirations have matured into this settled, effortless disposition, are said to hold aspiration that is genuinely innate. With all fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration now closed, the second of chapter three's three topics reaches its own complete conclusion; the treatise turns next to its third and final topic, the ten concrete undertakings these fifteen kinds of aspiration together accomplish across every stage of the path.

44. Section: Ten Accomplishments Open — Supreme Offering to the Three Jewels, and Precept-Keeping Even When Life Itself Is Threatened

The treatise turns to the third of chapter three's three topics: what are the ten undertakings these fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration accomplish? Through the most supreme aspiration, bodhisattvas are able to offer every kind of most excellent offering to the three jewels, this offering itself standing foremost among all the provisions gathered toward awakening. Through the restraining aspiration, bodhisattvas, even when facing a threat to life itself, never deliberately violate the pure precept-discipline they have received, and should any violation occur regardless, swiftly and thoroughly confess and remove it — exactly as the Nirvana Sutra describes the two kinds of genuinely capable practitioner: one who never commits wrongdoing at all, and one who, having committed it, is able to confess.

Chinese Source

何等為十？謂諸菩薩最上意樂，能於三寶修一切種最勝供養，普於一切菩提資糧為最第一。

又諸菩薩遮止意樂，能於所受淨戒律儀，命難因緣，亦不故思犯於所犯；設有所犯，疾疾悔除。

Ru-Yi Translation

What are these ten? Through a bodhisattva's most supreme aspiration, one is able to make every kind of most excellent offering to the three jewels, foremost among all provisions gathered toward awakening.

Furthermore, through a bodhisattva's restraining aspiration, one is able, even under threat to one's own life, never to deliberately violate the pure precept-discipline received; should any violation occur regardless, one swiftly and thoroughly confesses and removes it.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains that this third topic maps each of ten concrete accomplishments back to the specific excellent aspiration or aspirations that produce it, working through all fifteen kinds condensed into twelve groupings across ten items (since a few closely related aspirations are treated together). The first accomplishment follows directly from the most supreme aspiration's own reverence for the three jewels: a bodhisattva sustained by this aspiration naturally makes the most excellent offerings, material and doctrinal alike, and precisely because devotion to the three jewels is itself so fundamental, this offering ranks foremost among every provision a bodhisattva gathers toward awakening.

He illustrates the second accomplishment, flowing from the restraining aspiration's own delight in the precepts, with the Nirvana Sutra's own memorable image of the two kinds of truly capable practitioner: the first simply never commits wrongdoing at all, while the second, having committed it, is genuinely able to confess and restore purity — a bodhisattva sustained by restraining aspiration holds to the precepts so firmly that not even mortal danger could induce a deliberate violation, and should some involuntary lapse occur regardless, that same aspiration ensures swift, thorough confession rather than concealment or resignation. The treatise turns next to the third and fourth accomplishments, flowing from the aspiration of the perfections and the aspiration of genuine reality.

45. Section: Tireless Practice of the Perfections, and Remaining in Birth-and-Death Without Taint, Never Rushing Into Premature Nirvana

Through the aspiration of the perfections, bodhisattvas are able to diligently cultivate wholesome teachings continuously, abiding always free of heedlessness, dwelling constantly in the most excellent freedom from heedlessness. Through the aspiration of genuine reality, bodhisattvas are able, for the sake of sentient beings, to circulate through birth-and-death with an untainted mind, holding the excellent aspiration never to enter nirvana prematurely — since a bodhisattva who has genuinely realized the suchness of personal and phenomenal emptiness understands birth-and-death as it truly is, and can therefore remain within it without being stained by it, delighting in nirvana far more than any disciple yet declining to hasten toward it, precisely so as to continue maturing both self and beings within the very cycle a disciple would rush to escape.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩波羅蜜多意樂，能於善法常勤修習無放逸住，常住最勝無放逸住。

又諸菩薩真實義意樂，能為有情以無染心流轉生死，不入涅槃增上意樂。

《披》以無染心流轉生死等者：由諸菩薩於其生死如實了知，故無染心流轉生死。由於涅槃不多願樂，故不速疾入般涅槃。義如真實義品中說。（陵本三十六卷十一頁2945）

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, through a bodhisattva's aspiration of the perfections, one is able to diligently cultivate wholesome teachings continuously, dwelling free of heedlessness, abiding constantly in the most excellent freedom from heedlessness.

Furthermore, through a bodhisattva's aspiration of genuine reality, one is able, for the sake of sentient beings, to circulate through birth-and-death with an untainted mind, holding the excellent aspiration never to enter nirvana prematurely.

Commentary: Regarding "circulate through birth-and-death with an untainted mind" and the like — because bodhisattvas genuinely, correctly know birth-and-death as it truly is, they circulate through it with an untainted mind. Because they do not hold excessive longing for nirvana, they do not hasten to enter final nirvana. This meaning is as explained in the Chapter on Genuine Reality.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the third accomplishment as the natural fruit of genuine delight in cultivating the perfections: a bodhisattva who actually enjoys practicing giving through wisdom sustains that practice continuously, free of the distorted thinking, distorted deliberation, and distorted idle talk that constitute heedlessness, dwelling in what the treatise calls the most excellent freedom from heedlessness precisely because the underlying motivation is joy rather than mere obligation.

He unpacks the fourth accomplishment at greater length, since it addresses one of the great vehicle's own most distinctive teachings: a bodhisattva who has genuinely realized personal and phenomenal emptiness understands that birth-and-death itself is, at bottom, without genuine arising and without genuine ceasing, and can therefore remain within it, teaching and maturing beings, without being tainted by its usual afflictions — unlike an ordinary being, who fears birth-and-death out of ignorance, and unlike a disciple, who longs so intensely for nirvana that escaping birth-and-death becomes the overriding priority, a bodhisattva holds a correct, balanced understanding of nirvana that neither fears samsara nor rushes toward liberation, since premature entry into nirvana would foreclose the very opportunity to mature one's own bodhi and benefit beings that remaining in birth-and-death, precisely because it is not clung to as inherently threatening, actually makes possible. The treatise turns next to the fifth accomplishment, flowing from the aspiration of power.

46. Section: Tasting the Pure, Wondrous Flavor of the Teaching Through the Aspiration of Power — Never Satisfied With Mere Hearing and Reflecting Alone

Through the aspiration of power, bodhisattvas are able to genuinely taste, within the noble teaching, its own pure, wondrous flavor, and are further able to give rise to firm resolve regarding actual cultivation, delighting in sustained dwelling within it rather than remaining content with mere hearing and reflecting alone. The treatise's own commentary specifies this genuine tasting of the teaching's flavor through four correct dharmas that sustain the true teaching: seclusion, cultivation, the fruit of cultivation, and freedom from any contention within the noble teaching — a bodhisattva who cultivates calm-abiding and insight to genuinely, undistortedly penetrate the teaching's own meaning, free of any contradiction with it, is precisely one who tastes this pure, wondrous flavor. And such a bodhisattva, having achieved mastery over meditative absorption, capable of correctly countering every contaminated, defiling phenomenon and never again overpowered by it, thereby gives rise to firm resolve specifically regarding cultivation itself, delighting in sustained dwelling within it.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩威力意樂，能於聖教覺受淳淨上妙法味。

《披》能於聖教覺受淳淨上妙法味者：三摩呬多地說：有四正法，攝持正教。何等為四？一者、遠離，二者、修習，三者、修果，四者、於聖教中無有乖諍。乃至廣說。（陵本十三卷十一頁1112）此中聖教，應準彼釋。由修奢摩他、毗鉢舍那，如實通達聖教法義，無有乖諍，名能覺受淳淨上妙法味。

復能於修起堅固想，欣樂多住，不唯聞思便生喜足。

《披》於修起堅固想欣樂多住者：謂作是思：修習定心，得定自在，能正對治一切漏有漏法，不為漏有漏法之所勝伏。是名於修起堅固想，由是於修欣樂多住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, through a bodhisattva's aspiration of power, one is able to genuinely taste, within the noble teaching, its own pure, wondrous flavor.

Commentary: Regarding "genuinely taste, within the noble teaching, its own pure, wondrous flavor" — the Stage of Meditative Equipoise explains: there are four correct dharmas that sustain the true teaching. What are these four? First, seclusion; second, cultivation; third, the fruit of cultivation; fourth, freedom from any contention within the noble teaching. And so on, as explained at length. The "noble teaching" mentioned here should be understood according to that explanation. Through cultivating calm-abiding and insight, genuinely penetrating the noble teaching's own meaning, free of any contradiction with it, is what is called genuinely tasting this pure, wondrous flavor.

One is further able to give rise to firm resolve regarding actual cultivation, delighting in sustained dwelling within it, not remaining satisfied with mere hearing and reflecting alone.

Commentary: Regarding "give rise to firm resolve regarding actual cultivation, delighting in sustained dwelling" — this means reflecting as follows: cultivating a concentrated mind, attaining mastery over concentration, one is able to correctly counter every contaminated, defiling phenomenon, never again overpowered by such contaminated, defiling phenomena. This is called giving rise to firm resolve regarding cultivation, and it is precisely because of this that one delights in sustained dwelling within cultivation.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi unpacks the commentary's own citation of the four correct dharmas from the Stage of Meditative Equipoise, fascicle 13: seclusion, meaning physical seclusion in a place suited to practice and mental seclusion from affliction; cultivation, meaning diligent practice of calm-abiding and insight within that seclusion to actually sever affliction; the fruit of cultivation, meaning the noble fruits such practice produces, from the first fruit's severance of the very realm of affliction through the fourth fruit's complete extinction; and freedom from contention within the noble teaching, meaning that the Buddha and all genuine disciples teach the same words, the same phrases, the same meaning, and the same flavor without contradicting one another — together, these four dharmas describe what actually sustains the true teaching's own continued vitality, and a bodhisattva who cultivates calm-abiding and insight to genuinely penetrate this same teaching without contradiction is precisely the one who tastes its pure, wondrous flavor for themselves.

He explains the fifth accomplishment's second half as addressing a specific temptation: a practitioner might mistake merely hearing and intellectually reflecting on the teaching for genuine attainment, growing satisfied prematurely. A bodhisattva sustained by the aspiration of power instead reflects that only cultivating an actually concentrated mind, gaining genuine mastery over meditative absorption free of craving, wrong view, arrogance, ignorance, and doubt regarding that absorption itself, and thereby correctly countering every contaminated phenomenon so it can never again overpower the practitioner, constitutes real progress — this firm resolve regarding cultivation itself, rather than resting content with hearing and reflecting, is what "delighting in sustained dwelling within cultivation" describes. The treatise turns next to the sixth, seventh, and eighth accomplishments.

47. Section: Tireless Benefiting of Beings, Blazing Diligence, and Never Settling for Partial Attainment

Through the aspiration of benefit, the aspiration of ease, and the aspiration of liberation together, bodhisattvas are able to diligently cultivate every undertaking that genuinely benefits sentient beings, and though constantly engaged in such cultivation, never grow weary of it. Through the firm aspiration, bodhisattvas are able to give rise to every kind of blazing, vast diligence, sustaining effort that neither slackens nor breaks off. And through the undistorted aspiration, bodhisattvas are able to swiftly realize supernormal knowledge and ultimate wisdom with respect to whatever wholesome teachings they have taken up, never growing satisfied with some small, inferior, or weak degree of attainment along the way.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩利益意樂、安樂意樂、解脫意樂，能於一切饒益有情所作事業精勤修習，雖常修習而無厭倦。

又諸菩薩堅固意樂，能於種種熾然精進、廣大精進，發起安住無緩加行、無斷加行。

又諸菩薩無虛妄意樂，能於所引彼彼善法速證通慧，不於少分下劣薄弱差別證中而生喜足。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, through a bodhisattva's aspiration of benefit, aspiration of ease, and aspiration of liberation together, one is able to diligently cultivate every undertaking that benefits sentient beings, and though constantly cultivating it, never grow weary.

Furthermore, through a bodhisattva's firm aspiration, one is able to give rise to every kind of blazing, vast diligence, sustaining effort that neither slackens nor breaks off.

Furthermore, through a bodhisattva's undistorted aspiration, one is able to swiftly realize supernormal knowledge and ultimate wisdom with respect to whatever wholesome teachings have been taken up, never growing satisfied with some small, inferior, or weak degree of attainment.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the sixth accomplishment as drawing together three closely related aspirations already examined earlier — the wish to give beings wholesome teaching, the wish to give material ease, and freedom from attachment to either the beings helped or the results of helping — into a single, sustained practical outcome: precisely because this threefold aspiration is untainted by self-interest, a bodhisattva can engage in benefiting beings continuously, through giving, loving speech, beneficial conduct, and cooperation, without the fatigue that would eventually set in were the underlying motivation less pure.

He explains the seventh accomplishment as the direct fruit of firm, unwavering resolve toward awakening: a bodhisattva so resolved naturally gives rise to blazing, vigorous, and vast diligence, sustaining effort that is neither sluggish nor subject to interruption — the word "blazing" itself conveying the sheer intensity such diligence carries once genuinely established. He explains the eighth accomplishment as the practical safeguard undistorted wisdom provides against premature satisfaction: a bodhisattva possessing correct, undistorted understanding of what genuine attainment actually requires can accurately gauge their own progress, recognizing that some small, partial success does not yet constitute the fuller realization still to come, and is thereby spurred to continue advancing rather than resting on a lesser achievement — always pressing further, as the traditional expression has it, even from the very top of a hundred-foot pole. The treatise turns next to the ninth and tenth accomplishments, closing the entire fifteenfold-to-tenfold structure.

48. Section: The Aspiration Still to Be Tamed Draws Forth the Innate Aspiration, Which in Turn Swiftly Completes Awakening Itself — Closing the Ten Accomplishments

Through the aspiration for what is to be tamed, bodhisattvas are able to draw forth the innate aspiration — an ordinary bodhisattva who recognizes that the mind is not yet fully purified diligently cultivates calm-abiding and insight, applying the power of discernment to subdue affliction, and through sustained habituation gradually internalizes this practice until it becomes a spontaneous, settled disposition, drawing forth in turn the noble ones' own innate, pure and utterly pure aspiration — since everything unfolds by its own proper sequence, and it is precisely by recognizing one's own present insufficiency and diligently applying the aspiration still to be tamed that a bodhisattva eventually draws forth that innate aspiration. And through the innate aspiration itself, bodhisattvas are able to swiftly approach and realize unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening, and, once awakened, to bring genuine benefit, ease, and welfare to both gods and humans alike. With this tenth accomplishment, the treatise concludes: these are the ten undertakings a bodhisattva accomplishes through the fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩應調伏意樂，能引俱生意樂。

又諸菩薩俱生意樂，能於無上正等菩提速疾趣證，能與天人作諸義利利益安樂。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, through a bodhisattva's aspiration for what is to be tamed, one is able to draw forth the innate aspiration.

Furthermore, through a bodhisattva's innate aspiration, one is able to swiftly approach and realize unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening, and to bring gods and humans genuine welfare, benefit, and ease.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the ninth accomplishment as tracing the actual mechanism by which an ordinary bodhisattva's still-impure aspiration eventually matures into the settled, effortless innate aspiration already discussed at length: recognizing honestly that the mind remains impure, still tainted by the seeds of both obscurations, a bodhisattva applies discerning wisdom within calm-abiding and insight to actively subdue affliction, and through sustained, repeated habituation, this deliberate effort gradually transforms into a spontaneous, settled disposition — the very same process of habituation that produces "innate nature" wherever it appears in this chapter. He stresses that this progression follows a genuine, necessary sequence: a bodhisattva cannot skip directly to effortless, innate purity without first honestly recognizing and diligently working on present impurity, exactly as the aspiration for what is to be tamed names the starting point and the innate aspiration names its own eventual fruit.

He explains the tenth and final accomplishment as the ultimate payoff of this entire sequence: once the innate aspiration has been fully established, a bodhisattva's remaining progress toward buddhahood proceeds swiftly, since the underlying disposition sustaining that progress no longer requires deliberate effort to maintain, and the resulting buddhahood then extends its benefit to gods and humans across the three vehicles' own respective paths to liberation. With all ten accomplishments now traced back to their producing aspirations, he notes, the fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration reveal themselves not as fifteen disconnected virtues but as an integrated system, each aspiration contributing its own specific, concrete accomplishment toward the single overarching goal of complete awakening. The treatise turns next to explaining why three of the fifteen kinds were left out of this tenfold accounting.

49. Section: Three Aspirations Left Unrepeated, All Fifteen Comprehensively Cover Everything the Buddha Teaches — Chapter Three Closes in Full

The treatise explains why three of the fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration were left out of the tenfold accounting just completed: the aspiration for what is to be tamed is itself simply the impure aspiration, and the innate aspiration is itself simply the pure and utterly pure aspirations together — since these three were already fully covered under the ninth and tenth accomplishments, they are not separately repeated here. The treatise then closes the entire topic of excellent aspiration with a sweeping confirmation: whatever the world-honored one has taught, proclaimed, established, or disclosed regarding a bodhisattva's excellent aspiration, in whatever place and through whatever occasion, one should understand that all of it is comprised within these same fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration — and therefore every bodhisattva of the past, future, and present who holds this same wondrous, excellent aspiration and has already realized, will realize, or is presently realizing unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening does so entirely through these fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration just explained. Beyond these, there is nothing to be added and nothing lacking. Since these fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration bring a bodhisattva the very greatest benefit and fruit of awakening, the treatise concludes, bodhisattvas who rely on this aspiration swiftly realize unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening — closing chapter three, excellent aspiration, entirely in full.

Chinese Source

應調伏意樂即不清淨意樂，俱生意樂即清淨意樂、善清淨意樂，故不別說。

世尊所有為諸菩薩，於彼彼處種種宣說、施設、開示增上意樂，當知一切即此十五意樂所攝，是故過去、未來、現在妙善意樂諸菩薩眾，於其無上正等菩提曾當現證，一切皆由如是所說十五意樂。除此無有若過若增。

如是菩薩十五意樂，能得最大菩提果利，是故菩薩依此意樂，速證無上正等菩提。

Ru-Yi Translation

The aspiration for what is to be tamed is itself the impure aspiration; the innate aspiration is itself the pure aspiration and the utterly pure aspiration. This is why they are not separately explained again here.

Whatever the world-honored one has, in this place or that, variously taught, established, and disclosed to bodhisattvas regarding excellent aspiration, one should understand, is entirely comprised within these fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration. This is why every bodhisattva of the past, future, and present who holds this wondrous, excellent aspiration, and who has already realized, will realize, or presently realizes unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening, does so entirely through these fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration already explained. Beyond these, there is nothing to be added and nothing lacking.

Since these fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration a bodhisattva holds bring the very greatest benefit and fruit of awakening, bodhisattvas who rely on this aspiration swiftly realize unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this brief clarification as tidying up a point of internal consistency: since the tenfold accounting of accomplishments has already implicitly covered the eleventh through fifteenth aspirations through their equivalences with the ninth and tenth, the treatise simply notes this equivalence explicitly rather than repeating identical material under a different heading — the fourteenth aspiration and the eleventh being one and the same, and the fifteenth aspiration comprising the twelfth and thirteenth together.

He treats the closing, sweeping confirmation as mirroring precisely the same rhetorical move already made at the end of chapter two regarding the four dharmas — a comprehensive claim that this fifteenfold structure exhausts everything the Buddha has ever taught bodhisattvas about excellent aspiration, across every occasion and every teaching context, leaving no bodhisattva's own path to awakening explicable by any route outside it. With this, he notes, chapter three, excellent aspiration — covering the seven aspects of compassion, the fifteen kinds of excellent aspiration, and the ten accomplishments they produce — closes completely in full, a genuinely thorough account of the inner quality of aspiration sustaining a bodhisattva's practice from the very first stirrings of resolute practice all the way through the tenth and final ground. The treatise turns next to chapter four, the bodhisattva abodes, the final chapter of the second yoga foundation and, for this fascicle, the final topic to be opened.

50. Section: Chapter Four Opens — Twelve Bodhisattva Abodes Comprehensively Gathering Everything Already Explained, Plus the Thirteenth Abode of the Tathagata

Chapter four, the bodhisattva abodes, opens by explaining that everything already covered across the first three chapters of this second yoga foundation — a bodhisattva complete with the lineage, diligently training in everything already explained as what a bodhisattva should learn, correctly and equally manifesting every one of the marks already explained, diligently training in the applied efforts of the divisions already explained, and genuinely purifying and cultivating the excellent aspiration already explained — all of this together comprises, in brief, twelve bodhisattva abodes; through these twelve abodes, every bodhisattva abode and every bodhisattva practice whatsoever is comprehensively gathered in. Beyond these twelve, the treatise adds, there is a further, thirteenth abode belonging to the tathagata alone: through this abode, a bodhisattva manifests, directly before the mind, the vast, equal awakening to unsurpassed enlightenment itself — called the unsurpassed abode.

Chinese Source

本地分中菩薩地第二持隨法瑜伽處住品第四之一

如是始從種性具足，廣說乃至於如所說菩薩所學，正勤修學；於如所說菩薩諸相，正等顯現；於諸菩薩分加行中，正勤修學；於如所說菩薩意樂，能淨修治。諸菩薩眾略有菩薩十二種住，由此菩薩十二種住，普攝一切諸菩薩住，普攝一切諸菩薩行。

《披》普攝一切諸菩薩住等者：菩薩修作一切事業，有二時別。一者、行時，二者、住時。如說菩薩修不放逸，如如所應行，如如所應住，如是如是行，如是如是住，無有毀犯。如是行住，略攝菩薩一切作業。依此安立菩薩十二種住，說彼普攝一切行住。

復有如來第十三住，由此住故，現前等覺廣大菩提，名無上住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Within the Basic Section, the Bodhisattva Stage, the second yoga foundation of sustained practice, chapter four, the first part: the abodes.

This means that, beginning from being complete with the lineage, extensively explained through diligently training in what a bodhisattva should learn as already explained, correctly and equally manifesting the marks of a bodhisattva as already explained, diligently training in the applied efforts within the divisions of a bodhisattva as already explained, and being able to genuinely purify and cultivate the excellent aspiration of a bodhisattva as already explained — bodhisattvas, in brief, have twelve kinds of abode. Through these twelve kinds of abode, every bodhisattva abode is comprehensively gathered in, and every bodhisattva practice is comprehensively gathered in.

Commentary: Regarding "comprehensively gathered in, every bodhisattva abode" and the like — a bodhisattva's cultivation of every undertaking has two distinct occasions: first, the time of moving; second, the time of abiding still. As it is said, a bodhisattva cultivates freedom from heedlessness in accordance with however one should move, and in accordance with however one should abide still, moving in just this way and abiding still in just this way, without any violation. Such moving and abiding still, in brief, comprehensively gather in every undertaking of a bodhisattva. It is on this basis that the twelve kinds of bodhisattva abode are established, and it is said that they comprehensively gather in every instance of such moving and abiding still.

There is, furthermore, a thirteenth abode belonging to the tathagata. Through this abode, one manifests directly before the mind the vast, equal awakening to great awakening itself — this is called the unsurpassed abode.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi announces at the outset that chapter four, the bodhisattva abodes, is itself divided across two fascicles — this present fascicle covering only the first portion, with the remainder continuing into fascicle 48 — and explains that the second yoga foundation comprises exactly four chapters, marks, divisions, excellent aspiration, and now abodes, the last of which maps the entire bodhisattva path onto thirteen successive stages from the very beginning of the lineage through buddhahood itself. He treats the opening passage as a deliberate, sweeping act of synthesis: everything already explained across the initial eighteen-chapter foundation (fascicles 35 through 46, covering the lineage through the qualities of a bodhisattva) and across this second foundation's first three chapters (marks, divisions, and excellent aspiration) is now gathered together and mapped onto twelve successive abodes, tracing the entire path's own structure from beginning to end.

He unpacks the commentary's own explanation of why these twelve abodes comprehensively cover "every bodhisattva practice": all bodhisattva cultivation, the commentary explains, unfolds across only two kinds of occasion — moving, as when a renunciate bodhisattva goes among the villages on almsround and must apply the six perfections amid worldly encounters, and abiding still, as when a bodhisattva remains in a fixed dwelling cultivating calm-abiding and insight, the four investigations, and the four kinds of genuine wisdom — and a bodhisattva who cultivates freedom from heedlessness correctly in both occasions, maturing both self and others without ever violating the precepts, has thereby covered everything cultivation could possibly require; the twelve abodes are simply this same comprehensive cultivation organized by stage. He explains the thirteenth abode, belonging to the tathagata alone, as the tenth-ground bodhisattva's own final transition into buddhahood itself, manifesting complete, all-embracing enlightenment directly before the mind — rightly called the unsurpassed abode, since nothing beyond it remains to be attained. The treatise turns next to naming all twelve bodhisattva abodes, plus this thirteenth abode of the tathagata, in full.

51. Section: All Thirteen Abodes Named in a Single Summary Verse, From the Lineage Through Final Buddhahood

The treatise asks: what are these twelve bodhisattva abodes, and the thirteenth besides? A summary verse names all thirteen at once: the abode of the lineage, the abode of resolute practice, the abode of utmost joy, the abode of excellent moral discipline, the abode of excellent mind, then three abodes of excellent wisdom — associated with the factors of awakening, associated with the truths, and associated with the stilling of dependent origination's own flow — the abode with marks and effortful application, the abode without marks and without effortful application, and further the abode of unobstructed understanding, then the abode of the most excellent bodhisattva, and, at the very last, the abode of the tathagata in its own most ultimate form.

Chinese Source

云何菩薩十二住等？嗢柁南曰：種性勝解行　極喜增上戒　增上心三慧　無相有功用　無相無功用　及以無礙解　最上菩薩住　最極如來住

Ru-Yi Translation

What are these twelve bodhisattva abodes, and so forth? A summary verse states: "The lineage, resolute practice; utmost joy, excellent discipline; excellent mind, three [abodes of] wisdom; with marks and effortful application; without marks, without effortful application; and also unobstructed understanding; the most excellent bodhisattva abode; the most ultimate tathagata abode."

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi lays out the full correspondence between this verse's thirteen names and the ground structure already familiar from earlier fascicles: the abode of the lineage corresponds to a bodhisattva possessing only the seed-potential of the path, prior even to the ten stages of faith; the abode of resolute practice corresponds to the stage of resolute practice, spanning the provisioning and applied-effort stages; the abode of utmost joy corresponds to the first ground; the abode of excellent moral discipline to the second ground; the abode of excellent mind to the third ground; and the three abodes of excellent wisdom to the fourth, fifth, and sixth grounds respectively — excellent wisdom associated with the factors of awakening at the fourth ground (also called the ground of blazing wisdom), excellent wisdom associated with the four noble truths at the fifth ground (also called the ground hard to conquer), and excellent wisdom associated with stilling the flow of dependent origination at the sixth ground (also called the ground of manifestation).

He continues the mapping through the final stages: the abode with marks and effortful application corresponds to the seventh ground, where mark-free cultivation is possible but still requires deliberate application; the abode without marks and without effortful application corresponds to the eighth ground, where such cultivation has become entirely spontaneous; the abode of unobstructed understanding corresponds to the ninth ground, where the four kinds of unobstructed understanding are fully accomplished; the most excellent bodhisattva abode corresponds to the tenth and final ground, the very summit of bodhisattva practice prior to buddhahood; and the most ultimate tathagata abode, the thirteenth, corresponds to buddhahood itself, already introduced as the unsurpassed abode. He notes that this thirteen-stage scheme, while mapping cleanly onto the traditional ten grounds plus lineage, resolute practice, and buddhahood, offers its own distinctive angle on the path by naming each stage according to its own defining psychological and practical character rather than by ground-number alone — a scheme the treatise will now unpack stage by stage, beginning with the very first, the abode of the lineage, in its own full detail.

52. Section: All Twelve Bodhisattva Abodes Named in Full, Each Briefly Defined and Mapped to Its Corresponding Ground

The treatise now names all twelve bodhisattva abodes in the prose explanation, giving each a brief defining description: the abode of the lineage; the abode of resolute practice; the abode of utmost joy; the abode of excellent moral discipline; the abode of excellent mind; and three abodes of excellent wisdom — the abode of excellent wisdom associated with the factors of awakening, the abode of excellent wisdom associated with the truths, and the abode of excellent wisdom associated with the stilling of dependent origination's own flow, since bodhisattvas at these three stages genuinely, correctly know both the wisdom that observes and the reality observed, and know that beings, lacking such wisdom, continue circulating through suffering, while those who attain it bring that suffering to rest. The list continues with the abode with marks and effortful, uninterrupted application; the abode without marks and without effortful, uninterrupted application; the abode of unobstructed understanding; and the most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode. These, the treatise concludes, are the bodhisattva's twelve kinds of abode, and through these twelve, every bodhisattva abode and every bodhisattva practice whatsoever is comprehensively gathered in.

Chinese Source

謂菩薩種性住、勝解行住、極歡喜住、增上戒住、增上心住。增上慧住復有三種。一、覺分相應增上慧住，二、諸諦相應增上慧住，三、緣起流轉止息相應增上慧住。謂諸菩薩如實了知能觀真實、所觀真實，及於真實諸有情類，由無智故眾苦流轉，由有智故眾苦止息。如是菩薩，由於三門以慧觀察，故有三種增上慧住。及有加行有功用無間闕道運轉無相住、無加行無功用無間闕道運轉無相住、無礙解住、最上成滿菩薩住。是名菩薩十二種住。

如是菩薩十二種住，普攝一切諸菩薩住，普攝一切諸菩薩行。

Ru-Yi Translation

This means: the abode of the lineage; the abode of resolute practice; the abode of utmost joy; the abode of excellent moral discipline; the abode of excellent mind. The abode of excellent wisdom further comprises three: first, the abode of excellent wisdom associated with the factors of awakening; second, the abode of excellent wisdom associated with the truths; third, the abode of excellent wisdom associated with the stilling of the flow of dependent origination. This means that bodhisattvas genuinely, correctly know the wisdom that observes reality and the reality observed, and know that, among the classes of sentient beings involved with that same reality, it is through lack of wisdom that manifold suffering continues to circulate, and through possessing wisdom that manifold suffering comes to rest. Because bodhisattvas observe through these three approaches by means of wisdom, there are three abodes of excellent wisdom. There is furthermore the abode with marks and effortful, uninterrupted application in motion; the abode without marks and without effortful, uninterrupted application in motion; the abode of unobstructed understanding; and the most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode. These are called the bodhisattva's twelve kinds of abode.

These twelve kinds of bodhisattva abode, in this way, comprehensively gather in every bodhisattva abode, and comprehensively gather in every bodhisattva practice.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through each abode's own brief defining formula and its corresponding ground, drawing on the treatise's own fuller gloss: the abode of the lineage names a bodhisattva whose path-seeds are already accomplished and never regress, continuously advancing; the abode of resolute practice names every bodhisattva practice from the very first arousing of the aspiration for awakening up until pure aspiration is attained, corresponding to the provisioning and applied-effort stages; the abode of utmost joy names the first ground, where a bodhisattva's deep, inward faith in the three jewels' own qualities no longer depends on any further external condition, having attained the four kinds of unshakeable faith; the abode of excellent moral discipline names the second ground, where a bodhisattva's own nature has become genuinely disciplined, entirely free of stinginess or any precept-violation, that very completeness of discipline dedicated toward awakening; and the abode of excellent mind names the third ground, where a bodhisattva has become free of desire-realm craving and attained meditative absorption and its accompanying mastery.

He explains the three abodes of excellent wisdom as together tracking a bodhisattva's own deepening wisdom regarding dependent origination: the fourth ground, associated with the factors of awakening, names a bodhisattva who relies on calm-abiding to cultivate insight, genuinely awakening to the true nature of phenomena and attaining the wisdom of the selflessness of phenomena from the very perspective of the observing, non-discriminating wisdom itself; the fifth ground, associated with the truths, names a bodhisattva whose wisdom regarding the four noble truths harmonizes ultimate and conventional truth without obstruction, described here from the perspective of the ultimate truth actually observed; and the sixth ground, associated with stilling the flow of dependent origination, names a bodhisattva who has attained non-discriminating wisdom and the suchness of personal and phenomenal emptiness, no longer circulating through birth-and-death, suffering itself brought to rest, described from the perspective of the accomplished result itself. He notes that bodhisattvas across all three of these grounds correctly understand both the observing wisdom and the observed reality, and understand precisely why beings without such wisdom continue to suffer while those who attain it find that suffering stilled — three complementary angles on the very same deepening realization, which is why the treatise groups them together as three kinds of a single, shared "abode of excellent wisdom."

He completes the mapping through the final grounds: the abode with marks and effortful, uninterrupted application names the seventh ground, where mark-free abiding in personal and phenomenal emptiness continues without interruption yet still requires deliberate effort to sustain; the abode without marks and without effortful, uninterrupted application names the eighth ground, where this same mark-free abiding has become entirely spontaneous; the abode of unobstructed understanding names the ninth ground, where the four kinds of unobstructed understanding are fully accomplished, enabling unobstructed teaching of beings; and the most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode names the tenth and final ground, the very summit of bodhisattva attainment short of buddhahood itself. He notes in passing that a fuller correspondence table mapping these twelve abodes against the Avatamsaka Sutra's own ten grounds exists for readers wishing to study the parallel in greater depth. With this complete naming and mapping, the treatise concludes that these twelve abodes, taken together, comprehensively gather in absolutely everything a bodhisattva's own abode and practice could possibly comprise — the treatise turns next to explaining the tathagata's own thirteenth abode before returning to unpack each of the twelve bodhisattva abodes in its own full detail, beginning with the very first, the abode of the lineage.

53. Section: The Tathagata's Own Abode Deferred to a Later Chapter — the Abode of the Lineage Opens With Its First Mark, an Inborn Kindness Never Taught
