The treatise briefly addresses the thirteenth abode, belonging to the tathagata: this abode surpasses every bodhisattva abode entirely, manifesting directly before the mind the great awakening itself — its fuller explanation, the treatise notes, will be given in full within the later chapter on establishment, in the third yoga foundation of ultimate practice. Having thus flagged the tathagata's own abode for later treatment, the treatise turns to explaining each of the twelve bodhisattva abodes in full detail, opening with the very first: what is the abode of the lineage, and how does a bodhisattva actually abide within it? Such a bodhisattva, the treatise answers, is by inborn nature kind and virtuous, spontaneously accomplished in the qualities proper to a bodhisattva even before any of the many wholesome practices a bodhisattva should undertake have actually been displayed in visible conduct — this natural kindness itself compelling the bodhisattva toward wholesome action, not through any deliberate reflection or conscious restraint, but simply through the pressure of an already-wholesome disposition finding its own natural expression.

Chinese Source

如來住者，謂過一切諸菩薩住，現前等覺大菩提住。此中最後如來住者，於後究竟瑜伽處最後建立品，當具演說。

菩薩所有十二種住，如所安立，我今當說。

云何菩薩種性住？云何菩薩住種性住？

謂諸菩薩住種性住，性自仁賢，性自成就菩薩功德；菩薩所應眾多善法，於彼現行亦有顯現。由性仁賢逼遣方便，令於善轉，非由思擇有所制約、有所防護。

《披》性自仁賢等者：謂諸菩薩本性成就，其心溫潤、其心純淨，恚心、害心不久相續，隨生隨捨，起賢善心，是名性自仁賢。種性為依，有所堪任、有大勢力，能斷煩惱、所知二障，證得無上正等菩提，是故性自成就菩薩功德。有六波羅蜜多種性相，由此相故，令他了知真是菩薩，是名菩薩所應眾多善法，於彼現行亦有顯現。

Ru-Yi Translation

The tathagata's own abode surpasses every bodhisattva abode, manifesting directly before the mind the great awakening itself. This final, tathagata abode will be fully explained within the later chapter on establishment, in the yoga foundation of ultimate practice.

I shall now explain the bodhisattva's twelve kinds of abode, exactly as they have been established.

What is the bodhisattva's abode of the lineage? How does a bodhisattva abide within the abode of the lineage?

This means that bodhisattvas abiding in the abode of the lineage are, by inborn nature, kind and virtuous, spontaneously accomplished in the qualities proper to a bodhisattva; the many wholesome teachings proper to a bodhisattva are also displayed in that bodhisattva's own visible conduct. Through this inborn kindness and virtue, such a bodhisattva is compelled toward wholesome action by its own natural means, not through deliberate reflection producing any restraint or guarding.

Commentary: Regarding "by inborn nature, kind and virtuous" and the like — this means that bodhisattvas, through their own already-accomplished inborn nature, hold a mind that is warm and gentle, a mind that is genuinely pure; anger and the wish to harm do not persist for long, arising and then being relinquished as soon as they arise, giving rise instead to a kind, virtuous mind — this is called being, by inborn nature, kind and virtuous. Relying on this lineage, such a bodhisattva has genuine capacity and great strength, able to sever both the afflictive and cognitive obscurations and realize unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening — this is why it is said that such a bodhisattva's inborn nature spontaneously accomplishes the qualities proper to a bodhisattva. Possessing the characteristic seed-potential of the six perfections, by virtue of which others recognize this person as a genuine bodhisattva, is what is meant by the many wholesome teachings proper to a bodhisattva being displayed in that bodhisattva's own visible conduct.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes that the treatise deliberately defers its fullest account of the tathagata's own abode to a much later chapter, in the treatise's third major division devoted to ultimate practice — a sensible choice, since buddhahood itself deserves its own dedicated, extensive treatment rather than a hurried aside within a chapter primarily concerned with the bodhisattva path leading up to it. He then turns to the commentary's own observation that the treatise poses two distinct questions about the abode of the lineage at once — how this stage is actually established as a distinct position along the path, and what characteristics genuinely define a bodhisattva who occupies it — signaling that the answer to follow will address both the abode's own place in the larger sequence and its own inner content.

He unpacks the first of the abode's three defining marks, inborn kindness and virtue, at some length: a bodhisattva at this very first stage, prior to any formal contact with the Buddhist teaching, already possesses, through causes and conditions cultivated across countless past lives, a genuinely warm, gentle, and pure disposition — anger and the wish to cause harm may occasionally arise, as they would in any being not yet free of affliction, but never persist, dissolving almost as soon as they appear and giving way naturally to kindness instead. He stresses that this natural goodness is not the product of conscious moral effort at this stage — the bodhisattva does not deliberate and decide to restrain unwholesome impulses, but simply finds wholesome conduct arising spontaneously from an already-wholesome underlying character, much like the pleasant, easy comfort of a well-brewed cup of tea rather than the effortful discipline of following a rule. He explains that this inborn goodness is precisely what gives such a bodhisattva the capacity and strength eventually to sever both obscurations and attain awakening, and that the seed-potential of the six perfections already present in this disposition is visible enough in outward conduct — natural generosity, natural patience, natural diligence — that others can already recognize such a person as a genuine bodhisattva, long before any formal teaching has been undertaken. The treatise turns next to the second of the abode of the lineage's three marks, already possessing every seed of buddhahood itself.

54. Section: Already Possessing Every Buddha-Seed, and a Nature Naturally Free of Coarse Affliction — Closing the Abode of the Lineage in Full

The second mark of the abode of the lineage is that such a bodhisattva already sustains every seed of buddhahood — within this very body and mind, and within the underlying basis that supports it, every seed of every buddha-quality is already fully present, even though the many conditions needed for these seeds to actually manifest have not yet come together. The third mark is that such a bodhisattva's nature is naturally free of coarse defilement, incapable of giving rise to the most severe entanglements of affliction — the kind of affliction that would produce one of the five acts of immediate retribution or sever the very roots of wholesomeness altogether. The treatise then refers the reader to its own earlier, fuller treatment of the various marks of abiding in the lineage already given in the chapter on the lineage, and closes: this, then, is what constitutes a bodhisattva's abode of the lineage.

Chinese Source

若諸菩薩住種性住，任持一切佛法種子，於自體中、於所依中，已具足有一切佛法一切種子。

又諸菩薩住種性住，性離麤垢，不能現起上煩惱纏。由此纏故，造無間業，或斷善根。

如種性品所說種種住種性相，於此菩薩種性住中，亦應廣說，應如實知。

是名菩薩種性住。

Ru-Yi Translation

If bodhisattvas abide in the abode of the lineage, they sustain every seed of every buddha-quality; within their own body and mind, and within the basis that supports them, every seed of every buddha-quality is already fully present.

Furthermore, bodhisattvas abiding in the abode of the lineage are, by nature, free of coarse defilement, incapable of giving rise to severe afflictive entanglement — the kind of entanglement that would produce an act of immediate retribution, or sever wholesome roots altogether.

The various marks of abiding in the lineage already explained in the chapter on the lineage should likewise be explained at length within this account of the bodhisattva's abode of the lineage; one should genuinely understand them accordingly.

This, then, is called a bodhisattva's abode of the lineage.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the second mark through the classical language of seeds: "every buddha-quality" refers to the unsurpassed awakening a buddha ultimately realizes, and "every seed" refers to the accumulated potential — the ten powers, four fearlessnesses, eighteen unshared qualities, and every other buddha-quality — that has followed this bodhisattva's own eighth consciousness across countless past lives, present but not yet activated simply because the surrounding conditions have not yet converged to bring it into actual manifestation. He notes the doctrinal subtlety this involves: an ordinary being's underlying storehouse consciousness is itself defiled, while these buddha-seeds are themselves pure, so the two do not directly correspond; rather, these pure seeds simply attach to and are carried along by that same underlying consciousness as their support, which is precisely what makes eventual buddhahood possible for a being who, at present, remains thoroughly ordinary.

He explains the third mark as the direct fruit of the first two: a bodhisattva whose disposition is inherently kind and who already carries every buddha-seed is, by that very nature, incapable of the most extreme forms of affliction — the specific severe entanglements that would produce one of the five acts of immediate retribution (killing one's father, mother, or an arhat, wounding a buddha, or causing schism in the monastic community) or sever wholesome roots so completely that no capacity for future wholesome action remains. He notes the treatise's own cross-reference back to its much earlier chapter on the lineage, fascicle 35, where these same marks of abiding in the lineage received their first, fuller treatment — material the reader has, in fact, already studied at length many fascicles ago, now simply being applied specifically to this first of the twelve abodes. With all three marks now closed, the abode of the lineage, the very first of the twelve bodhisattva abodes, is complete in full; the treatise turns next to the second abode, the abode of resolute practice.

55. Section: The Abode of Resolute Practice — Every Bodhisattva Practice From First Arousing the Aspiration Until Pure Aspiration Is Attained

The treatise turns to the second of the twelve abodes: what is a bodhisattva's abode of resolute practice? Every bodhisattva practice whatsoever, from the very first arousing of the aspiration for awakening up until pure aspiration has not yet been attained, one should understand, is called the abode of resolute practice. With this brief account, the treatise has now given, in outline, the distinctive marks of the first two abodes belonging to an ordinary bodhisattva — the abode of the lineage and the abode of resolute practice.

Chinese Source

云何菩薩勝解行住？謂諸菩薩，從初發心乃至未得清淨意樂，所有一切諸菩薩行，當知皆名勝解行住。

Ru-Yi Translation

What is a bodhisattva's abode of resolute practice? This means that every bodhisattva practice whatsoever, from the very first arousing of the aspiration for awakening up until pure aspiration has not yet been attained, one should understand, is called the abode of resolute practice.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains that this second abode spans the entire provisioning and applied-effort stages — everything a bodhisattva undertakes from the very moment bodhicitta is first aroused, however conventional and unrefined that initial arousal may still be, all the way up to but not including the attainment of pure aspiration at the first ground, an attainment already explored at considerable length several milestones earlier under both the seven great natures and the ten kinds of bodhisattva in the previous fascicle. He notes that this abode, unlike the abode of the lineage, already involves active engagement with the teaching — a bodhisattva here has genuinely entered practice, cultivating the six perfections and the various marks and aspirations already explained across chapters one through three of this very yoga foundation, even though full purification still lies ahead.

He observes that the treatise has now completed its first, brief pass through the opening two abodes belonging to an ordinary bodhisattva, and signals that a second, more detailed pass is about to follow — one that will re-examine these same two abodes, together with the next two, from a different and more dynamic angle: not merely what each abode consists of in itself, but how a bodhisattva actually moves from one abode into the next, tracing the specific transition each stage makes toward the one that follows. The treatise turns next to this second, more detailed treatment.

56. Section: A Second, Dynamic Pass — How a Bodhisattva Actually Moves From the Lineage Through the First Four Abodes, Each Stage Already Setting Forth Toward the Next

The treatise now offers a second, more dynamic account of the first four abodes, tracing not merely what each consists of but how a bodhisattva actually progresses from one into the next. While still in the abode of the lineage, a bodhisattva's relation to all eleven remaining bodhisattva abodes and to the tathagata's own abode exists only at the level of cause — the seed-potential for them is gathered in, but no bodhisattva has yet set forth toward any of them, let alone attained or purified them, and the tathagata's own abode lies further still beyond reach. Once a bodhisattva reaches the abode of resolute practice, however, that bodhisattva is universally said to have set forth toward every remaining bodhisattva abode and toward the tathagata's own abode as well — though none of them has yet actually been attained or purified; and with respect to this very abode of resolute practice itself, such a bodhisattva has both set forth toward it and, in a further sense, already attained it, now cultivating correct practice specifically in order to bring it to genuine purity.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩種性住中，於餘十一諸菩薩住及如來住唯有因轉，攝受彼因。於餘所有諸菩薩住尚未發趣、未得、未淨，況如來住。

若諸菩薩勝解行住，普於一切餘菩薩住及如來住，皆名發趣，未得、未淨。即於如是勝解行住，亦名發趣、亦名為得，為令清淨而修正行。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, while bodhisattvas are in the abode of the lineage, with respect to the remaining eleven bodhisattva abodes and the tathagata's own abode, only the cause is operating — the cause for them is being gathered in. With respect to all the remaining bodhisattva abodes, such a bodhisattva has not yet set forth toward them, not yet attained them, and not yet purified them — how much less the tathagata's own abode.

If bodhisattvas are in the abode of resolute practice, with respect to all the remaining bodhisattva abodes and the tathagata's own abode, they are universally said to have set forth toward them, though not yet attained or purified them. With respect to this very abode of resolute practice itself, they are said both to have set forth toward it and to have attained it, cultivating correct practice specifically in order to bring it to purity.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains that this second pass introduces a genuinely useful set of three technical terms — setting forth, attaining, and purifying — that will structure the treatise's account of how a bodhisattva actually transitions from stage to stage throughout the remainder of this chapter. A bodhisattva in the abode of the lineage, he explains, possesses only the bare causal potential for every later abode, not having yet even formed the intention to move toward any of them; the possibility of eventual buddhahood exists, but purely as an unrealized seed, with no actual movement toward it yet underway.

The transition into the abode of resolute practice, he continues, marks the crucial moment when a bodhisattva first genuinely enters the teaching — having aroused bodhicitta, such a bodhisattva can now be said to have set forth toward every subsequent abode all at once, in the sense that the entire remaining path now lies open and intended, even though none of those later stages has actually been reached or purified yet. Toward the abode of resolute practice itself, however, this same bodhisattva has done more than merely set forth — having actually entered the provisioning and applied-effort stages, such a bodhisattva has also, in a genuine sense, attained this second abode, and now diligently cultivates correct practice specifically to bring its own remaining impurity to full purification before advancing further. He notes that this same threefold pattern — setting forth toward everything ahead, attaining the present stage, and cultivating toward that stage's own purification — will recur at every subsequent abode the treatise goes on to describe. The treatise turns next to applying this same threefold pattern to the transition from resolute practice into the abode of utmost joy.

57. Section: The Same Pattern Extends Through Utmost Joy, Excellent Discipline, and by Analogy All the Way to the Tenth Ground

Once a bodhisattva's abode of resolute practice has itself been purified, the abode of utmost joy, already set forth toward, is now said to be attained as well, and that same bodhisattva cultivates correct practice specifically to bring this new abode to its own purification. Once the abode of utmost joy has itself been purified, the abode of excellent moral discipline, already set forth toward, is likewise now attained, and correct practice is cultivated toward its purification in turn. The treatise then extends this identical pattern by analogy all the way through the remaining eight abodes: as each abode is purified, the next, already set forth toward from the very beginning, becomes newly attained, and cultivation toward its own purification begins — continuing in this same unbroken sequence until the most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode, the tenth and final ground, is itself reached.

Chinese Source

勝解行住既清淨已，極歡喜住先已發趣，今復名得，為令清淨而修正行。

極歡喜住既清淨已，增上戒住先已發趣，今復名得，為令清淨而修正行。

如是廣說，展轉乃至最上成滿菩薩住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Once the abode of resolute practice has been purified, the abode of utmost joy, already set forth toward, is now also called attained, and correct practice is cultivated in order to bring it to purity.

Once the abode of utmost joy has been purified, the abode of excellent moral discipline, already set forth toward, is now also called attained, and correct practice is cultivated in order to bring it to purity.

Extensively explained in this way, the same pattern continues in turn all the way to the most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through the transition into the abode of utmost joy as marking the passage from ordinary being to noble one: having purified the resolute-practice stage by passing through warmth, summit, forbearance, and supreme mundane dharma, a bodhisattva severs, in a single instant, the conceptually-generated clinging to a fixed self and fixed phenomena, attaining non-discriminating wisdom and the first ground's own accomplishment — already set forth toward from the moment bodhicitta was first aroused, this abode is now genuinely attained, though the first ground's own qualities are not yet completely perfected (only the perfection of giving reaches full completion there), so continued cultivation remains necessary. He explains the transition into the abode of excellent moral discipline as following the identical pattern: a first-ground bodhisattva who has already worked to remove the conceptually-generated afflictions and completes moral discipline's own perfection at the second ground thereby attains this next abode as well, though subtler afflictions still remain, again requiring continued cultivation.

He notes that the treatise, having now demonstrated this same pattern across three consecutive transitions, simply extends it by analogy through the remaining seven abodes rather than repeating identical language seven more times — each ground, once purified, reveals the next ground as newly attained, precisely because it had already been set forth toward from the very outset, all the way to the tenth ground's own final, most excellent accomplishment. He observes the deeper point this repeated pattern illustrates: even a bodhisattva who has already reached any given ground continues diligent, unflagging cultivation to purify that very ground before advancing further — a sobering reminder, he adds, that if even noble bodhisattvas from the first through the tenth ground never relax their effort, an ordinary practitioner still at the resolute-practice stage has still less excuse for laziness or complacency. The treatise turns next to explaining how the tathagata's own abode differs from every one of these bodhisattva abodes in the specific manner of its attainment.

58. Section: Sudden, Instantaneous Attainment at Buddhahood, Contrasted With the Bodhisattva's Own Gradual Attainment Across Three Great Incalculable Eons

Once this very same most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode has itself been purified, the tathagata's own abode, already set forth toward, is now attained without any interval whatsoever — one should understand that this attainment and this purification both occur suddenly, all at once. This, the treatise concludes, is the difference to be understood between the tathagata's own abode and every bodhisattva abode: where a bodhisattva progresses from one abode to the next gradually, purifying each stage in its own turn through sustained cultivation, a bodhisattva's final transition into buddhahood itself happens in a single, uninterrupted instant — the very last moment of vajra-like concentration bringing both the complete accomplishment of omniscient wisdom and the complete severing of every remaining trace of both obscurations simultaneously, with no further cultivation whatsoever required afterward.

Chinese Source

即此最上成滿菩薩住既清淨已，從此無間，其如來住先已發趣，當知於今頓得、頓淨。

是如來住於菩薩住，當知此中如是差別。

《披》頓得頓淨者：最上成滿菩薩住中，修果成滿，於如來住無間獲得，於煩惱障及所知障無間清淨，由爾所時無復修作，是故說名頓得、頓淨。此與前菩薩住一一住中漸得、漸淨有別，如是應知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Once this very most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode has been purified, the tathagata's own abode, already set forth toward from the beginning, is, from this point without any interval, now attained — one should understand that both this attainment and this purification occur suddenly.

This is the difference to be understood between the tathagata's own abode and the bodhisattva abodes.

Commentary: Regarding "sudden attainment, sudden purification" — within the most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode, the fruit of cultivation reaches its own completion, and the tathagata's own abode is obtained without any interval, both the afflictive and cognitive obscurations purified without interval as well; from that time onward, no further cultivation whatsoever remains to be done — this is why it is called sudden attainment, sudden purification. This differs from the gradual attainment, gradual purification found within each individual bodhisattva abode already described; one should understand this distinction accordingly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains "sudden attainment, sudden purification" as describing the singular, uninterrupted instant of vajra-like concentration at the very close of the tenth ground, where a bodhisattva's cultivation reaches its own complete fruition and buddhahood is obtained without so much as a moment's gap — omniscient wisdom fully accomplished and both obscurations' every remaining trace severed in that same instant, with absolutely nothing left afterward requiring further cultivation. This stands in sharp contrast, he notes, to every one of the preceding bodhisattva abodes, each of which, once attained, still requires sustained practice to bring to its own full purification before the next abode can be reached — a genuinely gradual, incremental progression at every stage prior to this final, instantaneous leap into buddhahood itself.

He recalls the treatise's own account of the three great incalculable eons this entire gradual progression spans: the first eon covering the abode of the lineage through resolute practice, terminating just before the abode of utmost joy; the second eon covering the abode of utmost joy through the seventh ground's own abode with marks and effortful application; and the third eon covering the eighth ground's abode without marks and effortful application through the tenth ground's most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode — after which this single, sudden, final instant completes the transition into buddhahood proper. He observes that this second, more dynamic pass through the first four abodes — introducing the threefold pattern of setting forth, attaining, and purifying, and now closing with this sharp contrast between the bodhisattva's gradual progression and the buddha's sudden completion — itself now closes in full. The treatise turns next to its third major subsection, unpacking each of the remaining ten abodes in its own full, individual detail, beginning once again with the abode of utmost joy.

59. Section: A Third, Intrinsic Pass Begins — Utmost Joy's Own Clarified Aspiration, and Excellent Discipline's Own Innate Precept-Nature

The treatise now turns to a third major treatment of the ten abodes from the first ground through the tenth, examining not their transitions but each one's own intrinsic defining nature in turn. What is a bodhisattva's abode of utmost joy? It is the abode of pure, excellent aspiration itself — a bodhisattva who has labored through the entire first great incalculable eon finally crosses from ordinary being to noble one, the resulting joy so intense that this abode takes its very name from that joy, the aspiration sustaining it now genuinely purified and matured. What is a bodhisattva's abode of excellent moral discipline? It is the abode where, taking pure, excellent aspiration as its condition, a bodhisattva abides joined with the innate precept-nature thereby attained — moral discipline no longer merely observed but genuinely become the bodhisattva's own spontaneous character.

Chinese Source

云何菩薩極歡喜住？謂諸菩薩淨勝意樂住。

云何菩薩增上戒住？謂諸菩薩，淨勝意樂為緣，所得性戒相應住。

《披》淨勝意樂為緣等者：如下自說：極歡喜住，由十種心意樂，已得意樂清淨。乃至廣說即由如是十種意樂成上品故，極圓滿故，是諸菩薩入證第二增上戒住。於此住中，性戒具足。極少邪惡業道所攝諸惡犯戒尚不現行，況中上品。又於十種圓滿業道，自性顯現。（陵本四十八卷一頁3815）此應準釋。

Ru-Yi Translation

What is a bodhisattva's abode of utmost joy? It is a bodhisattva's abode of pure, excellent aspiration.

What is a bodhisattva's abode of excellent moral discipline? It is the abode where, taking pure, excellent aspiration as its condition, a bodhisattva abides joined with the innate precept-nature thereby attained.

Commentary: Regarding "taking pure, excellent aspiration as its condition" and the like — as the text itself explains further below: at the abode of utmost joy, through ten kinds of mental aspiration, purity of aspiration has already been attained. And so on, extensively explained — it is precisely because these same ten kinds of aspiration have reached their superior grade, brought to the very fullest completion, that bodhisattvas enter and realize the second abode, excellent moral discipline. Within this abode, the innate precept-nature is complete; not even the very slightest unwholesome course of action belonging to precept-violation manifests, let alone one of middling or superior grade. Furthermore, with respect to the ten complete courses of wholesome action, their own intrinsic nature manifests spontaneously. This should be understood according to that fuller explanation.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains this third pass as complementary rather than redundant to the second: where the previous section traced how a bodhisattva moves from one abode into the next, this section pauses at each abode to ask what genuinely, intrinsically defines it, independent of its transition from the one before. The abode of utmost joy, he explains, is named directly for the intense joy a bodhisattva feels upon finally crossing, after an entire great incalculable eon of cultivation, from ordinary being into the ranks of the noble ones — the treatise's own later chapter will detail the ten specific kinds of mental aspiration (reverence toward every teacher regarded as a field of merit, freedom from deceit, and so on through the wish to bring every being every kind of benefit) that, having reached their superior, most complete grade, actually produce entry into this first ground.

He explains the abode of excellent moral discipline as the direct consequence of that same aspiration reaching completion: a first-ground bodhisattva's ten kinds of aspiration, brought to their fullest maturity, naturally produce a second-ground bodhisattva whose moral discipline has become genuinely innate rather than merely externally observed — not even the slightest unwholesome course of action, let alone anything more serious, could now manifest, and the ten wholesome courses of action instead arise spontaneously as this bodhisattva's own natural disposition. He notes the treatise's own forward cross-reference to fascicle 48 for the fuller list of these ten kinds of aspiration, material that will presumably be picked up once this fascicle's own account of the abodes continues there. The treatise turns next to the third abode, excellent mind.

60. Section: Excellent Mind's Own Meditative Stability, and Excellent Wisdom Named From the Very Wisdom That Observes Reality

What is a bodhisattva's abode of excellent mind? It is the abode where, taking the purity of excellent moral discipline as its condition, a bodhisattva abides in the worldly meditative absorption, concentration, and attainment thereby obtained — a third-ground bodhisattva's mind, no longer dull or scattered, resting clear and stable within contemplative practice. What is a bodhisattva's abode of excellent wisdom associated with the factors of awakening? It is the abode where, relying on the concentration that underlies worldly pure wisdom, a bodhisattva, in order to awaken to the truths, abides in the wondrous discernment cultivated through the thirty-seven factors of awakening beginning with the foundations of mindfulness — a fourth-ground bodhisattva whose wisdom, blazing like a flame capable of burning away every affliction, gives this ground its other name, the ground of blazing wisdom, named specifically from the vantage point of the observing wisdom itself.

Chinese Source

云何菩薩增上心住？謂諸菩薩，增上戒住清淨為緣，所得世間靜慮、等持、等至住。

云何菩薩覺分相應增上慧住？謂諸菩薩，以世間淨智所依等持為所依止，為覺諸諦，於正念住等三十七菩提分法妙揀擇住。

Ru-Yi Translation

What is a bodhisattva's abode of excellent mind? It is the abode where, taking the purity of excellent moral discipline as its condition, a bodhisattva abides in the worldly meditative absorption, concentration, and attainment thereby obtained.

What is a bodhisattva's abode of excellent wisdom associated with the factors of awakening? It is the abode where, relying on the concentration that underlies worldly, pure wisdom, in order to awaken to the truths, a bodhisattva abides in the wondrous discernment cultivated through the thirty-seven factors of awakening, beginning with the foundations of mindfulness.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains meditative concentration's classical definition, cited from the treatise's own Stage of Disciples: binding mindfulness to a single object, stilled and correctly examined with careful reflection — this is what "meditative absorption" itself means, while "concentration" names the broader category encompassing both the desire-realm's own scattered-mind concentration and the form and formless realms' deeper absorptions, and "attainment" refers more narrowly to the states from the first dhyana upward. A third-ground bodhisattva, having purified moral discipline at the second ground, now cultivates this same concentration to genuine stability, the mind neither sinking into dullness nor scattering into distraction — a settled, luminous clarity marking this ground's own distinctive accomplishment.

He explains the fourth ground's name as reflecting the vantage point of the observing wisdom itself: a bodhisattva here diligently cultivates the entire thirty-seven factors of awakening — the four foundations of mindfulness, four right efforts, four bases of supernormal power, five faculties, five powers, seven factors of awakening, and eightfold noble path — contemplating the four noble truths (or, in the great vehicle's own reading, both conventional and ultimate truth together) with wisdom that grows correspondingly more luminous as concentration deepens from worldly absorption toward the threshold of uncontaminated realization. This wisdom, capable of burning away affliction the way a blazing flame consumes what it touches, is precisely why the fourth ground is also called the ground of blazing wisdom — and because this abode is named specifically from the standpoint of the wisdom that observes reality, distinct from the fifth ground's own naming from the standpoint of what is observed, the treatise calls it "excellent wisdom associated with the factors of awakening" rather than simply repeating the same name for all three wisdom-grounds indiscriminately. The treatise turns next to the fifth ground, named instead from the object such wisdom actually observes.

61. Section: The Truths Themselves Give the Fifth Ground Its Name, and Dependent Origination's Flow and Stilling Give the Sixth

What is a bodhisattva's abode of excellent wisdom associated with the truths? It is the abode where, relying on the discerning wisdom already cultivated through the factors of awakening, a bodhisattva abides in genuine, correct realization with respect to the truths themselves — a fifth-ground bodhisattva who has achieved the genuinely difficult feat of harmonizing conventional and ultimate truth without contradiction, whether in meditative absorption or out of it, which is precisely why this ground is also called the ground hard to conquer, this abode named here specifically from the standpoint of the truths actually observed. And what is a bodhisattva's abode of excellent wisdom associated with the stilling of the flow of dependent origination? It is the abode where, through the very power of realizing the truths, a bodhisattva discerns and reveals both how suffering and its causes arise through lack of wisdom, and how suffering and its causes cease through the presence of wisdom — a sixth-ground bodhisattva whose penetrating discernment of dependent origination's own flow and stilling gives this ground its name, and its other title, the ground of manifestation, since non-discriminating wisdom here becomes fully present before the mind.

Chinese Source

云何菩薩諸諦相應增上慧住？謂諸菩薩，覺分揀擇為所依止，於諸諦中如實覺住。

云何菩薩緣起流轉止息相應增上慧住？謂諸菩薩，於諦能覺增上力故，揀擇顯示由無智故，苦及因起；揀擇顯示由有智故，苦及因滅住。

《披》由無智故苦及因起等者：此中無智，所謂無明。苦謂生及老死。餘緣起支能生苦故，苦胎藏故，皆名為因。義如有尋有伺地說。（陵本十卷十四頁800）是名緣起流轉。與此相違，名由有智。苦及因滅，是名緣起止息。

Ru-Yi Translation

What is a bodhisattva's abode of excellent wisdom associated with the truths? It is the abode where, relying on the discernment already cultivated through the factors of awakening, a bodhisattva abides in genuine, correct realization with respect to the truths.

What is a bodhisattva's abode of excellent wisdom associated with the stilling of the flow of dependent origination? It is the abode where, through the power of realizing the truths, a bodhisattva discerns and reveals that, through lack of wisdom, suffering and its cause arise; and discerns and reveals that, through the presence of wisdom, suffering and its cause cease.

Commentary: Regarding "through lack of wisdom, suffering and its cause arise" and the like — lack of wisdom here means ignorance. Suffering means the links of birth and of aging-and-death. The remaining links of dependent origination, since they are capable of producing suffering, harboring its seed within themselves, are all called cause. This meaning is as explained in the Stage With Both Investigation and Analysis. This is called the flow of dependent origination. Its opposite is called the presence of wisdom, whereby suffering and its cause cease — this is called the stilling of dependent origination.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains why the fifth ground earns the name "hard to conquer": from the first through the fourth ground, a bodhisattva can only access mark-free, non-discriminating absorption while actually seated in meditation, losing that same realization the moment ordinary discriminated activity resumes; only at the fifth ground does a bodhisattva achieve the genuinely difficult feat of harmonizing uncontaminated realization with everyday, discriminated engagement, in meditation and out of it alike, no longer needing to choose between them — precisely the difficulty this ground's own name honors. He notes that where the fourth ground was named from the standpoint of the observing wisdom, the fifth is named instead from the standpoint of the truths themselves as the object that wisdom observes, the same underlying realization simply described from its object's own side.

He then unpacks the sixth ground's own rich technical content, distinguishing dependent origination's twelve links into three functional categories the commentary itself gestures toward: birth and aging-and-death are the "generated" links, the visible fruit of suffering itself; ignorance and volitional formations are the "generating" links, the causes actively producing the entire chain; and consciousness, name-and-form, the six sense-bases, contact, and feeling are the "drawn-forth" links, seeds already implanted though not yet fully ripened, while craving, grasping, and existence are the links that actually activate those seeds into ripening suffering — every link apart from birth and aging-and-death itself thus counting as some form of cause, all of them together constituting what the treatise's own Stage With Both Investigation and Analysis, fascicle 10, explains at far greater length. A sixth-ground bodhisattva, having attained non-discriminating wisdom and the suchness of personal and phenomenal emptiness, genuinely understands both directions at once — what conditions produce continued flow through this same suffering, and what conditions instead bring it to rest — which is why this ground is named specifically for dependent origination's own flow and stilling, and is also called the ground of manifestation, since non-discriminating wisdom here becomes fully, directly present before the mind for the first time in this particular, integrated way. He notes that these three wisdom-grounds together — named from the observing wisdom, from the observed truths, and from dependent origination's own flow and stilling — all equally realize the selfless nature of phenomena through prajña wisdom, differing only in which angle of that single realization each name happens to highlight. The treatise turns next to the seventh ground.

62. Section: The Seventh Ground's Effortful Mark-Free Abiding, and the Eighth Ground's Effortless, Spontaneous Version of the Same

What is a bodhisattva's abode with marks and effortful application? It is the abode where, through the combined power of the three preceding abodes of excellent wisdom, a bodhisattva abides joined with effortful, applied, uninterrupted cultivation of the non-discriminating wisdom realizing the suchness of all phenomena — a seventh-ground bodhisattva who has genuinely realized that every phenomenon posited through language is ultimately empty, and that dependently-arisen phenomena, though real in their own illusion-like way, are equally beyond language's own grasp, yet who still requires deliberate, sustained effort to keep this mark-free realization continuously active. And what is a bodhisattva's abode without marks and without effortful application? It is the very same mark-free abiding, now thoroughly habituated through sustained practice at the seventh ground, become entirely spontaneous — an eighth-ground bodhisattva who abides in this same uninterrupted, mark-free path effortlessly, requiring no further deliberate application at all.

Chinese Source

云何菩薩有加行有功用無相住？謂諸菩薩，即由三種增上慧住增上力故，有加行、有功用、無闕無間，於一切法真如無分別慧修俱行住。

《披》於一切法真如等者：諸法無我，名一切法真如。由此為緣，達無少法及少品類可起分別，於一切法平等平等如實觀察，是名無分別慧。由此住中，於加行行修圓滿故，是名修俱行住。

云何菩薩無加行無功用無相住？謂諸菩薩，即於前無相住多修習已，任運自然、無闕無間運轉道隨行住。

Ru-Yi Translation

What is a bodhisattva's abode with marks and effortful application? It is the abode where, through the combined power of the three abodes of excellent wisdom, a bodhisattva abides joined with effortful, applied, uninterrupted cultivation of the non-discriminating wisdom realizing the suchness of all phenomena.

Commentary: Regarding "the suchness of all phenomena" and the like — the selflessness of all phenomena is called the suchness of all phenomena. Taking this as the condition, realizing that not the slightest phenomenon or category of phenomena can any longer give rise to discrimination, observing all phenomena with genuine, equal, undiscriminating accuracy, is called non-discriminating wisdom. Because, within this abode, the practice of applied effort has reached its own completion, this is called cultivation joined with practice.

What is a bodhisattva's abode without marks and without effortful application? It is this same abode, mark-free abiding already thoroughly cultivated, now spontaneous and natural, continuing without interruption along this same path.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the seventh ground's achievement as building directly on the three wisdom-grounds just closed: a bodhisattva here has genuinely penetrated that everything the sixth consciousness posits through language — form, feeling, perception, formations, consciousness, even nirvana itself — is ultimately empty of any real, fixed self or possession, this being the "suchness" of all phenomena, and, taking this realization as condition, no longer finds even the smallest phenomenon or category capable of provoking discriminating thought, observing everything with genuinely equal, undistorted accuracy. Yet this mark-free abiding, however genuine, still requires deliberate, sustained application to remain continuously active — the seventh ground's own defining limitation, still needing conscious effort even though free of gaps or interruption once that effort is actually applied.

He explains the eighth ground's own advance as simply this identical mark-free realization, now so thoroughly habituated through the seventh ground's own sustained cultivation that it continues spontaneously, without any further deliberate effort needed at all — from the eighth ground onward, a bodhisattva abides in accord with personal and phenomenal emptiness at every moment, free of conceptual overlay, free of merely designated appearance, and free of any mentally-constructed image, entirely as a matter of natural course rather than sustained exertion. He notes that this same effortless, spontaneous mark-free abiding, once achieved at the eighth ground, is precisely what the previous fascicle's own account of the eighth ground as "the ground of certainty" already described from a different angle — the same attainment, examined here specifically through the lens of effort versus effortlessness. The treatise turns next to the ninth ground, the abode of unobstructed understanding.

63. Section: The Ninth Ground's Boundless Retention and Unobstructed Eloquence, and the Tenth Ground's Reception of the Great Dharma-Consecration — Closing All Ten Abodes' Own Defining Nature

What is a bodhisattva's abode of unobstructed understanding? It is the abode where, relying on the genuinely pure, unmoving wisdom and concentration already attained, a bodhisattva obtains vast wisdom capable of teaching others, and, grounded in this unsurpassed capacity, abides in the wondrous discernment of the teaching's own varied approaches, its meaning, its explanatory terms, and its distinctions — a ninth-ground bodhisattva who has attained boundless mnemonic retention and, through the four kinds of unobstructed understanding, can address any audience with inexhaustible, perfectly matched eloquence. And what is a bodhisattva's most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode? It is the abode where, having brought the entire bodhisattva path to its own ultimate completion, a bodhisattva, already receiving the great dharma-consecration toward unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening, either bound to only one more life or abiding already in the very last existence, proceeds without any interval from this abode directly into the realization of unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening itself, thereby able to perform every deed a buddha performs.

Chinese Source

云何菩薩無礙解住？謂諸菩薩，即以善清淨無動慧等持為所依止，得廣大慧，為他說法；無上為依，能於諸法異門、義趣、釋詞、差別妙揀擇住。

《披》得廣大慧等者：謂於諸法起智加行，能為他說一切種法，普於一切說法所作皆如實知，是名得廣大慧，為他說法。獲得無量陀羅尼門，於一切種音詞支具剖析善巧，辯才無盡，是名無上為依，乃至妙揀擇住。

云何菩薩最上成滿菩薩住？謂諸菩薩安住於此，於菩薩道已到究竟，於阿耨多羅三藐三菩提已得大法灌頂，或一生所繫，或居最後有。從此住無間，即於爾時證覺無上正等菩提，能作一切佛所作事。

Ru-Yi Translation

What is a bodhisattva's abode of unobstructed understanding? It is the abode where, relying on the genuinely pure, unmoving wisdom and concentration already attained, a bodhisattva obtains vast wisdom for teaching others; and, grounded in this unsurpassed capacity, abides in the wondrous discernment of the varied approaches, meaning, explanatory terms, and distinctions of all phenomena.

Commentary: Regarding "obtains vast wisdom" and the like — this means giving rise to applied wisdom with respect to all phenomena, able to teach every kind of teaching to others, genuinely knowing everything involved in every kind of teaching activity — this is called obtaining vast wisdom for teaching others. Obtaining boundless gateways of mnemonic retention, thoroughly and skillfully analyzing every kind of phonetic and verbal element, with inexhaustible eloquence — this is called grounded in this unsurpassed capacity, down through wondrous discernment.

What is a bodhisattva's most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode? It is the abode where a bodhisattva, abiding here, has brought the bodhisattva path to its own ultimate completion, has already received the great dharma-consecration toward unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening, and is either bound to only one more life or already abides in the very last existence. From this abode, without interval, at that very moment, such a bodhisattva realizes unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening, thereby able to perform every deed a buddha performs.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi illustrates the ninth ground's boundless mnemonic retention with a vivid contrast: where an ordinary being's memory functions like a sieve, losing what it takes in even after deliberate effort to memorize it, a ninth-ground bodhisattva's mind functions like a camera, retaining everything encountered in full, including lengthy mantras composed of many syllables without any inherent conceptual meaning, entirely through the sheer power of cultivated mindfulness. Such a bodhisattva, having attained the four kinds of unobstructed understanding to their fullest degree, can thoroughly and skillfully analyze the meaning of any teaching in any language whatsoever, addressing whatever audience presents itself with perfectly matched, inexhaustible eloquence — the treatise's own name for this ground, the ground of excellent wisdom, capturing precisely this combination of retentive power and eloquent, unobstructed teaching ability.

He explains the tenth and final ground as the very summit of bodhisattva attainment: having brought the ten perfections to their own ultimate completion, such a bodhisattva has already received what the tradition calls the great dharma-consecration from the buddhas of the ten directions, confirming imminent buddhahood — illustrated by the bodhisattva Maitreya, presently dwelling in the Tushita heaven, bound to only one more life before descending to attain buddhahood in the human realm, or by a bodhisattva already in this very last existence, about to display the eight aspects of a buddha's own life. From this tenth-ground abode, the treatise specifies, the transition into buddhahood itself occurs without any interval whatsoever, that same bodhisattva now able to perform every deed a buddha performs — teaching, liberating, and manifesting throughout the ten directions. With this tenth and final ground now closed, Cháng Bǎi observes, the treatise has completed its third major pass through the ten grounds, examining each one's own intrinsic, defining nature in turn — a treatment genuinely distinct from, and complementary to, the earlier pass examining how a bodhisattva transitions from each ground into the next. The treatise turns next to a further, fourth topic: the specific differences in how cultivation and attainment actually unfold across these same abodes.

64. Section: How Cultivation of the Six Perfections Itself Grows in Scope — Narrow at Resolute Practice, Vast From the First Ground, Immeasurable From the Seventh

The treatise turns to a fourth major topic, examining in detail the specific differences in how cultivation and attainment actually unfold across the twelve abodes — opening with differences in the very scope of practicing the six perfections themselves. Bodhisattvas at the abode of resolute practice, still ordinary beings who have aroused bodhicitta but whose aspiration toward wholesome cultivation is not yet firmly settled, find their own practice of the six perfections narrow in scope, incomplete in execution, and inconstant in continuity, so that whatever is accomplished can still regress. From the abode of utmost joy onward, however, through the three abodes of excellent wisdom, a bodhisattva's practice of the six perfections becomes vast in scope, complete without gaps, and settled beyond wavering, so that whatever is attained never again regresses or reverses. And from the very first mark-free abode, the seventh ground, all the way through the most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode, the tenth, a bodhisattva's practice becomes immeasurable in scope, complete without gaps, and settled beyond wavering, so that whatever is attained is never lost.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩勝解行住，於菩薩修所作狹小、所作有闕、所作不定，所得有退。

《披》於菩薩修者：此說六波羅蜜多應知。

極歡喜住，於菩薩修所作廣大、所作無闕、所作決定，隨所獲得無復退轉。如極歡喜住，乃至三種增上慧住，應知亦爾。

從初無相住乃至最上成滿菩薩住，於菩薩修所作無量、所作無闕、所作決定，隨所獲得終無退轉。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, bodhisattvas at the abode of resolute practice, with respect to a bodhisattva's own cultivation, find it narrow in scope, incomplete in execution, and inconstant, so that whatever is attained can regress.

Commentary: Regarding "a bodhisattva's own cultivation" — this refers to the six perfections, one should understand.

At the abode of utmost joy, with respect to a bodhisattva's own cultivation, it becomes vast in scope, complete without gaps, and settled, so that whatever is attained never again regresses. One should understand that the same holds from the abode of utmost joy through the three abodes of excellent wisdom.

From the first mark-free abode through the most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode, with respect to a bodhisattva's own cultivation, it becomes immeasurable in scope, complete without gaps, and settled, so that whatever is attained is never lost.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains this fourth major topic as complementing the three prior passes with yet another angle — not what each abode consists of, nor how a bodhisattva transitions between them, but specifically how the scale and reliability of actual practice changes as a bodhisattva advances. An ordinary bodhisattva at the resolute-practice stage, not yet having settled aspiration firmly, naturally practices the six perfections within a genuinely limited scope, with real gaps in execution, and inconsistently over time — sometimes practicing diligently, sometimes lapsing — which is precisely why whatever merit accumulates at this stage remains vulnerable to regression.

He explains that this vulnerability disappears entirely once the first ground is reached: from the abode of utmost joy through the sixth ground's own three abodes of excellent wisdom, a bodhisattva's practice of the six perfections expands into genuinely vast scope, proceeds without interruption or omission, and settles into an unwavering constancy, such that nothing gained at this stage can ever again be lost — the qualitative transformation marking entry into the noble path itself. And from the seventh ground onward, he continues, this same practice expands further still into what the treatise calls immeasurable scope, the six perfections now cultivated on a scale an ordinary being could scarcely conceive, with the same complete, unwavering constancy sustained all the way through the tenth ground's own final accomplishment. The treatise turns next to examining this same threefold progression — narrow, vast, and immeasurable — specifically with respect to mark-free cultivation itself.

65. Section: The Five Stages of Mark-Free Cultivation — Setting Forth, Attaining, Fully Realizing, Purifying, and Finally Receiving Its Fruit

The treatise now examines mark-free cultivation itself through five distinct stages across the very same abodes. Bodhisattvas at the abode of resolute practice, with respect to mark-free cultivation, should be understood as having set forth toward it — an ordinary bodhisattva who has not yet entered the noble path but has already aroused the aspiration toward the language-transcending true nature of phenomena that mark-free cultivation ultimately realizes. Bodhisattvas at the abodes of utmost joy, excellent moral discipline, excellent mind, and the three abodes of excellent wisdom, with respect to mark-free cultivation, should be understood as having attained it — a first-through-sixth-ground bodhisattva who has already obtained the mark-free absorption itself. Bodhisattvas at the first mark-free abode, the seventh ground, with respect to mark-free cultivation, should be understood as having fully realized it. Bodhisattvas at the second mark-free abode, the eighth ground, with respect to mark-free cultivation, should be understood as having purified it, now abiding in spontaneous accord with mark-free absorption. And bodhisattvas at the abode of unobstructed understanding and the most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode, the ninth and tenth grounds, with respect to the fruit of mark-free cultivation, should be understood as receiving it.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩勝解行住，於菩薩無相修，當知發趣。

《披》於菩薩無相修者：此說諸法離言自性。真實義相名為無相應知。

極歡喜住、增上戒住、增上心住、增上慧住，於菩薩無相修，當知獲得。

初無相住，於菩薩無相修，當知圓證。

第二無相住，於菩薩無相修，當知清淨。

無礙解住、最上成滿菩薩住，於菩薩無相修果，當知領受。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, bodhisattvas at the abode of resolute practice, with respect to a bodhisattva's mark-free cultivation, should be understood as having set forth toward it.

Commentary: Regarding "a bodhisattva's mark-free cultivation" — this refers to the language-transcending nature of all phenomena. The mark of genuine reality is called mark-free, one should understand.

Bodhisattvas at the abodes of utmost joy, excellent moral discipline, excellent mind, and excellent wisdom, with respect to a bodhisattva's mark-free cultivation, should be understood as having attained it.

Bodhisattvas at the first mark-free abode, with respect to a bodhisattva's mark-free cultivation, should be understood as having fully realized it.

Bodhisattvas at the second mark-free abode, with respect to a bodhisattva's mark-free cultivation, should be understood as having purified it.

Bodhisattvas at the abode of unobstructed understanding and the most excellent, fully accomplished bodhisattva abode, with respect to the fruit of a bodhisattva's mark-free cultivation, should be understood as receiving it.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi identifies this same fivefold structure — setting forth, attaining, fully realizing, purifying, and receiving — as the very passage the previous fascicle's own commentary already cited when explaining the sixth of the eight factors comprising the entire great vehicle, at the time only partially quoted and now, at last, appearing here in its own full, primary context. He explains "mark-free" as referring to the language-transcending true nature of all phenomena — reality's own mark being precisely the absence of any mark expressible through ordinary conceptual language — and walks through each stage in turn: an ordinary bodhisattva at resolute practice has only set forth toward this realization, aspiration aroused but direct realization not yet attained; a bodhisattva from the first through the sixth ground has genuinely attained the mark-free absorption itself, though still needing the seventh ground's own sustained effort to make it fully, continuously operative; the seventh-ground bodhisattva has brought this cultivation to full, complete realization, though still requiring deliberate application to sustain it; the eighth-ground bodhisattva has purified this same realization into spontaneous, effortless accord, no longer needing any application whatsoever; and the ninth and tenth-ground bodhisattvas, having reached this settled, effortless accomplishment, now receive its own resulting fruit — the eloquence and dharma-consecration already described under each ground's own intrinsic nature.

He observes that this fivefold structure and the immediately preceding threefold structure (narrow, vast, immeasurable) describe the very same overall progression from two complementary angles — one tracking the six perfections' own general cultivation, the other tracking specifically the deepening of mark-free, non-discriminating realization — both converging on the same basic shape of the path: limited and unstable at the outset, genuinely secure from the first ground, and immeasurably vast and finally fruitful from the seventh ground onward. With this, the fourth major topic within chapter four's treatment of the twelve abodes — differences in how cultivation and attainment actually unfold — closes in full. The treatise turns next to an extensive, eleven-part detailed analysis, beginning with a much closer examination of the abode of resolute practice's own specific shortcomings and limitations.

66. Section: An Eleven-Part Deep Dive Opens — What Conduct, State, and Mark Characterizes a Bodhisattva Turning Within the Abode of Resolute Practice?

The treatise now opens an eleven-part extensive analysis, examining the conduct, inner state, and aspiration proper to each of the twelve abodes in genuinely fine-grained detail — far beyond the three prior passes already completed. It begins with the abode of resolute practice, posing the question directly: when a bodhisattva turns within the abode of resolute practice, what conduct, what state, and what mark should be understood as characterizing that bodhisattva? The treatise's own commentary clarifies these three terms before the answer proper begins: conduct refers to setting forth, state refers to attaining, and mark refers to the natural outflow that follows attainment — since the text itself will later explain that the various practices a bodhisattva undertakes, capable of leading into genuine realization, are called conduct; the moment of actually entering that realization is called attainment; and the fruit and benefit that come to full completion once one has already entered is called the natural outflow. This same threefold framework, once introduced here, will be applied identically across all twelve abodes to follow.

Chinese Source

問：勝解行住菩薩轉時，應知何行、何狀、何相？

《披》應知何行何狀何相者：行謂發趣，狀謂獲得，相謂等流。如下說言：諸行能入，說名為行；若正入時，說名為得；入已果利成辦圓證，說名等流。如是次第配釋應知。下皆準釋。

Ru-Yi Translation

Question: When a bodhisattva turns within the abode of resolute practice, what conduct, what state, and what mark should be understood as characterizing that bodhisattva?

Commentary: Regarding "what conduct, what state, and what mark should be understood" — conduct refers to setting forth; state refers to attaining; mark refers to the natural outflow. As explained further below: the practices capable of leading into realization are called conduct; the moment of actually entering it is called attaining; and, once one has entered, the fruit and benefit brought to full, complete realization is called the natural outflow. This same sequence of correspondence should be understood accordingly; everything that follows should be explained according to this same standard.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi announces that this eleven-part section marks a genuinely new level of granularity beyond anything the chapter has offered so far — where the three prior passes examined the abodes' own transitions, intrinsic natures, and differing scales of cultivation, this fourth treatment will examine, abode by abode, the specific conduct, inner psychological state, and resulting character each stage's own bodhisattva actually displays, addressing questions like what such a bodhisattva still fears, what such a bodhisattva has not yet mastered, and in what specific ways such a bodhisattva's own attainment might still be vulnerable to regression.

He explains the commentary's own threefold vocabulary carefully, since it will recur unchanged across all eleven parts to follow: "conduct" names the actual practices a bodhisattva undertakes that are capable, in principle, of leading toward genuine realization — the six perfections and related cultivation; "state" names the specific moment such practice actually culminates in entering a new level of realization; and "mark" names the settled character, the natural outflow of qualities, that follows once that realization has been brought to its own full completion. He notes that this careful terminological setup is necessary precisely because the analysis to follow will be so detailed and so consistently structured across each of the twelve abodes that a reader needs this vocabulary firmly in hand before the actual content begins. The treatise turns next to answering the question just posed, opening with a surprisingly candid account of everything the resolute-practice bodhisattva has not yet achieved.

67. Section: Not Yet Spontaneous, Not Yet Firmly Settled — and Five Fears an Ordinary Bodhisattva Has Not Yet Overcome

The treatise answers with striking candor: when a bodhisattva turns within the abode of resolute practice, the power of discerning reflection predominates — such a bodhisattva can only actually carry out the various applied efforts proper to a bodhisattva by means of discriminating wisdom, reflecting again and again before acting, not yet able to accomplish what needs to be done spontaneously, as a matter of course. Such a bodhisattva has not yet attained the firm, continuous, irreversible superior cultivation that belongs to the abode of utmost joy — resolve not yet fully settled, practice not yet sustained without gaps, and whatever is attained still liable to regress or change. Correspondingly, such a bodhisattva has likewise not yet attained superior cultivation's own resulting fruits — the various kinds of unobstructed understanding, resolute understanding, supernormal knowledge, liberation, concentration, and meditative attainment — and has not yet transcended five specific fears: fear of not surviving, fear of ill repute, fear of death, fear of falling into an unfortunate rebirth, and fear of speaking timidly before an assembly.

Chinese Source

答：勝解行住菩薩轉時，思擇力勝；於諸菩薩所作加行，以分別慧數數思擇方能修作，未能任性成辦所作。

未得堅固相續無退菩薩勝修。

《披》未得堅固相續無退菩薩勝修者：所作決定，是名堅固。所作無闕，是名相續。隨所獲得無復退轉，是名無退。極歡喜住有此修相，故名勝修。勝解行住未能現前，故名未得。

如於勝修，於勝修果種種無礙、勝解、神通、解脫、等持、等至，亦未能得。

未能超越五種怖畏。謂不活畏、惡名畏、死畏、惡趣畏、處眾怯畏。

Ru-Yi Translation

Answer: When a bodhisattva turns within the abode of resolute practice, the power of discerning reflection predominates; with respect to the applied efforts proper to a bodhisattva, only by means of discriminating wisdom, reflecting again and again, can such efforts actually be carried out — not yet able to accomplish what needs to be done spontaneously, as a matter of course.

Such a bodhisattva has not yet attained the firm, continuous, irreversible superior cultivation of a bodhisattva.

Commentary: Regarding "not yet attained the firm, continuous, irreversible superior cultivation of a bodhisattva" — what is undertaken with settled resolve is called firm. What is undertaken without any gap is called continuous. Whatever is attained, never again regressing, is called irreversible. The abode of utmost joy possesses this pattern of cultivation, which is why it is called superior cultivation. The abode of resolute practice has not yet manifested this, which is why it is said to have not yet attained it.

Just as with superior cultivation itself, such a bodhisattva has likewise not yet attained superior cultivation's own various fruits — unobstructed understanding, resolute understanding, supernormal knowledge, liberation, concentration, and meditative attainment.

Such a bodhisattva has not yet transcended five kinds of fear — namely, fear of not surviving, fear of ill repute, fear of death, fear of falling into an unfortunate rebirth, and fear of timidity before an assembly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this opening answer as a genuinely honest, almost humbling portrait of where an ordinary bodhisattva actually stands: even having aroused bodhicitta and begun sincere practice, such a bodhisattva still requires deliberate, repeated reflection before acting rightly — the six perfections not yet second nature but still requiring conscious effort each time, a far cry from the effortless spontaneity a noble bodhisattva eventually attains. He unpacks the commentary's own precise definitions of firm, continuous, and irreversible cultivation, noting that the first-ground bodhisattva genuinely possesses all three together, which is exactly why that ground's cultivation earns the name "superior," while the resolute-practice bodhisattva, lacking settled resolve, lacking uninterrupted continuity, and still vulnerable to regression, has simply not yet reached this same threshold.

He walks through the five fears in vivid, concrete terms: fear of not surviving drives an ordinary bodhisattva, even while practicing giving, to hold back some reserve out of anxiety about future livelihood, unable to give away everything without restraint; fear of ill repute makes such a bodhisattva hesitant to teach or correct others too directly, worried about how doing so might be perceived; fear of death, since the acceptance of the non-arising of phenomena has not yet been attained, makes such a bodhisattva still cling to life and hold back from the most extreme forms of self-sacrifice, such as giving away one's own body; fear of an unfortunate rebirth, since the causes leading to the three lower realms have not yet been fully eliminated, produces genuine anxiety about what might follow this present life; and fear of speaking timidly before an assembly, since the four kinds of unobstructed understanding have not yet been attained, makes such a bodhisattva prone to hesitation and nervousness when teaching before a large audience. He notes that this candid catalogue of remaining fears and limitations is not meant to discourage but to clarify exactly what distinguishes an ordinary bodhisattva's own genuine, sincere effort from a noble bodhisattva's settled, fearless accomplishment — precisely the kind of honest self-assessment a serious practitioner needs. The treatise turns next to how this same incompleteness shows up specifically in a bodhisattva's relationship with other beings.

68. Section: Compassion That Still Requires Effort, Occasional Lapses Into Wrong Conduct, and Trust in the Three Jewels Not Yet Personally Verified

The treatise continues its candid portrait: with respect to whatever should be done to benefit sentient beings, a bodhisattva at the abode of resolute practice must still consciously exhort and encourage the self before actually managing to act, not yet able to give rise spontaneously to compassionate, loving concern for beings; such a bodhisattva may, at times, still give rise to wrongful conduct of body, speech, or mind toward beings; may, at times, still give rise to attachment toward some object; and may, at times, still display genuine stinginess regarding material possessions. The treatise then turns to this same bodhisattva's inner aspiration, beginning with attitude toward the three jewels: such a bodhisattva acts on trust in the buddhas and bodhisattvas, not yet having personally, inwardly realized the truth for themselves — believing in the Buddha, the teaching, the community, genuine reality, the maturation of beings, the buddhas' and bodhisattvas' own supernormal power, causes, results, what is to be attained, and the means of attaining it, entirely by following what has been taught rather than through direct, personal verification, and thereby accomplishing only a narrow wisdom of hearing and reflecting, not yet the boundless wisdom that follows genuine realization.

Chinese Source

於所應作利有情事，策勵思惟方能修作，未能任性哀愍愛念。或於一時，於諸有情，由身語意發起邪行；或於一時，於諸境界發起貪著；或於一時，於資生具現有慳吝。

信他諸佛菩薩而行，未能自內了知真實。

謂於如來、或法、或僧、或真實義、或有情事、或佛菩薩神通威力、或因、或果、或應得義、或得方便、或於所行皆隨他信。成就狹小聞所成智、思所成智，而非無量。

Ru-Yi Translation

With respect to what should be done to benefit sentient beings, such a bodhisattva must consciously exhort and reflect before being able to act, not yet able to give rise spontaneously to compassionate, loving concern. At times, such a bodhisattva may still give rise, through body, speech, or mind, to wrongful conduct toward beings; at times may give rise to attachment toward some object; at times may display genuine stinginess regarding material possessions.

Such a bodhisattva acts on trust in the buddhas and bodhisattvas, not yet having personally, inwardly realized the truth.

This means that, with respect to the tathagata, or the teaching, or the community, or genuine reality, or the maturation of beings, or the buddhas' and bodhisattvas' own supernormal power, or cause, or result, or what is to be attained, or the means of attainment, or with respect to one's own conduct, such a bodhisattva follows entirely on trust in what has been taught by others, thereby accomplishing only a narrow wisdom of hearing and a narrow wisdom of reflection, not yet boundless wisdom.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this passage's frank admission that an ordinary bodhisattva may still, at times, lapse into wrongful conduct, attachment, or stinginess as a further instance of the same honest self-assessment already seen with the five fears — genuine sincerity in the path does not require pretending to a perfection not yet actually achieved, and a bodhisattva at this stage should expect occasional lapses precisely because the deeper transformation belonging to the noble grounds has not yet occurred. Compassion here still requires deliberate self-exhortation rather than arising as an automatic, settled disposition — a meaningful but real distinction from the effortless compassion a noble bodhisattva eventually displays.

He then unpacks the commentary's own citation of the eight bases of resolute understanding from the chapter on the power of the lineage, fascicle 38, which this present passage draws directly upon: resolute understanding regarding the qualities of the three jewels, regarding the buddhas' and bodhisattvas' own power, regarding genuine reality, regarding cause, regarding result, regarding what is to be attained (nirvana, whether the lesser vehicle's own or the great vehicle's own unabiding great nirvana), regarding the means of attaining it, and regarding skillful speech and exposition. A resolute-practice bodhisattva holds genuine confidence across all eight of these, Cháng Bǎi explains, but that confidence remains a matter of trust in the buddhas' and bodhisattvas' own testimony rather than personal, direct verification — an outer-ordinary-being bodhisattva's mind still operates, in a sense, outside the ultimate truth itself, prone to losing hold of correct mindfulness regarding it, while an inner-ordinary-being bodhisattva at the applied-effort stage has begun operating within reach of that truth though not yet fully matured, and only a noble bodhisattva has genuinely entered and abides within it. This is precisely why the wisdom such trust produces, however genuine, remains narrow — confined to what hearing and reflecting alone can establish — rather than the boundless wisdom that follows actual, personal realization. The treatise turns next to further specific limitations still characterizing this same abode of resolute practice.

69. Section: Teachings Sometimes Forgotten, and Aspiration Toward Great Awakening Not Yet Vigorous, Firm, or Unshakeable

The treatise continues: precisely because such a bodhisattva's wisdom remains narrow, confined to hearing and reflecting, there are times when even what has been trusted and understood is simply forgotten — a real lapse in retention. Turning next to this bodhisattva's aspiration toward great awakening itself, the treatise observes that such a bodhisattva, belonging to the class of practitioners whose progress is painful and slow, has not yet developed vigorous eagerness toward great awakening, not yet blazing diligence, and not yet a profound, unshakeable, pure faith regarding it — a bodhisattva whose wholesome roots remain at a lesser grade of maturity, whose corresponding eagerness and effort remain correspondingly modest.

Chinese Source

又即於此或時忘失，有忘失法。

成就菩薩苦遲通行，於大菩提無猛利樂欲，無熾然精進，無有甚深牢固淨信。

《披》於大菩提無猛利樂欲等者：成熟品說：若時菩薩住中品成熟，爾時便有中品欲樂、中品加行。此盡第二無數劫邊際，熾然、無動覺品善法已得相應。即於此中，性淳厚故，極猛盛故，能有上品廣大果故，大勝利故，名為熾然。不轉還故，不退墮故，常勝進故，名為無動。（陵本三十七卷二十六頁3093）今勝解行住菩薩住下品成熟，唯有下品欲樂、下品加行。由是說無猛利樂欲、熾然精進。有轉還故，有退墮故，非為無動，由是說無甚深牢固淨信。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, precisely within this same narrow wisdom, there are times when it is simply forgotten — there is a genuine capacity for forgetting.

Such a bodhisattva belongs to the class whose progress toward awakening is painful and slow, holding no vigorous eagerness toward great awakening, no blazing diligence, and no profound, unshakeable, pure faith regarding it.

Commentary: Regarding "no vigorous eagerness toward great awakening" and the like — the Chapter on Maturation explains: when a bodhisattva abides in middling-grade maturity, there is then middling-grade eagerness and middling-grade applied effort. Reaching all the way to the boundary of the second incalculable eon, such a bodhisattva's blazing, unshakeable factors of awakening have already become joined with wholesome qualities. Within this, because the disposition itself has become genuinely deep and sincere, and extremely vigorous, capable of producing a superior, vast result and great benefit, this is called blazing. Because there is no turning back, no falling into regression, and constant, superior advancement, this is called unshakeable. The present bodhisattva at the abode of resolute practice, however, abides in lesser-grade maturity, possessing only lesser-grade eagerness and lesser-grade applied effort — this is why it is said such a bodhisattva has no vigorous eagerness and no blazing diligence. Since there is still the possibility of turning back and falling into regression, such a bodhisattva is not yet unshakeable — this is why it is said such a bodhisattva has no profound, unshakeable, pure faith.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes that this admission of occasional forgetfulness follows directly from the narrow wisdom already established — a wisdom confined to hearing and reflecting, not yet grounded in direct realization, naturally remains vulnerable to lapses of memory that a noble bodhisattva's own settled, cultivated wisdom would not suffer. He then unpacks the commentary's own three-tiered account of maturity from the chapter on maturation, fascicle 37: lesser-grade maturity belongs to the resolute-practice bodhisattva, middling-grade maturity belongs to bodhisattvas from the first through the seventh ground (spanning the second great incalculable eon), and — by clear implication — superior-grade maturity belongs to the eighth through tenth grounds.

He explains the middling-grade bodhisattva's own qualities in some detail: a genuinely deep and sincere disposition toward the three jewels and toward benefiting others, an extremely vigorous eagerness capable of producing vast results and great benefit (called "blazing"), and a settled irreversibility that never turns back or falls into regression, only ever advancing (called "unshakeable"). By contrast, he notes, the present resolute-practice bodhisattva, still at the lesser grade, possesses only correspondingly modest eagerness and effort, remains genuinely capable of turning back or regressing, and therefore lacks the profound, unshakeable faith the higher grade eventually produces — a further honest acknowledgment of exactly where this stage's own limitations lie. The treatise turns next to naming three specific circumstances in which this resolute-practice bodhisattva is especially prone to losing correct mindfulness altogether.

70. Section: Three Places Where Mindfulness Is Lost — Amid Sense Objects, Across Successive Rebirths, and Regarding What Was Once Learned

The treatise names three specific places where this bodhisattva loses correct mindfulness. First, amid pleasant and unpleasant sights, sounds, smells, tastes, textures, and mental objects, such a bodhisattva's mind may, at times, become distorted, correct mindfulness lost amid the pull of desire or aversion. Second, having taken rebirth in this or that body across countless past lives, such a bodhisattva, once reborn, forgets the previous life entirely. And third, regarding teachings once received and retained, long practiced and long expounded, such a bodhisattva may, at times, simply forget them. These, the treatise concludes, are the three places where this bodhisattva loses correct mindfulness.

Chinese Source

於其三處有忘失念。一、於境界可意、不可意色聲香味觸法中，或於一時其心顛倒，忘失正念；二、於受生彼彼身中，既受生已，忘失前生；三、於所受、所持諸法，久作久說，或於一時有所忘失。

於是三處有忘失念。

Ru-Yi Translation

There are three places where this bodhisattva loses correct mindfulness. First, amid pleasant and unpleasant sights, sounds, smells, tastes, textures, and mental objects, at times the mind becomes distorted, correct mindfulness lost. Second, having taken rebirth in this or that body, once reborn, the previous life is forgotten. Third, regarding teachings once received and retained, long practiced and long expounded, at times these are simply forgotten.

These are the three places where this bodhisattva loses correct mindfulness.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi illustrates the first place with the ordinary friction of communal life among beginning practitioners: still lacking sufficient concentration and wisdom to remain undisturbed by pleasant or unpleasant sense-contact, an ordinary bodhisattva's mind occasionally slips into the deluded misperceptions of permanence, pleasure, self, and purity, or into outright craving and aversion — much like potatoes tumbling together in a basket, inevitably knocking against one another, requiring real effort and renewed mindfulness before genuine harmony becomes possible.
