He illustrates the second with the simple, universal fact that beings, having wandered beginninglessly through the heavens, the human realm, and the three lower destinies, forget their own previous existence entirely upon each new rebirth — most people cannot recall even their immediately preceding life, let alone the one before that. And he illustrates the third with the mundane experience of simply forgetting material once studied and even once taught to others, since this stage's bodhisattva has not yet attained the boundless mnemonic retention that only a much later ground provides. He notes that all three places share a common root: a mindfulness not yet stabilized by direct realization remains vulnerable to disruption from present circumstance, from the sheer discontinuity of rebirth, and from simple the passage of time. The treatise turns next to describing this same bodhisattva's inconsistent intelligence and memory.

71. Section: Intelligence and Memory That Come and Go, and Teaching Others Only Through Strained, Uncertain Effort — Like Shooting an Arrow in the Dark

The treatise turns to a further limitation: at times such a bodhisattva possesses genuine intelligence, able to receive and retain the teaching and penetrate its meaning, while at other times this is simply not the case. At times such a bodhisattva possesses genuine retentive memory, while at other times forgetfulness prevails instead. And since such a bodhisattva has not yet genuinely, correctly known the skillful means for taming beings, nor genuinely, correctly known the skillful means for drawing forth the teaching within, whatever instruction or admonition is offered to others in teaching the true teaching proceeds only through strained, effortful exertion — and because it proceeds this way, it does not genuinely, correctly know its own effect, sometimes accomplishing nothing at all and sometimes accomplishing something after all, exactly like shooting an arrow in complete darkness, sometimes striking the target and sometimes missing, the outcome simply following whatever chance allows.

Chinese Source

或於一時具足聰慧，於其諸法能受、能持，於其義理堪能悟入，或於一時則不如是。

或於一時具足憶念，或於一時成忘念類。

於諸有情未能了知如實調伏善巧方便，於自佛法亦未了知如實引發善巧方便。為他說法教授教誡，勉勵而轉；勉勵轉故，不如實知，或時虛棄、或不虛棄。如闇中射，或中不中，隨欲成故。

《披》隨欲成故者：由彼意樂未清淨故，致有斯失應知。

Ru-Yi Translation

At times such a bodhisattva possesses genuine intelligence, able to receive and retain the teaching, capable of penetrating its meaning; at other times this is not the case.

At times such a bodhisattva possesses genuine retentive memory; at other times forgetfulness prevails instead.

Not yet genuinely, correctly knowing the skillful means for taming sentient beings, and not yet genuinely, correctly knowing the skillful means for drawing forth one's own buddha-teaching, such a bodhisattva, in instructing and admonishing others through teaching the true teaching, proceeds only through strained exertion; because it proceeds through strained exertion, it does not genuinely, correctly know its own effect — at times accomplishing nothing, at times not accomplishing nothing. It is like shooting an arrow in darkness, sometimes striking and sometimes missing, the result simply following whatever chance allows.

Commentary: Regarding "following whatever chance allows" — because such a bodhisattva's own aspiration is not yet pure, this is precisely why such a shortcoming occurs, one should understand.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi illustrates the fluctuating intelligence and memory with plainly relatable examples: sharp in youth yet forgetful by middle age, clear-headed when healthy yet dulled when unwell, lucid when free of affliction yet clouded when afflictions stir — the ordinary, uneven texture of an unenlightened mind's own capacities, rising and falling with circumstance rather than remaining stably reliable.

He dwells at some length on the vivid image of shooting an arrow in the dark: a resolute-practice bodhisattva who has not yet mastered the specific skillful means for taming particular beings, nor the skillful means for drawing forth the teaching's own deeper meaning within, still sincerely undertakes teaching others whenever the occasion arises — but does so only through effortful, self-exhorted determination rather than natural, confident skill, and consequently cannot actually tell in advance, or even reliably afterward, whether a given teaching has genuinely helped its intended listener. Sometimes the effort comes entirely to nothing, the words simply failing to connect, though even then, he notes, a distant seed for future awakening may still have been planted; sometimes, unexpectedly, the teaching lands well, teacher and listener both left genuinely delighted. He attributes this unpredictability, following the commentary's own explanation, directly to this bodhisattva's own aspiration not yet being fully purified — unable to see clearly enough to know in advance what will actually land, such a bodhisattva's teaching efforts necessarily remain something of a gamble. He adds that Maitreya's own candor here, describing beginning bodhisattvas with such unflinching honesty, itself deserves admiration — and that a sincere beginning bodhisattva should take this account not as discouragement but as simple encouragement to keep trying regardless, grateful for whatever causes and conditions have brought the opportunity to teach at all. The treatise turns next to naming six specific ways this bodhisattva's own attainment can still regress.

72. Section: Six Ways Attainment Can Still Regress — Abandoning Bodhicitta, Giving Back the Precepts, Wearying of Beings, Repeated Violation, Fearing the Great Vehicle, and Losing Great Compassion

The treatise closes this account of the abode of resolute practice's shortcomings by naming six specific ways such a bodhisattva's own attainment can still regress. First, though the aspiration for great awakening has already been aroused, at times it is abandoned and relinquished after all. Second, at times such a bodhisattva even discards the pure precept-discipline of a bodhisattva once received, no longer able to sustain it. Third, though diligently cultivating what benefits beings, such a bodhisattva may, partway through, grow weary and discouraged, abandoning that very work of benefiting beings — wishing, out of this same weariness, simply to secure one's own ease, and needing renewed, deliberate reflection before the wish to bring others ease can be rekindled. Fourth, such a bodhisattva mostly, but not completely, knows the various ways the bodhisattva precepts can be violated, and has not yet entirely and permanently eliminated their causes, which is why repeated violations still occur. Fifth, at times such a bodhisattva's own grasp of the bodhisattva canon's teaching and discipline is overtaken by rival forces, and at times, upon hearing the profound, vast teachings of the great vehicle, genuine terror arises, the mind wavering, hesitant, and doubtful. And sixth, such a bodhisattva remains far from displaying great compassion continuously toward all beings, able to wish only a small portion of beings immediate benefit and ease, not yet vast, not yet boundless. With this sixth item, the treatise concludes: these are the marks of cultivation belonging to a bodhisattva at the abode of resolute practice — narrow and incomplete, inconsistent, and still liable to regress.

Chinese Source

或於一時，於大菩提雖已發心，而復退捨。

或於一時，棄捨菩薩先所受學淨戒律儀，不能受學。

或於一時，雖勤修習利有情事，而於中間生厭倦故，復還棄捨利有情事。由意樂故，欲令自樂；由思擇故，欲令他樂。

於諸菩薩所有違犯，多分遍知，非數遍知、無餘永斷，由於毀犯數現行故。

或於一時，於菩薩藏法毗奈耶他所引奪。或於一時，聞說甚深廣大法教而生驚怖，其心搖動、猶豫、疑惑。

於諸有情，遠離一切現行大悲。於諸有情，少分現前利益安樂，未能廣大、未能無量。

Ru-Yi Translation

At times, though the aspiration for great awakening has already been aroused, it is abandoned and relinquished after all.

At times, such a bodhisattva even discards the pure precept-discipline of a bodhisattva already received, no longer able to sustain it.

At times, though diligently cultivating what benefits sentient beings, midway through, weariness and discouragement arise, and the work of benefiting beings is abandoned once again. Out of this aspiration, one wishes to secure one's own ease; only through deliberate reflection does one again wish to bring others ease.

With respect to every possible violation among the bodhisattva precepts, such a bodhisattva mostly, but not completely, knows them, and has not yet entirely and permanently eliminated them, which is why repeated violation still occurs.

At times, such a bodhisattva's own grasp of the bodhisattva canon's teaching and discipline is overtaken by rival forces. At times, upon hearing the profound, vast teachings expounded, genuine terror arises, the mind wavering, hesitant, and doubtful.

Such a bodhisattva remains far from all continuous display of great compassion toward sentient beings. Toward sentient beings, only a small portion can be wished immediate benefit and ease — not yet vast, not yet boundless.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats these six regressions as a sobering but necessary completion of the entire preceding portrait — precisely because a resolute-practice bodhisattva has not yet reached the noble grounds' own irreversibility, even the most fundamental commitments, bodhicitta itself and the bodhisattva precepts, remain genuinely at risk of being abandoned when a discouraging teacher or unwelcome circumstance is encountered. He explains the third regression as describing a particularly human pattern: sustained effort at benefiting others gradually wearing thin, tempting a practitioner to retreat into simply seeking personal comfort, and requiring conscious, renewed reflection each time to rekindle the original, other-directed aspiration rather than that aspiration sustaining itself automatically.

He explains the fourth regression as a matter of incomplete rather than absent understanding — such a bodhisattva knows most, but not all, of what actually constitutes a precept violation, and has not eliminated the underlying causes of violation permanently, so lapses continue to recur even with genuine sincerity. He explains the fifth regression as capturing two distinct vulnerabilities at once: losing one's own grasp of the bodhisattva canon's own teaching to competing, perhaps non-Buddhist, influences, and experiencing genuine fear and doubt upon first encountering the great vehicle's own most profound scriptures — the Avatamsaka, the Prajnaparamita, the great Nirvana Sutra — wondering, in a moment of hesitation, whether buddhahood is really attainable at all, or merely a beautiful but implausible story. And he explains the sixth regression by direct contrast with the vast, boundless compassion already described several milestones earlier under the seven aspects of compassion in chapter three — a resolute-practice bodhisattva's own compassion, however sincere, remains selective and limited in practice, extending readily to those immediately at hand while struggling to extend equally to beings distant, difficult, or hostile.

With all six regressions now named, he notes, the treatise's extended, remarkably candid portrait of the abode of resolute practice's own narrow scope, incomplete execution, inconsistency, and vulnerability to regression reaches its own complete conclusion — a portrait he treats as ultimately encouraging rather than discouraging, since naming these limitations honestly is precisely what allows a sincere practitioner to recognize them, work with them, and progress steadily beyond them toward the settled, irreversible accomplishment the first ground and beyond will eventually provide. The treatise turns next to a further sub-topic distinguishing this same abode's own gradations of training.

73. Section: Not Yet Fully Learned, Not Yet Fully Manifested, Feeling Far From Awakening — Closing the Full Account of This Abode's Conduct, State, and Mark

The treatise draws together everything already explained by referring it back to the treatise's own earlier chapters: within all the complete bodhisattva training already explained above, such a bodhisattva has not yet universally mastered it; within all the complete marks of a bodhisattva already explained above, such a bodhisattva has not yet accomplished them all; within all the complete applied efforts belonging to both divisions of bodhisattva already explained above, such a bodhisattva has not yet manifested them equally; and the excellent aspiration of a bodhisattva already explained above remains, for such a bodhisattva, still impure. Turning to a further, especially telling point, the treatise adds that such a bodhisattva regards unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening as something genuinely far away, not yet having cultivated a deep, firm excellent aspiration toward nirvana — remaining instead, for a correspondingly long time, caught in the circulation of birth-and-death, not yet having accomplished the blazing, unshakeable, wondrously excellent factors of awakening. With this, the treatise concludes: all of this together is what should be understood as the conduct, state, and mark characterizing a bodhisattva turning within the abode of resolute practice.

Chinese Source

於如上說一切圓滿菩薩學中，未能普學。於如上說一切圓滿菩薩諸相，未皆成就。於如上說一切圓滿二分菩薩正加行中，未等顯現。於如上說菩薩意樂，猶未清淨。

於其無上正等菩提，自謂為遠，未於涅槃增上意樂安立深固。如於生死長時流轉，於其熾然、無動、妙善菩提分法，未能成就。

如是等類，當知是名勝解行住菩薩轉時，諸行狀相。

《披》如於生死長時流轉等者：由彼菩薩猶往惡趣，是故說言長時流轉。如是熾然、無動、極善清淨覺品善法，當知一切皆未相應。如成熟品說。（陵本三十七卷二十六頁3093）此應準知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Within all the complete bodhisattva training already explained above, such a bodhisattva has not yet universally mastered it. Within all the complete marks of a bodhisattva already explained above, such a bodhisattva has not yet accomplished them all. Within all the complete applied efforts of both divisions of bodhisattva already explained above, such a bodhisattva has not yet manifested them equally. The excellent aspiration of a bodhisattva already explained above remains, for such a bodhisattva, not yet purified.

Toward unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening, such a bodhisattva regards it as far away, not yet having established a deep, firm excellent aspiration toward nirvana. Correspondingly caught in the long circulation of birth-and-death, such a bodhisattva has not yet accomplished the blazing, unshakeable, wondrously excellent factors of awakening.

All of this together, one should understand, is called the conduct, state, and mark of a bodhisattva turning within the abode of resolute practice.

Commentary: Regarding "correspondingly caught in the long circulation of birth-and-death" and the like — because such a bodhisattva may still go to an unfortunate rebirth, this is why it is said to be a long circulation. One should understand that such a bodhisattva has not yet become joined with any of these blazing, unshakeable, utterly pure factors of awakening whatsoever, exactly as explained in the Chapter on Maturation. This should be understood accordingly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this closing summary as a deliberate act of cross-referencing back across the entire treatise studied so far: the "complete bodhisattva training" refers to the eighteen chapters of the initial yoga foundation from the chapter on the lineage through the chapter on the qualities of a bodhisattva; the "complete marks" refers to chapter one of this very yoga foundation, the five true marks; the "complete applied efforts of both divisions" refers to chapter two's four dharmas for householder and renunciate alike; and the "excellent aspiration" refers to chapter three's seven aspects of compassion and fifteen kinds of aspiration — a resolute-practice bodhisattva, however sincere, has not yet fully mastered or purified any of this vast body of teaching and practice already explained.

He explains the especially poignant final point — that such a bodhisattva regards awakening as genuinely far away — as following directly from not yet having cultivated the deep, settled excellent aspiration toward nirvana that a first-ground bodhisattva possesses; the commentary's own explanation, that such a bodhisattva may still fall into an unfortunate rebirth, gives real weight to the phrase "long circulation," since the danger here is not merely metaphorical distance from awakening but literal continued vulnerability to the lower realms. With this comprehensive summary, he notes, the treatise's own extended answer to the opening question — what conduct, state, and mark characterizes a bodhisattva at the abode of resolute practice — reaches its full and complete conclusion, an unusually candid and thorough portrait matched by nothing else in the chapter so far. The treatise turns next to a further sub-topic, distinguishing three internal grades within this same abode of resolute practice, before comparing it directly against the abode of utmost joy.

74. Section: Three Grades Within Resolute Practice Itself, and the Seamless, Instantaneous Transition Into Utmost Joy

The treatise now distinguishes three grades within the abode of resolute practice itself: when such a bodhisattva turns at the stage of lower forbearance, all the shortcomings of conduct, state, and mark already explained above should be understood as present in their superior, most pronounced grade; when turning at the stage of middling forbearance, these same shortcomings should be understood as present in their middling grade; and when turning at the stage of superior forbearance, these same shortcomings should be understood as present in their inferior grade, their underlying nature having grown genuinely faint and thin. The treatise then explains the transition into the very next abode: precisely at this stage of superior forbearance, all the shortcomings of conduct, state, and mark already explained are gradually and completely severed without remainder, and from this point, without any interval whatsoever, one should understand that such a bodhisattva enters the abode of utmost joy.

Chinese Source

是諸菩薩勝解行住，下忍轉時，如上所說諸行狀相，當知上品；中忍轉時，如上所說諸行狀相，當知中品；上忍轉時，如上所說，當知下品，其性微薄。

即於如是上忍轉時，於上所說諸行狀相，漸次能令無餘永斷，從此無間，當知菩薩入極喜住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Bodhisattvas at the abode of resolute practice, when turning at the stage of lower forbearance, should be understood, with respect to the conduct, state, and mark already explained above, to be at the superior grade; when turning at the stage of middling forbearance, should be understood to be at the middling grade; when turning at the stage of superior forbearance, should be understood to be at the inferior grade, their underlying nature having grown faint and thin.

Precisely at this stage of superior forbearance, with respect to the conduct, state, and mark already explained above, these are gradually, completely severed without remainder, and from this point, without any interval, one should understand that such a bodhisattva enters the abode of utmost joy.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the counter-intuitive naming here carefully: "lower forbearance" refers to the earliest phase of the applied-effort stage, where the shortcomings already catalogued remain at their most pronounced, "superior" grade of severity, while "superior forbearance," the very threshold of the path of seeing, corresponds to the "inferior," most attenuated grade of these same shortcomings — the terminology tracks the stage of practice, not the severity of the shortcomings, which move in the opposite direction as practice deepens. He notes that this threefold grading, corresponding to the classical stages of warmth, summit, forbearance, and supreme mundane dharma, gives concrete texture to what might otherwise seem an undifferentiated stretch of practice, showing that even within a single abode, real, gradual progress is continuously occurring.

He treats the transition described next — a single, uninterrupted instant carrying a bodhisattva from the very peak of superior forbearance directly into the abode of utmost joy, every remaining shortcoming severed without remainder in that same moment — as a striking parallel to the sudden, instantaneous transition into buddhahood itself already described several milestones earlier: just as the tenth ground's own transition into buddhahood happens without interval, so too does the applied-effort stage's own transition into the first ground, the entire path apparently punctuated by these decisive, instantaneous leaps at its two most significant thresholds, gradual cultivation on either side of each leap converging into a single moment of genuine transformation. The treatise turns next to explaining precisely what distinguishes the purity of the first ground's own aspiration from anything the resolute-practice stage, even at its most refined, could ever produce.

75. Section: Defiled Versus Pure, and Why Resolute Practice Still Cannot Be Called Excellent Aspiration — Closing the Abode of Resolute Practice Entirely

The treatise now draws a second contrast: whereas everything explained under the abode of resolute practice belongs entirely to defiled experience, a first-ground bodhisattva who has attained the skillful means of utmost joy finds none of these shortcomings present at all — instead every contrary wholesome quality manifests fully, and by accomplishing these, such a bodhisattva turns and comes to be called one of pure, excellent aspiration. Turning to a third, closely related contrast, the treatise distinguishes resolute practice from excellent aspiration itself: even though a resolute-practice bodhisattva may possess a small portion of gradually purifying resolute understanding at the lesser, middling, or superior grade, this still cannot be called excellent aspiration, because such resolute understanding continues to turn defiled by numerous secondary afflictions — named in the chapter on the lineage as laxity, wrong view, distraction, and constraint — whereas a bodhisattva abiding in utmost joy has permanently and forever cut off every one of these same secondary afflictions, and turns with resolute understanding that is pure, free of any secondary affliction whatsoever. With this contrast fully drawn, the entire eleven-part analysis's first part — the abode of resolute practice — reaches its complete conclusion.

Chinese Source

由得方便極喜住中，勝解行住所說諸法皆無所有，與彼相違所有一切白品諸法皆悉顯現。由諸菩薩成就此故，轉得名為淨勝意樂。

勝解行住菩薩轉時，雖有少分軟中上品方便展轉清淨勝解，而未得名淨勝意樂。何以故？由此勝解為彼多種諸隨煩惱染汙而轉。

極歡喜住菩薩住時，一切勝解諸隨煩惱皆悉永斷，離隨煩惱淨勝解轉。

《披》為彼多種諸隨煩惱染汙而轉者：種性品說：有四隨煩惱與白法相違，是名多種諸隨煩惱。若具不具，為所染汙，名染汙轉。四隨煩惱如彼條列應知。（陵本三十五卷七頁2845）

Ru-Yi Translation

Because a bodhisattva has attained the skillful means present within the abode of utmost joy, none of the dharmas already explained under the abode of resolute practice are present at all — instead, every contrary wholesome quality manifests fully. Because such bodhisattvas accomplish this, they turn and come to be called ones of pure, excellent aspiration.

When a bodhisattva at the abode of resolute practice turns, although there is a small portion of gradually purifying resolute understanding at the lesser, middling, or superior grade, this still cannot be called pure, excellent aspiration. Why is this? Because this resolute understanding turns defiled by numerous secondary afflictions.

When a bodhisattva abiding in the abode of utmost joy turns, all secondary afflictions connected with resolute understanding are permanently and forever cut off, and, free of secondary affliction, one turns with pure resolute understanding.

Commentary: Regarding "turns defiled by numerous secondary afflictions" — the chapter on the lineage explains: there are four secondary afflictions contrary to wholesome dharmas, called the numerous secondary afflictions. Whether complete or incomplete, being defiled by them is called turning defiled. The four secondary afflictions should be understood exactly as listed there (fascicle 35, page 2845 of the Nanjing edition).

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi identifies the four secondary afflictions the commentary cross-references from the chapter on the lineage, chapter 35: laxity, where a resolute-practice bodhisattva, carrying forward past habituations of affliction and indulging them further, generates intense, prolonged afflictive states; wrong view, where a foolish, unskillful bodhisattva, lacking discernment, attaches to bad companions and non-Buddhist teachers, absorbing mistaken views that cause retreat from the bodhisattva path; distraction, where a bodhisattva constrained and pressed upon by superiors, a spouse, a king, bandits, or enemies loses bodily and mental freedom, the mind's discerning power growing dull and confused; and constraint, where a bodhisattva, impoverished and lacking life's basic necessities, becomes so bound up in anxious toil over life and wealth that the bodhisattva lineage itself cannot manifest. He notes that a first-ground bodhisattva has cut off every one of these four permanently, which is precisely why resolute understanding there turns pure rather than defiled.

He treats this entire passage — first contrasting defiled resolute practice against pure excellent aspiration, then precisely explaining why even refined resolute understanding still falls short of that name — as the treatise's own final, most exacting word on the abode of resolute practice, closing out an unusually extended and candid eleven-part-analysis opening section that began several milestones earlier with the roadmap of transitions across all thirteen abodes. Having now fully closed this first of the eleven granular parts, he notes, the treatise turns immediately to the second part: the abode of utmost joy itself, opened by a direct question asking what conduct, state, and mark characterizes a bodhisattva there.

76. Section: The Abode of Utmost Joy Opens — An Eightfold Question, and the First Mark of Well-Determined Vow

The treatise now opens the second of its eleven granular parts, turning from the abode of resolute practice to the abode of utmost joy itself. Following the same question-and-answer structure used throughout, the treatise asks: when a bodhisattva at the abode of utmost joy turns, what conduct, what state, what mark should be understood? The answer begins by first drawing a contrast with the immediately preceding abode: before entering utmost joy, a bodhisattva's own great vow toward unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening had not yet skillfully penetrated awakening's own intrinsic nature, nor skillfully penetrated awakening's own skillful means, and so, mostly following conditions imposed by others, remained not yet well determined. Having now abandoned this shortcoming entirely upon entering utmost joy, such a bodhisattva gives rise to six distinguishing marks of a newly well-determined vow, one that is inwardly realized as cultivated nature and constitutes the bodhisattva's own great vow.

Chinese Source

辰二、極歡喜住　巳一、問

問：極歡喜住菩薩轉時，應知何行何狀何相？

巳二、答　午一、略說　未一、別辨相　申一、善決定願　酉一、簡前住

答：若諸菩薩，從勝解行住入極歡喜住，先於無上正等菩提菩薩弘願，未善通達菩提自性，未善通達菩提方便，多分隨順他緣而轉，不善決定。

酉二、顯六相　戌一、初三相攝

除捨彼故，發起六相新善決定、內證修性菩薩大願。

Ru-Yi Translation

Question: When a bodhisattva at the abode of utmost joy turns, what conduct, what state, what mark should be understood?

Answer: If bodhisattvas, entering the abode of utmost joy from the abode of resolute practice, had previously — regarding their bodhisattva's great vow toward unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening — not yet skillfully penetrated awakening's own intrinsic nature, nor skillfully penetrated awakening's own skillful means, mostly following conditions imposed by others, not yet well determined;

because this shortcoming is now abandoned, six marks arise: a newly well-determined vow, inwardly realized as cultivated nature, constituting the bodhisattva's great vow.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi reads this opening as deliberately mirroring the treatise's own earlier account of resolute practice's shortcomings — a bodhisattva there had not yet skillfully penetrated either awakening's intrinsic nature (identified via the Discourse Record as the twofold severance of the two hindrances together with the twofold wisdom severing them) or its skillful means (the fifteen items of the factors of awakening already catalogued in fascicle 45, from conscientiousness through the four dharma-summaries), and so remained largely at the mercy of external conditions rather than internally settled. He treats the six marks now introduced — new well-determination, inwardly realized cultivated nature, and the bodhisattva's great vow constituting the first three, with the remaining three named a few lines later as peerless, uncommon, and unchanging-inexhaustible — as together cashing out precisely what "well-determined" means for a first-ground bodhisattva, each mark corresponding to one of the six well-determinations praised in Vasubandhu's Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra.

He notes the technical point that this abode is explicitly said to no longer belong to the resolute-practice stage at all — the moment a bodhisattva enters utmost joy via realizing the nature of dharmas directly, the bodhisattva has genuinely crossed from the ordinary, "believing and understanding" stage into the noble path itself, never to return to resolute practice's own uncertain, other-directed condition. The treatise turns next to explaining these six marks one by one, grouped first as an initial three.

77. Section: The First Three of Six Marks — New Well-Determination, Inwardly Realized Cultivated Nature, and the Bodhisattva's Great Vow

The treatise now explains the first three of the six marks distinguishing a first-ground bodhisattva's well-determined vow, each mapped onto one of the six well-determinations praised in Vasubandhu's Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra. New well-determination corresponds to the first, well-determined in view: a first-ground bodhisattva's newly attained non-discriminating wisdom observes that all dharmas are decisively suchness-emptiness, self and dharmas both empty in a single undifferentiated flavor, the observing wisdom itself free of discrimination and unmixed with ignorance. Inwardly realized cultivated nature corresponds to the second, well-determined in truth: this same wisdom internally realizes the ultimate truth of dharma-nature directly, a world-transcending object no longer within the reach of worldly, deluded discrimination. And the bodhisattva's great vow corresponds to the third, well-determined in excellence: the suchness such wisdom observes is itself the great dharma-realm, the very root from which every buddha's own buddhahood is accomplished, pervading all dharmas without limit or boundary — and the bodhisattva's own vow-power, sharing this same nature, likewise pervades the entire dharma-realm without limit.

Chinese Source

《披》發起六相新善決定內證修性菩薩大願者：於此住中，由證法性發菩提心，是名內證修性。菩薩大願初無漏生真實智攝，是名新善決定。齊此名為已入初地，非勝解行地攝故。

十地經論說：此善決定有六種。一者、觀相善決定。真如觀一味相故，如經無雜故。二者、真實善決定。非一切世間境界，出世間故，如經不可見故。三者、勝善決定。大法界故，一切佛根本故，如經廣大如法界故。

四者、因善決定。有二種。一、成無常愛果因善決定。是因如虛空，依是生諸色，色不盡故，如經究竟如虛空故。二、常果因善決定。得涅槃道，如經盡未來際故。五者、大善決定。隨順作利益他行，如經覆護一切眾生界故。六者、不怯弱善決定。入一切諸佛智地不怯弱故，如經佛子是諸菩薩乃至入現在諸佛智地故。此說六相，如彼應釋。又復此中，新善決定，當知即彼觀相善決定攝；內證修性，當知即彼真實善決定攝；菩薩大願，當知即彼勝善決定攝。如所說義，配釋可了。

Ru-Yi Translation

Commentary: Regarding "six marks arise — a newly well-determined vow, inwardly realized as cultivated nature, constituting the bodhisattva's great vow" — within this abode, because bodhicitta is aroused through realizing dharma-nature directly, this is called inwardly realized cultivated nature. The bodhisattva's great vow is subsumed within the newly arisen undefiled, true wisdom, and this is called new well-determination. Precisely at this point one is called having already entered the first ground, no longer subsumed within the stage of resolute practice.

The Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra explains: this well-determination has six kinds. First, well-determined in view — because the observation of suchness is of a single flavor, as the sutra says, "without admixture." Second, well-determined in truth — because it is not any worldly object at all, being world-transcending, as the sutra says, "cannot be seen." Third, well-determined in excellence — because it is the great dharma-realm, the root of all buddhas, as the sutra says, "vast as the dharma-realm."

Fourth, well-determined in cause, of two kinds: first, well-determined in the cause that accomplishes the impermanent, cherished fruit — this cause is like empty space, and, relying on it, forms are generated, the forms never exhausted, as the sutra says, "ultimate, like empty space." Second, well-determined in the cause of the constant fruit — attaining the path of nirvana, as the sutra says, "to the very end of future time." Fifth, well-determined in greatness — according with conduct that benefits others, as the sutra says, "shelters and protects the entire realm of sentient beings." Sixth, well-determined in fearlessness — because one enters the ground of all buddhas' wisdom without timidity, as the sutra says, "these bodhisattvas, sons of the buddhas, even now enter the present buddhas' own ground of wisdom." These six marks should be explained exactly as there. Furthermore, within this passage, new well-determination should be understood as subsumed within well-determination in view there; inwardly realized cultivated nature should be understood as subsumed within well-determination in truth there; the bodhisattva's great vow should be understood as subsumed within well-determination in excellence there. Matching the meanings explained, this may be understood.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through Vasubandhu's own six well-determinations one at a time before mapping them back onto this treatise's three (and later, three more) marks: well-determined in view praises a first-ground bodhisattva's non-discriminating wisdom for observing that every dharma is decisively empty of both self and dharmas, one undifferentiated flavor, the observing wisdom itself unmixed with ignorance; well-determined in truth praises this same wisdom as genuinely world-transcending, its object neither seen nor graspable by any worldly, discriminating mind, since both what is observed and the observing wisdom itself are empty; and well-determined in excellence praises the observed suchness as the "great dharma-realm" — dharma-realm being simply another name for suchness, the emptiness that is the very possibility-condition for all dharmas whatsoever, and hence the root from which every buddha's own buddhahood is ultimately accomplished, vast and boundless exactly as the bodhisattva's own vow-power, sharing this same nature, is itself vast and boundless.

He notes the significance of the closing line "precisely at this point one is called having already entered the first ground, no longer subsumed within the stage of resolute practice" as the commentary's own explicit confirmation of what was already implied — this is not a matter of degree but a genuine crossing, the ordinary bodhisattva's believing-and-understanding stage left behind entirely. Having now mapped the first three marks onto the first three well-determinations, he notes, the treatise turns next to the remaining three marks — peerless, uncommon, and unchanging-inexhaustible — mapped onto the remaining well-determinations in cause, greatness, and fearlessness.

78. Section: The Remaining Three Marks — Peerless, Uncommon, Unchanging-Inexhaustible — Closing the Well-Determined Vow Entirely

The treatise completes its account of the six marks. The fourth and fifth marks, peerless and uncommon, are explained together: bodhisattvas who have not yet realized dharma-nature directly still cultivate the six perfections as their own kind of wholesome vow, and though these produce worldly, cherished fruits such as great wealth or noble birth, a first-ground bodhisattva's vow born of realizing dharma-nature directly surpasses every such worldly object entirely — this is called peerless — and because such a bodhisattva vows to follow and rescue every sentient being from suffering throughout birth-and-death, uncommon to any hearer or solitary-realizer who has not made this same vow, this is called uncommon. Even arising for a single instant, this vow of realizing dharma-nature directly is such that its own nature spontaneously secures the bodhisattva immeasurable, cherished wholesome fruits. The sixth mark, unchanging and inexhaustible, is explained next: because this great vow accords with the constant fruit of attaining the path of nirvana lasting to the very end of future time, its own nature can never be caused to regress, alter, or differ by any other condition whatsoever — and being of this excellent quality, such a bodhisattva is assured, without any timidity, of eventually reaching the furthest boundary, the greatest awakening itself. With this sixth mark named, the treatise closes by noting that this entire well-determined vow may also simply be called the arising of bodhicitta — completing the first of the eight aspects distinguishing a first-ground bodhisattva.

Chinese Source

戌二、次二相攝　超過一切餘白淨願；無等不共，果是世間，超過一切世間境界；隨救一切有情苦故，不共一切聲聞、獨覺；雖一剎那生起此願，法性自爾能得菩薩無量白法可愛之果；

戌三、後一相攝　又此大願無變無盡自性得已，無異因緣令其退轉、變異可得；又是勝分，墮後邊際極大菩提。

酉三、結異名　如此菩薩善決定願，亦名發心。

Ru-Yi Translation

This vow of realizing dharma-nature directly surpasses every other wholesome, pure vow. Peerless and uncommon: its fruit, though worldly, surpasses every worldly object entirely; because one vows to follow and rescue every sentient being's suffering, it is uncommon to any hearer or solitary-realizer. Even arising for a single instant, this vow, by its own nature, spontaneously secures the bodhisattva immeasurable, cherished wholesome fruits.

Furthermore, once this great vow's unchanging, inexhaustible own-nature is attained, no other cause or condition whatsoever can bring about its regression or alteration. Furthermore, this is the excellent portion, assured of eventually reaching the furthest boundary, the greatest awakening.

Such a bodhisattva's well-determined vow may also be called the arising of bodhicitta.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi maps these final three marks precisely: peerless and uncommon both belong to the fourth well-determination, in cause — specifically its first branch, well-determination in the cause accomplishing the impermanent, cherished fruit — since ordinary beings' worldly fruit from the six perfections can never equal a first-ground bodhisattva's own attainment (hence peerless), and a first-ground bodhisattva's vow to remain within birth-and-death rescuing beings is uncommon to hearers and solitary-realizers who seek only their own final nirvana (hence uncommon, mapped instead to the fifth well-determination, greatness, according with conduct that benefits others). Unchanging-inexhaustible belongs to the fourth well-determination's second branch, the cause of the constant fruit — nirvana itself being unconditioned, without arising, abiding, changing, or ceasing, and therefore constant — while the closing note about reaching the furthest boundary without timidity belongs to the sixth well-determination, fearlessness, a first-ground bodhisattva already possessing unshakeable confidence that buddhahood will eventually be reached.

He treats the closing identification — this entire well-determined vow simply being another name for the arising of bodhicitta — as tying off the first of eight aspects distinguishing the abode of utmost joy, all six marks together constituting nothing other than what it concretely means for a first-ground bodhisattva's aspiration to be genuinely, irreversibly settled. The treatise turns next to the second of the eight aspects: a fourfold analysis of this same arising of bodhicitta, asking what marks it, what object it takes, what state and nature it arises with, and what benefit it brings.

79. Section: The Second Aspect Opens — A Fourfold Analysis of Arising Bodhicitta, Beginning With Its Cause

The treatise now opens the second of the eight aspects distinguishing a first-ground bodhisattva: a fourfold analysis of the very arising of bodhicitta already named at the close of the previous aspect. Four questions are posed: first, what mark does a bodhisattva's arising bodhicitta bear; second, what object does arising bodhicitta take as its concern; third, what state, what mark, and from what intrinsic nature does arising bodhicitta arise; and fourth, what benefit does arising bodhicitta bring? Turning to answer the first question — the cause-aspect of arising bodhicitta — the treatise explains simply: such bodhisattvas, while still at the abode of resolute practice, have already skillfully accumulated every kind of wholesome root, and have already definitively transcended ordinary bodhisattva-stage practice; this, in brief, is the mark of a bodhisattva's arising bodhicitta.

Chinese Source

申二、四相發心　酉一、標　又即如是菩薩發心，略有四相應當了知。

酉二、徵　何等為四？

酉三、釋　戌一、列相　一者、何相菩薩發心？二者、發心何所緣慮？三者、發心何狀、何相、何自性起？四者、發心有何勝利？由此四相，應當了知菩薩發心。

戌二、辨義　亥一、因相　謂諸菩薩勝解行住，已善積集一切善根，於菩薩行已正超出。略說是相菩薩發心。

《披》一切善根等者：十地經論中，略以十句分別依何身生如是心，即此何相菩薩發心。彼說厚集善根為初，現大慈悲為後。十句差別中，初句是總，即此已善積集一切善根。餘句是別，即此於菩薩行已正超出。言超出者，謂能任性成辦所作，非如勝解行住，以分別慧數數思擇，方能修作故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, such a bodhisattva's own arising bodhicitta, in brief, has four marks that should be understood.

What are the four?

First, what mark does a bodhisattva's arising bodhicitta bear? Second, what object does arising bodhicitta take as its concern? Third, what state, what mark, and from what intrinsic nature does arising bodhicitta arise? Fourth, what benefit does arising bodhicitta bring? By these four marks, a bodhisattva's arising bodhicitta should be understood.

Namely, such bodhisattvas, at the abode of resolute practice, have already skillfully accumulated every wholesome root, and have already definitively transcended bodhisattva-stage practice. This, in brief, is the mark of a bodhisattva's arising bodhicitta.

Commentary: Regarding "every wholesome root" and the like — the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra, in brief, distinguishes with ten phrases what body one relies upon to give rise to such a mind, namely this text's own "what mark does a bodhisattva's arising bodhicitta bear." That text places thickly accumulated wholesome roots first and manifesting great compassion last. Among the ten distinct phrases, the first phrase is the general statement, namely this text's own "already skillfully accumulated every wholesome root"; the remaining phrases are the particular statements, namely this text's own "already definitively transcended bodhisattva-stage practice." The word "transcended" means being able to accomplish one's undertakings by simply according with one's own nature, unlike the abode of resolute practice, where only through repeated discriminating deliberation could one manage to cultivate and act at all.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi highlights the ten phrases the commentary cross-references from the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra, quoting the Ten Grounds Sutra itself: thickly accumulating wholesome roots; well accumulating wholesome conduct; well accumulating states of meditative absorption; well making offerings to the buddhas; well accumulating pure, wholesome dharmas; being well protected by good spiritual friends; a well-purified mind; entering a profound, expansive mind; delighting in the great dharma and earnestly seeking the buddhas' own wisdom; and manifesting great compassion — placed first and last respectively. He explains that this treatise deliberately compresses all ten into just two: "already skillfully accumulated every wholesome root" as the general summary standing for the first phrase, and "already definitively transcended bodhisattva-stage practice" as standing for all nine remaining particular phrases together.

He draws out the meaning of "transcended" carefully: a resolute-practice bodhisattva can only act skillfully through effortful, repeated discriminating deliberation each time, weighing and choosing before managing to cultivate at all, whereas a first-ground bodhisattva, having already thickly accumulated every wholesome root across the entire resolute-practice stage, now accomplishes the same undertakings simply and spontaneously, according with one's own already-matured nature rather than through effortful calculation — a first concrete illustration of the difference between effortful and spontaneous practice that the treatise will return to repeatedly across the remaining grounds. Having now answered the first of the four questions — the cause of arising bodhicitta — he notes, the treatise turns next to the second question: what object arising bodhicitta takes as its own concern.

80. Section: The Second Aspect — What Object Arising Bodhicitta Takes as Its Concern

The treatise now answers the second of the four questions about arising bodhicitta: what object does it take as its own concern? Four things are named. First, without error and swiftly, the complete fulfillment of every provision for awakening in the world to come — the cause of self-benefit. Second, the complete fulfillment of every bodhisattva's own task of benefiting sentient beings — the cause of benefiting others. Third, the complete fulfillment of unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening's every kind, every buddha-dharma — the fruit of self-benefit, glossed by the commentary as the buddhas' own wisdom of every kind, fully and equally awakened to whatever brings about a gathering of benefit, a gathering of no benefit, or a gathering that is neither. And fourth, the complete fulfillment of the buddhas' own undertakings — the fruit of benefiting others. In brief, a bodhisattva takes exactly these as the concern giving rise to bodhicitta.

Chinese Source

亥二、緣慮　又諸菩薩，緣當來世無倒速疾一切菩提資糧圓滿，一切菩薩利有情事圓滿，無上正等菩提一切種一切佛法圓滿，諸佛所作事業圓滿。略說緣慮如是發心。

《披》一切種一切佛法圓滿者：此說如來一切種妙智。謂於能引有義聚法、能引無義聚法、能引非有義非無義聚法，皆現等正覺故。如是三聚，名一切種。即於其中所有諸法，說名一切佛法應知。

《披》諸佛所作事業圓滿者：十地經論中，略以十句分別為何義生如是心，即此略說緣慮如是發心一分所攝。彼說十句，為得佛智為初，乃至為得轉大法輪無畏為後。於中佛智者，謂無上智，知斷證修，故此佛智有九種業差別。為求彼故，生如是心，乃至廣說。當知佛智，即此前說無上正等菩提一切種一切佛法圓滿。九業差別，即此所說諸佛所作事業圓滿。

Ru-Yi Translation

The second aspect: what object arising bodhicitta takes as concern. Furthermore, bodhisattvas take as their concern: the complete fulfillment, without error and swiftly, of every provision for awakening in the world to come; the complete fulfillment of every bodhisattva's own task of benefiting sentient beings; the complete fulfillment of unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening's every kind, every buddha-dharma; the complete fulfillment of the buddhas' own undertakings. In brief, it is by taking these as concern that bodhicitta arises in this way.

Commentary: Regarding "complete fulfillment of every kind, every buddha-dharma" — this speaks of the Tathāgata's own wondrous wisdom of every kind. Namely, being fully and equally awakened to whatever brings about a gathering of dharmas of benefit, a gathering of dharmas without benefit, and a gathering of dharmas neither of benefit nor without benefit. These three gatherings are called "every kind." All the dharmas contained within them, one should understand, are called every buddha-dharma.

Commentary: Regarding "complete fulfillment of the buddhas' own undertakings" — the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra, in brief, distinguishes with ten phrases for what meaning such a mind arises, namely this text's own brief statement of taking these as concern, giving rise to bodhicitta in this way. That text places "in order to attain the buddhas' own wisdom" first, down to "in order to attain fearlessness in turning the great dharma-wheel" last. Within this, the buddhas' own wisdom means unsurpassed wisdom — knowing suffering, severing its origin, realizing cessation, cultivating the path — and so this buddha-wisdom has nine distinct undertakings. Seeking this, such a mind arises, and so on at length. One should understand that the buddhas' own wisdom is exactly what was said before, the complete fulfillment of unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening's every kind, every buddha-dharma; the nine distinct undertakings are exactly what is said here, the complete fulfillment of the buddhas' own undertakings.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi organizes the four objects of concern into a clean grid mirroring the earlier well-determinations: fulfillment of awakening's provisions is the cause of self-benefit, fulfillment of benefiting sentient beings is the cause of benefiting others, fulfillment of every buddha-dharma is the fruit of self-benefit, and fulfillment of the buddhas' own undertakings is the fruit of benefiting others — a bodhisattva's arising bodhicitta takes concern with nothing other than this fourfold cause-and-fruit of self- and other-benefit together, in sharp contrast to an ordinary being's mind, which takes greed, hatred, and delusion as its habitual concern. He walks through the commentary's own quoted ten phrases from the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra in full: in order to attain the buddhas' own wisdom; in order to attain the ten powers; in order to attain great fearlessness; in order to attain the buddhas' own impartial dharma; in order to rescue the entire world; in order to purify great compassion; in order to attain unremainder-wisdom in all ten directions; in order to attain unobstructed, pure wisdom regarding every world-system; in order to attain awakening to the affairs of all three times within a single instant; and in order to attain fearlessness in turning the great dharma-wheel.

He notes that this treatise's own compressed pair — fulfilling every buddha-dharma, and fulfilling the buddhas' own undertakings — stand respectively for the first of these ten phrases (the buddhas' wisdom itself) and for the nine distinct undertakings that wisdom performs (phrases two through ten), a first-ground bodhisattva's own arising bodhicitta already oriented, from its very first instant, toward exactly this ultimate object. Having now answered the second question — what object bodhicitta's concern takes — he notes, the treatise turns next to the third question: what state, mark, and intrinsic nature does bodhicitta arise with.

81. Section: The Third Aspect — the State, Mark, and Nature From Which Bodhicitta Arises

The treatise now answers the third question: what state, mark, and intrinsic nature does arising bodhicitta bear? Four phrases are given, mapped in the commentary onto ten phrases from the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra, which places great compassion first as the intrinsic nature and nine further qualities of greatness as the resulting states and marks. Without error and swiftly giving rise to every provision for awakening corresponds to increase-greatness, wisdom's own heightened strength. According with all sentient beings in every bodhisattva undertaking corresponds to four qualities together — inclusion-greatness, thoroughness-greatness, immeasurability-greatness, and certainty-greatness — the skillful means, straightforward and profound sincerity, and power of certainly determining sentient beings' own faculties that make genuine benefit possible. According with attaining unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening's own teacherless, spontaneous, wondrous wisdom corresponds to accordance-greatness, the capacity to awaken without a teacher in a future life. And according with the buddhas' own undertakings pervading every kind, according with the vast, resolved mind, corresponds together to reception-greatness, most-wondrous-greatness, and abiding-exhaustion-greatness — receiving all buddha-dharma to teach and transform beings, and a compassion as vast as the dharma-realm itself, lasting to the very end of future time, ultimate as empty space.

Chinese Source

亥三、狀相　又諸菩薩，無倒速疾發起一切菩提資糧，隨順於諸有情一切菩薩所作，隨順獲得無上正等菩提無師自然妙智，隨順遍一切種諸佛所作事業，隨順廣大願心。

《披》無倒速疾發起一切菩提資糧等者：此釋前說發心何狀、何相、何自性起。十地經論中，略以十句分別以何因生如是心，即此所顯。彼說十句，以大悲為首，乃至究竟如虛空盡未來際為後。由是當知，大悲為首，即是發心生起自性；所餘諸句，皆其狀相。彼說智慧增上，即此無倒速疾發起一切菩提資糧。彼說方便善巧所攝、直心深心淳至、如來力無量、善決定眾生力智力，即此隨順於諸有情一切菩薩所作。彼說隨順自然智，即此隨順獲得無上正等菩提無師自然妙智。彼說能受一切佛法以智慧教化，即此隨順遍一切種諸佛所作事業。彼說廣大如法界、究竟如虛空盡未來際，即此隨順廣大願心。

Ru-Yi Translation

The third aspect: the state, mark, and nature from which arising bodhicitta arises. Furthermore, bodhisattvas, without error and swiftly, give rise to every provision for awakening; according with sentient beings, they undertake every bodhisattva task; according with attaining unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening, they gain teacherless, spontaneous, wondrous wisdom; according with pervading every kind of the buddhas' own undertakings, they according with the vast, resolved mind.

Commentary: Regarding "without error and swiftly give rise to every provision for awakening" and the like — this explains the earlier question, what state, what mark, and from what intrinsic nature does arising bodhicitta arise. The Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra, in brief, distinguishes with ten phrases by what cause such a mind arises, namely what is displayed here. That text places great compassion first, down to "ultimate as empty space, to the very end of future time" last. From this one should understand: great compassion placed first is exactly the intrinsic nature from which arising bodhicitta arises; all the remaining phrases are its state and mark. That text's "wisdom's own heightened strength" is exactly this text's "without error and swiftly give rise to every provision for awakening." That text's "being subsumed within skillful means, a straightforward mind and profound mind both genuinely sincere, the Tathāgata's own immeasurable power, and the power of wisdom that well determines sentient beings" is exactly this text's "according with sentient beings, undertaking every bodhisattva task." That text's "according with spontaneous wisdom" is exactly this text's "according with attaining unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening's teacherless, spontaneous, wondrous wisdom." That text's "being able to receive all buddha-dharma, teaching and transforming through wisdom" is exactly this text's "according with pervading every kind of the buddhas' own undertakings." That text's "vast as the dharma-realm, ultimate as empty space, to the very end of future time" is exactly this text's "according with the vast, resolved mind."

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi lays out the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra's own ten-part list of greatness qualities of compassion in full: great compassion as the head; then increase-greatness, inclusion-greatness, thoroughness-greatness, immeasurability-greatness, certainty-greatness, accordance-greatness, reception-greatness, most-wondrous-greatness, and abiding-exhaustion-greatness — nine qualities in total following the head, ten phrases altogether. He treats great compassion placed first as the commentary's own explicit answer to "what intrinsic nature does bodhicitta arise from" — every subsequent quality is simply compassion's own state and mark unfolding, compassion itself being the root reason a bodhisattva wishes to benefit sentient beings at all.

He walks through the mapping carefully: increase-greatness (heightened wisdom) matches swiftly fulfilling awakening's provisions; the middle cluster of inclusion, thoroughness, immeasurability, and certainty-greatness — needing skillful means plus a straightforward mind and profound mind free of any grasping at accomplishment, further reinforced by the Tathāgata's own immeasurable powers such as the ten powers and four fearlessnesses, together with wisdom that can certainly determine each sentient being's own faculties — together match according with every bodhisattva task for the sake of sentient beings; accordance-greatness (spontaneous wisdom, awakening without a teacher in a future life) matches the teacherless, spontaneous wisdom of unsurpassed awakening; and the final cluster of reception, most-wondrous, and abiding-exhaustion-greatness — receiving all buddha-dharma to teach with, and a compassion vast as the dharma-realm lasting to the very end of future time, ultimate as empty space — together match according with the buddhas' own undertakings and the vast, resolved mind. Having now answered the third question, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fourth and final question: what benefit does arising bodhicitta bring.

82. Section: The Fourth Aspect Opens — Surpassing the Ordinary Bodhisattva Stage Through Eight Kinds of Transcendence

The treatise now turns to the fourth and final question about arising bodhicitta: what benefit does it bring? The first benefit is surpassing the stage of an ordinary, unenlightened bodhisattva entirely. Having given rise to this mind, such a bodhisattva surpasses the bodhisattva stage of an ordinary being, is certified into the bodhisattva's own true nature free of afflictive birth, is born into the Tathāgata's own family, becomes a genuine child of the buddhas, is assured of proceeding toward correct, complete awakening, and is assured of carrying forward the Tathāgata's own noble lineage. The commentary maps this onto five of the eight kinds of transcendence named in the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra — entering one's rank, transcending one's family, transcending one's lineage, transcending the path, and ultimate transcendence — while noting that three further kinds, transcending the dharma-body, transcending one's sphere, and transcending karmic action, are here left unexplained.

Chinese Source

亥四、勝利　天一、超過凡地　又諸菩薩發是心已，超過菩薩凡異生地，證入菩薩正性離生，生如來家，成佛真子，決定趣向正等菩提，決定紹繼如來聖種。

《披》超過菩薩凡異生地等者：自下釋前發心有何勝利。十地經論中，略以種種分別心生時相，即此所顯。謂過凡夫地是總，即此超過菩薩凡異生地。過有八種是別，謂入位過為初，畢定過為後。彼云：一者、入位過，初成出世間心，如始住胎相似法故，如經入菩薩位故。即此證入菩薩正性離生。彼云：二者、家過，家生相似法故，如經生在佛家故。即此生如來家。彼云：三者、種姓過，子相似法大乘行生故，如經種姓尊貴無可譏嫌故。即此成佛真子。彼云：四者、道過，世間出世間道不攝攝故，異道生相似法故，如經過一切世間道入出世間道故。即此決定趣向正等菩提。彼云：八者、畢定過，佛種不斷究竟涅槃道成就相似法故，如經如來種中畢定究竟阿耨多羅三藐三菩提故。即此決定紹繼如來聖種。彼云：五者、法體過，六者、處過，七者、業過。今於此中略不具說。

Ru-Yi Translation

The fourth aspect, benefit; first, surpassing the ordinary bodhisattva stage. Furthermore, bodhisattvas, having given rise to this mind, surpass the bodhisattva stage of an ordinary, unenlightened being, are certified into the bodhisattva's own true nature free of afflictive birth, are born into the Tathāgata's own family, become genuine children of the buddhas, are assured of proceeding toward correct, complete awakening, and are assured of carrying forward the Tathāgata's own noble lineage.

Commentary: Regarding "surpass the bodhisattva stage of an ordinary, unenlightened being" and the like — from here the treatise explains the earlier question, what benefit does arising bodhicitta bring. The Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra, in brief, distinguishes various marks of the moment such a mind arises, exactly what is displayed here. Surpassing the ordinary stage is the general statement, exactly this text's "surpass the bodhisattva stage of an ordinary, unenlightened being." Transcendence has eight kinds as particular statements, entering one's rank placed first, ultimate transcendence placed last. That text says: first, entering one's rank — first accomplishing a world-transcending mind, resembling first dwelling in the womb, as the sutra says, "entering the bodhisattva's own rank"; this is exactly "certified into the bodhisattva's own true nature free of afflictive birth." That text says: second, transcending one's family — resembling being born into a family, as the sutra says, "born into the buddhas' own family"; this is exactly "born into the Tathāgata's own family." That text says: third, transcending one's lineage — resembling a child arising from Mahāyāna practice, as the sutra says, "one's lineage now noble, beyond any reproach"; this is exactly "becoming a genuine child of the buddhas." That text says: fourth, transcending the path — because neither the worldly nor world-transcending path fully contains it, resembling being born onto a different path, as the sutra says, "passing beyond every worldly path, entering the world-transcending path"; this is exactly "assured of proceeding toward correct, complete awakening." That text says: eighth, ultimate transcendence — the buddha-lineage never severed, resembling the accomplishment of the ultimate path of nirvana, as the sutra says, "within the Tathāgata's own lineage, assured of ultimately reaching unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening"; this is exactly "assured of carrying forward the Tathāgata's own noble lineage." That text says: fifth, transcending the dharma-body; sixth, transcending one's sphere; seventh, transcending karmic action. Here, in brief, these are not explained in full.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi draws out the imagery embedded in each of the five transcendences the commentary does explain: entering one's rank is likened to a first moment of conception, the world-transcending mind arising for the very first time, utterly unlike an ordinary being's habitually discriminating, defiled mind; transcending one's family is likened to being born, at last, into an actual family — the buddhas' own family — rather than an ordinary household; transcending one's lineage is likened to a genuine child resembling its parent, a first-ground bodhisattva's own Mahāyāna conduct now resembling the buddhas' own conduct closely enough to be called their true child, one's lineage beyond any possible reproach; transcending the path is likened to being born onto an entirely different road, no longer the worldly path an ordinary being's karma follows, nor even the shared path a bodhisattva and hearer might once have walked together, but the world-transcending path alone; and ultimate transcendence affirms that the buddha-lineage, once truly entered, can never again be severed, this bodhisattva's own eventual buddhahood already assured beyond any possibility of failure.

He notes that the commentary candidly acknowledges omitting three of the eight — transcending the dharma-body, one's sphere, and karmic action — perhaps because these three concern more technical distinctions (the substance of the dharma-body itself, the domain in which a first-ground bodhisattva now operates, and the changed nature of karmic action once compassion rather than affliction drives it) that this treatise's own text does not take up explicitly. Having completed this first benefit — surpassing the ordinary stage — he notes, the treatise turns next to the second benefit: the abundant, multiplied joy characterizing the ground of utmost joy itself, unfolded across six distinct causes.

83. Section: Abundant Joy Opens — Certified Purity, and Freedom From Anger, Harm, and Contention

The treatise now turns to the second benefit of arising bodhicitta: abundant, multiplied joy, unfolded across six causes. The first cause is genuinely attaining certified purity exactly as it is — with undefiled wisdom, truly certifying the truth of the four noble truths, arousing undefiled faith toward the three jewels and, arising together with this, an undefiled precept-discipline, a bodhisattva at utmost joy accomplishes the four certified purities and gives rise to the greatest joy, since undefiled dharma is what the noble ones themselves delight in. The second cause is freedom from anger, harm, and contention toward other beings: even when other sentient beings display distorted, unruly conduct, such a bodhisattva gives rise to no resentment, no wish to oppress, no fighting, and no disputation, the three karmic doors of body, speech, and mind all free of anger — resentment and harm belonging to mental karma, fighting to bodily karma, and disputation to verbal karma.

Chinese Source

天二、極多歡喜　地一、辨　玄一、由得證淨　又正獲得如實證淨，極多歡喜。

《披》又正獲得如實證淨等者：信敬三寶多歡喜故，十地經論中敬歡喜攝。

玄二、由離瞋恚　於他有情遠離多分忿、害、鬥諍。

《披》遠離多分忿害鬥諍者：見諸眾生不如說修行、威儀不正時，忍不瞋故，名遠離忿，十地經論中不瞋歡喜攝。教化他、攝取眾生時，慈悲調柔，名遠離害，十地經論中不惱歡喜攝。自心調伏、論義解說時，心不擾動，名遠離鬥諍，十地經論中不壞歡喜攝。如是遠離言多分者，由經中說，多不瞋恨、多不惱眾生、多不壞他義故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, abundant joy; first, distinguishing six causes; first, genuinely attaining certified purity. Furthermore, genuinely attaining certified purity exactly as it is, one experiences abundant joy.

Commentary: Regarding "genuinely attaining certified purity exactly as it is" and the like — because faith in and reverence for the three jewels brings abundant joy, the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra subsumes this within reverence-joy.

Second, freedom from anger. Toward other sentient beings, one is free of most resentment, harm, and contention.

Commentary: Regarding "free of most resentment, harm, and contention" — seeing sentient beings not practicing as taught, or of improper conduct, and yet forbearing rather than growing angry, this is called freedom from resentment, subsumed in the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra within non-anger-joy. Teaching and gathering in others with a compassion that is gentle and pliant is called freedom from harm, subsumed within non-affliction-joy. One's own mind subdued, remaining unperturbed while debating and explaining doctrine, is called freedom from contention, subsumed within non-corruption-joy. Saying "free of most" in this way is because the sutra says "mostly without resentment, mostly not afflicting sentient beings, mostly not corrupting others' meaning."

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi introduces this section by naming, ahead of time, all nine kinds of joy Vasubandhu's Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra lists for a first-ground bodhisattva dwelling in utmost joy: abundant faith and reverence, abundant love and cherishing, abundant delight, abundant gentleness and pliancy, abundant eagerness, abundant capacity, abundant non-corruption of others' intent, abundant non-affliction of sentient beings, and abundant non-resentment — grouped further into joy of mind, joy of substance, and joy of faculty. This treatise's own six causes selectively draw on this ninefold list rather than repeating it wholesale, the first cause (certified purity) corresponding to reverence-joy alone.

He explains why the word "mostly" qualifies freedom from resentment, harm, and contention — a first-ground bodhisattva has only just attained forbearance regarding the non-arising of dharmas, afflictions not yet entirely severed, so a small residue of habitual tendency can still remain even amid this abundant freedom; the word signals genuine, near-complete transformation rather than absolute perfection. Having explained the first two of six causes of abundant joy, he notes, the treatise turns next to the third cause: freedom from arrogance and conceit.

84. Section: Freedom From Arrogance, From Craving Joy, From Alarm — and the Sixfold Freedom From Fear Named

The treatise continues through the remaining causes of abundant joy. The third cause is freedom from arrogance: toward every kind of bodhisattva undertaking that benefits sentient beings, every kind of provision for awakening, every kind of buddha-dharma belonging to unsurpassed awakening, and every kind of the buddhas' own undertaking, such a bodhisattva, with a pure, heightened aspiration, grasps and enters with resolute understanding, observing that one's own person genuinely accords correctly with all this — swiftly and completely realizing these dharmas — and so, understanding in this way, experiences abundant joy free of any conceit. The fourth cause is freedom from craving joy: observing the wondrously excellent vastness of the seven noble treasures — faith, discipline, conscientiousness, shame, learning, generosity, and wisdom — capable of drawing one out of worldly attachment, undefiled and peerless, gathering in benefit for body and mind, such a bodhisattva accomplishes all these immeasurable, blazing wholesome dharmas without any craving. The fifth cause is freedom from alarm: knowing for oneself that unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening is now near at hand, that one's own excellent aspiration toward great awakening has become purified, and that one is now free of every fear — by this very reason, abundant joy arises. The treatise then opens the sixth cause, freedom from the five fears, listing them: because such bodhisattvas have already given rise to a well-determined mind, all five fears — of not surviving, of ill repute, of death, of unfortunate rebirth, and of facing an assembly — are entirely eliminated and severed.

Chinese Source

玄三、由離憍慢　於一切種菩薩所作利眾生事，於一切種菩提資糧圓滿，於一切種無上菩提一切佛法，於一切種佛所作事，以淨增上意樂，攀緣勝解趣入。於是諸法速疾圓證，自觀己身能正隨順，如是解了，極多歡喜。

玄四、由離貪喜　又自觀見妙善廣大，能引出離，無染無等，攝受饒益身心歡喜，於此無量熾然善法皆悉成就。

玄五、由離驚怖　又自了知：我於無上正等菩提，今已鄰近，於大菩提我勝意樂已得清淨，我今已離一切怖畏。由是因緣，極多歡喜。

玄六、由離怖畏　黃一、標　由諸菩薩已能發起善決定心，於五怖畏皆悉除斷。

Ru-Yi Translation

Third, freedom from arrogance. Toward every kind of bodhisattva undertaking that benefits sentient beings, every kind of complete fulfillment of awakening's provisions, every kind of buddha-dharma of unsurpassed awakening, and every kind of the buddhas' own undertaking, with a pure, heightened aspiration, one grasps and enters with resolute understanding. Swiftly and completely realizing these dharmas, observing that one's own person genuinely accords correctly, understanding in this way, one experiences abundant joy.

Fourth, freedom from craving joy. Furthermore, observing for oneself the wondrously excellent vastness able to draw one out of attachment, undefiled and peerless, gathering in benefit for body and mind with joy, one accomplishes all these immeasurable, blazing wholesome dharmas.

Fifth, freedom from alarm. Furthermore, knowing for oneself: toward unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening, I am now near at hand; toward great awakening, my own excellent aspiration has already become pure; I am now free of every fear. By this cause, abundant joy arises.

Sixth, freedom from fear; first, naming them. Because such bodhisattvas have already given rise to a well-determined mind, the five fears are all entirely eliminated and severed.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi cross-references the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra for the third and fourth causes: freedom from arrogance corresponds there to capacity-joy, seeing that one's own realization is now near at hand; the fourth cause draws on three joys at once — having-love-joy, delighting in observing suchness itself, matched with the wondrous, vast dharmas able to draw one out of attachment; gentleness-joy and eagerness-joy, one's own body and mind pervasively benefited and satisfied; and delight-joy, one's own realization, weighed against ordinary grasping, now recognized as genuinely superior.

He introduces the five fears named here — not surviving, ill repute, death, unfortunate rebirth, and facing an assembly — as classical concerns any practitioner recognizes: an ordinary beginner, even while practicing generosity, still hoards resources out of fear of not surviving; still fears slander when mixing with unsavory company for the sake of teaching; still fears death despite great aspiration, unable to fully relinquish the body; still fears falling into an unfortunate rebirth through unwholesome karma; and still fears faltering before a learned, powerful assembly. He notes that the treatise will now explain, one by one, exactly why a first-ground bodhisattva is free of every one of these five — the topic of the immediately following section.

85. Section: The Five Fears Explained One by One — Egolessness Removes Every Ground for Fear

The treatise now explains each of the five fears in turn, tracing every freedom back to a bodhisattva's own realization of egolessness. Freedom from the fear of not surviving: because such a bodhisattva has thoroughly cultivated the wondrous wisdom of egolessness, the very notion of discriminating "self" no longer even arises, much less any discrimination of "what is loved as mine" or attachment to life's necessities — and so there is no fear of not surviving. Freedom from the fear of ill repute: because such a bodhisattva has nothing to hope for from others, constantly arousing instead the aspiration "I should bring benefit to every sentient being," not seeking anything from sentient beings in return — and so there is no fear of ill repute. Freedom from the fear of death: because such a bodhisattva has departed from the view of self, no notion of "self" being lost or destroyed arises — and so there is no fear of death. Freedom from the fear of unfortunate rebirth: knowing for oneself that after death, in the world to come, one will certainly encounter buddhas and bodhisattvas — and by this certainty, there is no fear of unfortunate rebirth. And freedom from the fear of facing an assembly: because, through one's own aspiration, one observes that in the entire world there is not even a single being equal to oneself, let alone superior — and so there is no timidity before any assembly.

Chinese Source

黃二、釋　宇一、無不活畏　由善修習無我妙智，分別我想尚不復轉，況當得有分別我愛、或資生愛。由是因緣，無不活畏。

宇二、無惡名畏　由於他所無所希望，常自發起如是欲樂：我當饒益一切有情，非於有情有所求覓。由是因緣，無惡名畏。

宇三、無有死畏　由離我見，於我無有失壞想轉，故無死畏。

宇四、無惡趣畏　自知死後，於當來世決定值遇諸佛菩薩，由此決定無惡趣畏。

宇五、無處眾畏　由意樂見一切世間，尚無有一與我齊等，何況殊勝，是故無有處眾怯畏。

《披》由意樂見一切世間等者：由自了知：於大菩提我勝意樂已得清淨故。見一切世間皆無齊等。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, explaining; first, freedom from the fear of not surviving. Because one has thoroughly cultivated the wondrous wisdom of egolessness, the discriminating notion of "self" no longer even arises — much less arising any discrimination of what is loved as one's own, or attachment to life's necessities. By this cause, there is no fear of not surviving.

Second, freedom from the fear of ill repute. Because one has nothing to hope for from others, constantly arousing for oneself this very aspiration: I should bring benefit to every sentient being, not seeking anything at all from sentient beings in return. By this cause, there is no fear of ill repute.

Third, freedom from the fear of death. Because one has departed from the view of self, no notion of "self" being lost or destroyed arises. Therefore there is no fear of death.

Fourth, freedom from the fear of unfortunate rebirth. Knowing for oneself that after death, in the world to come, one will certainly encounter buddhas and bodhisattvas, by this certainty there is no fear of unfortunate rebirth.

Fifth, freedom from the fear of facing an assembly. Because, through one's own aspiration, one observes the entire world and finds not even a single being equal to oneself — let alone superior — therefore there is no timidity before any assembly.

Commentary: Regarding "through one's own aspiration, one observes the entire world" and the like — because one knows for oneself: toward great awakening, my own excellent aspiration has already become pure. One observes the entire world and finds none equal.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats all five explanations as variations on a single insight — genuine realization of egolessness dissolves every fear's own actual ground, since each fear presupposes a self that could fail to survive, be shamed, die, suffer, or be humiliated before others. He draws out the fifth explanation's own subtlety: it is not arrogance that grounds a first-ground bodhisattva's fearlessness before an assembly, but precisely the opposite — having realized that self and others are equally empty and equally ungraspable, there is no "self" left over that could be exalted above or diminished below anyone else, so the very distinction of superior and inferior loses its grip, and confidence arises not from feeling superior but from no longer clinging to any measure of comparison at all.

He notes, following the treatise's own closing remark just after this passage (not translated here in full), that a first-ground bodhisattva's freedom extends further still — freedom from terror at hearing the profound true dharma expounded, freedom from all arrogance and conceit, freedom from resentment toward others' harmful conduct, and freedom from craving worldly wealth, culminating in an undefiled, unwavering enthusiasm for both worldly and world-transcending wholesome dharmas equally, free of any merely worldly notion, capable of perfectly realizing every wholesome dharma — with the Avataṃsaka Sūtra and other great Mahāyāna scriptures elaborating a first-ground bodhisattva's excellent qualities at even greater length than this treatise attempts here. Having now explained all five fears individually, he notes, the treatise turns next to a summary section drawing every one of the six causes of abundant joy together into a single closing statement.

86. Section: The Summary Closes Abundant Joy — and With It, the Entire Fourfold Analysis of Arising Bodhicitta

The treatise now draws every strand of this section together into a single summary, closing not only the sixth cause of abundant joy but the entire second aspect distinguishing a first-ground bodhisattva. Bodhisattvas, having thus become free of all five fears, are likewise free of all terror at hearing the profound true dharma expounded, free of all arrogance and conceit, free of all resentment arising from others' harmful, distorted conduct, and free of all craving for worldly wealth — undefiled, and so without aversion toward any being; blazing with wholesome enthusiasm, and so without merely worldly intent; and thereby able to perfectly realize every wholesome dharma. The commentary maps each clause back onto items already explained: freedom from the five fears already named restates itself; certified purity toward great awakening restates freedom from terror at the profound dharma; correctly according with and understanding every kind restates freedom from arrogance; freedom from resentment, harm, and contention toward other beings restates freedom from resentment at harmful conduct; and the capacity to draw out of attachment, undefiled and peerless, gathering benefit for body and mind with joy, restates freedom from craving worldly wealth — the whole passage concluding this account of joy, which is here named a genuine benefit of arising bodhicitta. With this, the fourfold analysis of arising bodhicitta — its cause, its object of concern, its state and mark, and its benefit — reaches its complete conclusion.

Chinese Source

地二、結　菩薩如是遠離一切五種怖畏，遠離一切聞說甚深正法驚怖，遠離一切高慢憍傲，遠離一切他不饒益種種邪行所起瞋恚，遠離一切世財貪喜。無染汙故，無所憎背；有熾然故，無俗意樂；能圓滿證一切善法。

《披》菩薩如是遠離一切五種怖畏等者：此中隨應結前所說。於五怖畏皆悉除斷，是即遠離一切五種怖畏。於大菩提如實證淨，已離一切怖畏，是即遠離一切聞說甚深正法驚怖。於一切種能正隨順解了，是即遠離一切高慢憍傲。於他有情遠離忿、害、鬥諍，是即遠離一切他不饒益種種邪行所起瞋恚。能引出離，無染無等，攝受饒益身心歡喜，是即遠離一切世財貪喜。如是別別結前說已，復總結顯生歡喜相，是故說無染汙及有熾然，由是能圓滿證一切善法。此結歡喜，說名勝利。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, the summary. Bodhisattvas, thus free of all five fears, are free of all terror at hearing the profound true dharma expounded, free of all arrogance and conceit, free of all resentment arising from others' harmful, distorted conduct, and free of all craving for worldly wealth. Undefiled, and so without aversion toward any being; blazing, and so without merely worldly intent; able to perfectly realize every wholesome dharma.

Commentary: Regarding "bodhisattvas, thus free of all five fears" and the like — this section, as appropriate, summarizes what was said before. The five fears entirely eliminated and severed is exactly "free of all five fears." Genuinely certified pure toward great awakening, already free of every fear, is exactly "free of all terror at hearing the profound true dharma expounded." Correctly according with and understanding every kind is exactly "free of all arrogance and conceit." Free of resentment, harm, and contention toward other beings is exactly "free of all resentment arising from others' harmful, distorted conduct." Able to draw out of attachment, undefiled and peerless, gathering in benefit for body and mind with joy, is exactly "free of all craving for worldly wealth."
