Having thus summarized each point individually, the text then draws a total summary displaying the mark of arising joy — hence saying "undefiled" and "blazing" — by which one is able to perfectly realize every wholesome dharma. This summary of joy is here named a benefit.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this summary as doing genuine double duty: line by line, it maps each closing phrase back onto something already explained across the six causes, but taken as a whole, it also names the general mark of abundant joy itself — undefiled (hence no aversion toward any being at all) and blazing (hence no merely worldly intent whatsoever), the very combination that makes perfect realization of every wholesome dharma possible. He treats this as the treatise's own considered judgment: joy this abundant is not incidental to a first-ground bodhisattva's realization but constitutive of it, a natural consequence of egolessness removing fear's every ground, certified purity removing doubt, and compassion removing self-concern.

He notes the larger structural significance of this closing line: with benefit now explained in full — first surpassing the ordinary stage, now abundant joy through six causes — the entire fourfold analysis of arising bodhicitta (its cause, its object, its state and mark, and its benefit) reaches complete conclusion, and with it, the second of the eight aspects distinguishing the abode of utmost joy as a whole. The treatise turns next to the third aspect: how the great vow already established gives rise to ten specific great vows, beginning with the vow to make offerings to the buddhas.

87. Section: The Third Aspect Opens — Ten Great Vows Arising From the Purified, Excellent Aspiration

The treatise now opens the third of the eight aspects distinguishing the abode of utmost joy: the arising of ten great vows. In the present life, a bodhisattva at utmost joy, led by the heightened power of faith in the three jewels, is able to give rise to every kind of diligence in bodhisattva practice without ever growing lax; and in the world to come, exactly as the chapter on the factors of awakening already explained in fascicle 45, ten great vows — to make offerings to buddhas, to uphold the true dharma, to display appearance in the world, to receive and cultivate wholesome dharma, to bring sentient beings to maturity, to display oneself pervasively, to purify one's own buddha-field, to enter the same vehicle together with others, to labor for others' benefit unceasingly, and to swiftly realize awakening — are now, at this very abode of utmost joy, able to be fully brought forth, precisely because a purified, excellent aspiration has now been attained. Turning to the vows one by one, arising from specific matters, the first vow arises for the sake of making offerings to the most excellent being among sentient beings — the genuine field of merit, the great teacher and lord of dharma, namely the Buddha.

Chinese Source

申三、引發正願　酉一、標　又現法中，能起菩薩一切精進，信增上力為前導故。於當來世，如前所說菩提分品十種大願，今即於此極歡喜住能具引發，由得清淨勝意樂故。

《披》於當來世如前所說菩提分品十種大願者：菩提分品中說：菩薩大願，當知即從正願所出。此復十種，乃至廣說。（陵本四十五卷十七頁3659）由彼正願，願於當來攝受一切菩薩善法，攝受一切所有功德。十種大願從彼所出，是故說言於當來世。

酉二、釋　戌一、別辨相　亥一、就事起願　天一、為供養佛　為欲供養最勝有情真實福田大師法主，是故引發第一大願。

Ru-Yi Translation

Third aspect: giving rise to a well-determined vow; first, naming them. Furthermore, in the present life, led by the heightened power of faith as the forerunner, one is able to give rise to every kind of bodhisattva diligence. In the world to come, exactly as the chapter on the factors of awakening already explained, ten great vows — now, at this very abode of utmost joy, are able to be fully brought forth, because a purified, excellent aspiration has been attained.

Commentary: Regarding "in the world to come, exactly as the chapter on the factors of awakening already explained, ten great vows" — the chapter on the factors of awakening says: a bodhisattva's great vow should be understood as issuing from a well-determined vow. This again has ten kinds, explained at length (fascicle 45, page 3659 of the Nanjing edition). From that well-determined vow, one vows that in the future one will receive and gather every bodhisattva's wholesome dharma, and gather every kind of merit whatsoever. The ten great vows issue from that vow, and this is why it is said "in the world to come."

Second, explaining; first, distinguishing each mark individually; first, vows arising from specific matters; first, for the sake of making offerings to the Buddha. In order to make offerings to the most excellent being among sentient beings, the genuine field of merit, the great teacher and lord of dharma, this is why the first great vow is given rise to.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi reminds readers that this ten-vow list was already introduced in outline back in fascicle 45's chapter on the factors of awakening, the commentary now cross-referencing that exact passage — the vow itself is said to issue from the well-determined vow (the very first of the eight aspects, already fully explained across milestones 76 through 78), a first-ground bodhisattva's purified aspiration naturally overflowing into these ten specific commitments the way a well-established root naturally sends up specific branches. He lists all ten by name as the treatise itself will now take up in turn: to make offerings to buddhas, to uphold the true dharma, to display appearance in the world, to receive and cultivate wholesome dharma, to bring sentient beings to maturity, to display oneself pervasively, to purify one's own buddha-field, to enter the same vehicle together with others, to labor for others' benefit unceasingly, and to swiftly realize awakening.

He explains the first vow's own reasoning: the Buddha alone is called the genuine field of merit because offerings made to the Buddha yield an unsurpassed, inexhaustible return, unlike offerings to any lesser recipient, and a first-ground bodhisattva, having personally realized suchness, now genuinely appreciates for the first time just how supremely worthy of veneration a buddha actually is — vowing, in the future, to make offerings to countless buddhas with every manner of excellent offering-substance once one's own merit and wisdom reach full perfection. Having opened this third aspect and explained its very first vow, he notes, the treatise turns next to the second vow — to uphold the true dharma — continuing through all ten vows arising from specific matters one by one.

88. Section: Vows Two Through Seven — Upholding the Dharma, Turning the Wheel, Right Conduct, Ripening Beings, Visiting Buddha-Lands, Purifying One's Own Field

The treatise continues through the ten great vows arising from specific matters. The second vow arises for the sake of upholding the excellent true dharma proclaimed by the buddhas, protecting it from destruction and ensuring its continued transmission in the world. The third vow arises for the sake of entreating buddhas to turn the wondrous, unprecedented true dharma-wheel — also called the vow of the eight marks of accomplishing the way, from descending from Tuṣita heaven through entering great nirvana. The fourth vow arises for the sake of according with and practicing the bodhisattva conduct of the ten perfections themselves. The fifth vow arises for the sake of bringing to maturity those sentient beings who are genuine vessels — beings established in one of the three vehicle lineages, capable of receiving the true dharma and, guided accordingly, entering the three kinds of awakening and reaching ultimate liberation. The sixth vow arises for the sake of journeying to every buddha-land, beholding the tathāgatas, attending upon and making offerings to them, and receiving the true dharma directly from them. And the seventh vow arises for the sake of purifying and cultivating one's own buddha-field, explained through seven aspects of a pure buddha-land drawn from the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra: identity-purity, sovereignty-purity, adornment-purity, enjoyment-purity, purity of the beings dwelling there, cause-purity, and fruit-purity.

Chinese Source

天二、為受持法　為欲受持彼所宣說無上正法，是故引發第二大願。

天三、為轉法輪　為欲勸請轉未曾有妙正法輪，是故引發第三大願。

天四、為順正行　為欲順彼行菩薩行，是故引發第四大願。

《披》為欲順彼行菩薩行者：十種波羅蜜多，名菩薩行。此是一切菩薩之所應行，為欲順彼，故發大願。

天五、為熟有情　為欲成熟彼器有情，是故引發第五大願。

《披》為欲成熟彼器有情者：無量所調伏界，是名彼器有情。由住種姓堪受正法，是器義故。隨其所應為說正法，於三菩提令得趣入，究竟出離，是名成熟。

天六、為遍示現　為欲往趣諸佛國土，奉見如來，承事供養，聽受正法，是故引發第六大願。

天七、為淨自土　為淨修治自佛國土，是故引發第七大願。

《披》為淨修治自佛國土者：十地經論說：淨佛國土有七種相。一者、同體淨，二者、自在淨，三者、莊嚴淨，四者、受用淨，五者、住處眾生淨，六者、因淨，七者、果淨。如彼應釋。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, for the sake of upholding the dharma. In order to uphold the unsurpassed true dharma proclaimed by the buddhas, this is why the second great vow is given rise to.

Third, for the sake of turning the dharma-wheel. In order to entreat the turning of the wondrous, unprecedented true dharma-wheel, this is why the third great vow is given rise to.

Fourth, for the sake of according with right conduct. In order to accord with and practice bodhisattva conduct, this is why the fourth great vow is given rise to.

Commentary: Regarding "in order to accord with and practice bodhisattva conduct" — the ten perfections are called bodhisattva conduct. This is what every bodhisattva should practice; in order to accord with this, the great vow is given rise to.

Fifth, for the sake of ripening sentient beings. In order to bring to maturity those sentient beings who are genuine vessels, this is why the fifth great vow is given rise to.

Commentary: Regarding "in order to bring to maturity those sentient beings who are genuine vessels" — the immeasurable realm of beings capable of being subdued is called the genuine vessel. Because being established in the lineage makes one capable of receiving the true dharma, this is the meaning of "vessel." Teaching the true dharma as appropriate to each, causing entry into the three kinds of awakening and ultimate liberation, is called maturation.

Sixth, for the sake of appearing everywhere. In order to journey to every buddha-land, behold the tathāgatas, attend upon and make offerings to them, and receive the true dharma, this is why the sixth great vow is given rise to.

Seventh, for the sake of purifying one's own field. In order to purify and cultivate one's own buddha-land, this is why the seventh great vow is given rise to.

Commentary: Regarding "in order to purify and cultivate one's own buddha-land" — the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra says: purifying a buddha-land has seven aspects. First, identity-purity. Second, sovereignty-purity. Third, adornment-purity. Fourth, enjoyment-purity. Fifth, purity of the beings dwelling there. Sixth, cause-purity. Seventh, fruit-purity. This should be explained exactly as there.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through the seven purities of a buddha-land the commentary cross-references in full: identity-purity, that every buddha-land is, in a sense, a single buddha-land, and a single buddha-land is every buddha-land; sovereignty-purity, that all lands, whether seemingly defiled or pure, appear equally pure to a bodhisattva's own vision; adornment-purity, lands endowed with unhindered spiritual powers, luminous splendor, and the seven kinds of treasure — gold, silver, lapis lazuli, giant clam shell, agate, coral, and amber; enjoyment-purity, beings there having departed from every affliction and accomplished the pure path together; purity of the beings dwelling there, immeasurably wise beings filling such a land entirely; cause-purity, entering the buddhas' own wondrously equal state through two kinds of cause — conduct such as generosity and discipline, and virtuous practice such as the samādhi of a pure land; and fruit-purity, a land manifesting however each being's own mind takes delight in it, displaying the buddhas' own wisdom and spiritual power.

He notes that vow three's alternate name — the vow of the eight marks of accomplishing the way — points to the Discourse Record's identification of the classical eightfold pattern of a buddha's career: descending from Tuṣita heaven, entering the womb, dwelling in the womb, being born, renouncing the household life, accomplishing the way, turning the dharma-wheel, and entering final nirvana — a first-ground bodhisattva already vowing, in this single moment of arising bodhicitta, to one day walk this entire path in full. Having now covered vows two through seven, he notes, the treatise turns next to the final three vows — entering the same vehicle together with others, laboring for others' benefit unceasingly, and swiftly realizing awakening — closing out all ten vows arising from specific matters.

89. Section: The Final Three Vows — Shared Path, Ceaseless Benefit, Swift Awakening — Closing All Ten Vows, With Their Alternate Names

The treatise completes the ten vows arising from specific matters. The eighth vow arises for the sake of never, in any place of rebirth whatsoever, becoming separated from the buddhas and bodhisattvas, and of always sharing the same single flavor of aspiration and application together with fellow bodhisattvas — that single flavor being the Mahāyāna itself, all practitioners oriented in the very same direction. The ninth vow arises so that one's efforts to benefit all sentient beings are never spent in vain, every undistorted application of practice bearing genuine fruit. And the tenth vow arises for the sake of realizing unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening, in order to perform every one of the buddhas' own undertakings. The treatise then supplies alternate names drawn from the Discourse Record for all ten vows together: the vow of offering, the vow of upholding and transmitting, the vow of entreating the dharma-wheel (also the vow of the eight marks), the vow of practice, the vow of maturing sentient beings, the vow of taking the dharma as one's foremost concern, the vow of purifying one's own land, the vow of shared mind and shared conduct, the vow of the three karmic doors never exhausted, and the vow of accomplishing awakening.

Chinese Source

天八、為同一趣　為於一切在所生處，常不遠離諸佛菩薩，與諸菩薩常同一味意樂加行，是故引發第八大願。

天九、為恆利他　常為利益一切有情，曾不空過，是故引發第九大願。

天十、為作佛事　為證無上正等菩提，作諸佛事，是故引發第十大願。

Ru-Yi Translation

Eighth, for the sake of entering the same vehicle together. In order that, in every place of rebirth whatsoever, one never becomes far removed from the buddhas and bodhisattvas, always sharing with fellow bodhisattvas the very same single flavor of aspiration and application, this is why the eighth great vow is given rise to.

Ninth, for the sake of ceaselessly benefiting others. Constantly benefiting all sentient beings, never once spent in vain, this is why the ninth great vow is given rise to.

Tenth, for the sake of performing the buddhas' own undertakings. In order to realize unsurpassed, correct, complete awakening and perform every one of the buddhas' own undertakings, this is why the tenth great vow is given rise to.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi supplies the Discourse Record's own list of alternate names for all ten vows, useful for recalling their gist at a glance: the vow of offering (first, to the buddhas); the vow of upholding and transmitting (second, receiving and passing on the teaching); the vow of entreating the turning of the dharma-wheel, also called the vow of the eight marks of accomplishing the way (third); the vow of practice (fourth, walking the bodhisattva path life after life); the vow of maturing sentient beings (fifth, bringing the roots of faith, diligence, mindfulness, concentration, and wisdom to realize reality itself); the vow of taking the dharma as one's foremost concern (sixth, wishing to travel to buddha-lands, draw near the tathāgatas, make offerings, and receive the true dharma); the vow of purifying one's own land (seventh); the vow of shared mind and shared conduct (eighth, teacher and fellow practitioners alike delighting in the Mahāyāna together); the vow of the three karmic doors never exhausted (ninth, every action of body, speech, and mind aimed at awakening and benefiting beings never wasted); and the vow of accomplishing awakening (tenth, wishing to become a buddha quickly precisely in order to genuinely perform every buddha-deed for beings' sake).

With all ten vows now named and explained, he notes, this closes the first subsection of the third aspect — vows arising from specific matters. The treatise turns next to a second subsection: vows arising from a vow already made, showing how each of these ten specific vows further gives rise to the vow that they themselves never come to an end.

90. Section: Vows Arising From a Vow — Endless as the Realm of Beings — Closing the Entire Third Aspect

The treatise now explains the second subsection of vow-generation: vows arising from a vow already made. Having established the ten vows above, such a bodhisattva makes this further vow: just as the realm of sentient beings continues on and on, never coming to an end, and just as the way of the world continues on and on, never coming to an end, so too may this great vow of mine continue on, life after life, all the way to the very boundary of ultimate awakening, never once departed from, never once forgotten, never once contradicted. Taking this oath for oneself, one's mind gives rise to a well-determined vow — echoing the Avataṃsaka Sūtra's own famous declaration that only when empty space itself is exhausted, only when the realm of sentient beings is exhausted, only when sentient beings' karma is exhausted, only when sentient beings' afflictions are exhausted, will this vow of mine likewise be exhausted. The treatise then briefly clarifies the overall structure just completed: the first ten vows arose from specific matters one should vow about, while this final vow arose from the vow itself, continuing that same vow onward without end. With a concluding summary — these ten great vows, taken as the foremost concern, give rise to countless hundreds of thousands of further well-determined vows, such a bodhisattva thereby equipped with every great vow for the world to come, and arousing great diligence in the present life to carry them out — the entire third aspect, giving rise to a well-determined vow, reaches its complete conclusion.

Chinese Source

亥二、就願起願　作是願言：如有情界展轉相續，終無斷盡；亦如世道展轉相續，終無斷盡；我此大願生生相續，乃至究竟菩提邊際，常不遠離、常不忘失、常不乖離。如是自誓，心發正願。

戌二、顯略義　當知此中，前就所應願事起願，後即就願以起於願。

《披》前就所應願事起願等者：前說十種大願，名就所應願事起願。後說願無斷盡，名即就願以起於願。

酉三、結　如是菩薩十種大願以為上首，能生無數百千正願。如是菩薩，於當來世具諸大願，於現法中發大精進。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, vows arising from a vow. One makes this vow: just as the realm of sentient beings continues on and on, never coming to an end; just as the way of the world continues on and on, never coming to an end; so too may this great vow of mine continue, life after life, all the way to the very boundary of ultimate awakening — never departed from, never forgotten, never contradicted. Taking this oath for oneself, one's mind gives rise to a well-determined vow.

Second, clarifying the brief meaning. One should understand that within this, the former vows arose from the specific matters one should vow about; the latter arose from the vow itself, giving rise to a further vow.

Commentary: Regarding "the former vows arose from the specific matters one should vow about" and the like — the ten great vows spoken of earlier are called vows arising from the specific matters one should vow about. The latter statement, that the vow never comes to an end, is called vows arising from the vow itself.

Third, the summary. These ten great vows of a bodhisattva, taken as the foremost concern, are able to give rise to countless hundreds of thousands of well-determined vows. Such a bodhisattva is thus equipped with every great vow for the world to come, and in the present life arouses great diligence.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi highlights the direct echo of the Avataṃsaka Sūtra's own celebrated vow-formula here — "when empty space is exhausted, when the realm of sentient beings is exhausted, when sentient beings' karma is exhausted, when sentient beings' afflictions are exhausted, then my vow too shall be exhausted" — noting that since none of these four are ever actually exhausted, the vow itself is thereby genuinely endless, a structural feature rather than mere rhetorical flourish: a bodhisattva's vow is deliberately built to outlast every possible terminus.

He treats the closing structural note — ten vows from specific matters, one further vow from the vow itself — as the treatise's own tidy summary of exactly how this section unfolded, and the final summary line as tying together the entire third aspect: from these ten foremost vows spring countless further vows, a first-ground bodhisattva already possessing, in this single moment, the complete future shape of an entire bodhisattva career, while in the present moment already arousing the diligence needed to begin walking it. With the third aspect (giving rise to a well-determined vow) now fully closed, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fourth of the eight aspects: ten dharmas purifying the abode of utmost joy itself, needed because even a first-ground bodhisattva's innate afflictions, though the discriminated kind are already severed, remain to be gradually purified through continued diligent practice.

91. Section: The Fourth Aspect Opens — Ten Dharmas That Purify Utmost Joy, Beginning With Pure Faith, Great Compassion, Great Loving-Kindness, and Giving

The treatise now opens the fourth of the eight aspects: ten dharmas that purify the abode of utmost joy, needed because a first-ground bodhisattva, though having severed the discriminated afflictions, has not yet severed the innate ones, and so must continue diligent practice to swiftly purify this abode's own qualities. The first purifying dharma is pure faith: deeply arousing a pure faith in the buddha-dharmas — the buddhas' own realized awakening and the teaching and discipline they proclaimed. The second is great compassion: observing the principle of dependent origination governing sentient beings' own cyclic existence, realizing that this amounts to nothing but a pure mass of great suffering, and so arousing great compassion. The third is great loving-kindness: having observed this suffering, vowing for oneself, "I shall cause these very sentient beings to be liberated from this pure mass of great suffering and attain the foremost happiness," and so arousing great loving-kindness — compassion contemplating dependent origination, loving-kindness vowing to remove suffering and grant happiness. And the fourth is giving: in order to rescue beings from every anxiety and suffering, being entirely without attachment to oneself, and so, free of any such attachment, able to relinquish both inner body and outer wealth entirely, giving generously to sentient beings according to their need.

Chinese Source

申四、淨修住法　酉一、標十法　復有十種淨修住法，由是能令極歡喜住速得清淨。

酉二、別列相　戌一、淨信　一者、於諸佛法深生淨信。

戌二、大悲　二者、觀諸有情緣起道理，證得唯有純大苦蘊，發起大悲。

戌三、大慈　三者、觀見彼已，自誓願言：我當令彼諸有情類，解脫如是純大苦蘊，得第一樂，發起大慈。

戌四、惠施　四者、為欲救拔一切憂苦，自無顧戀；無顧戀故，能捨內外一切身財，於諸有情而行惠施。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fourth aspect: dharmas purifying the abode; first, naming the ten dharmas. There are furthermore ten dharmas that purify the abode, by which the abode of utmost joy is able to swiftly attain purity.

Second, listing each mark individually; first, pure faith. First, deeply arousing pure faith in the buddha-dharmas.

Second, great compassion. Second, observing the principle of dependent origination governing sentient beings, realizing that this is nothing but a pure mass of great suffering, one arouses great compassion.

Third, great loving-kindness. Third, having observed this, one vows for oneself: I shall cause these very kinds of sentient beings to be liberated from this pure mass of great suffering and attain the foremost happiness — thus arousing great loving-kindness.

Fourth, giving. Fourth, in order to rescue beings from every anxiety and suffering, one is oneself entirely without attachment; being without attachment, one is able to relinquish both inner body and outer wealth entirely, giving generously to sentient beings.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains why purification is still needed at all at this stage: a first-ground bodhisattva has severed only the discriminated afflictions — those arising from mistaken intellectual views, easily cut at the moment of first realizing egolessness — while the innate afflictions, habituated over countless past lives and far more deeply rooted, still remain to be gradually worn away across the remaining grounds. These ten dharmas are the concrete practice by which this gradual purification proceeds, beginning here with the four he groups as a natural pair of pairs: pure faith and great compassion (in brief, faith contemplating ultimate truth and compassion contemplating dependent origination), and great loving-kindness and giving (loving-kindness vowing to grant happiness after compassion vows to remove suffering, giving putting that very vow into visible practice by relinquishing body and wealth alike without attachment).

He notes the precise structure — pure faith turned toward the ultimate truth of the buddha-dharmas, great compassion and loving-kindness turned toward dependent origination and the suffering it produces, and giving as the first of the six perfections now made concrete through non-attachment — as together establishing the emotional and volitional foundation on which the remaining six purifying dharmas, still to come, will build a first-ground bodhisattva's complete practice. Having explained the first four of ten purifying dharmas, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fifth: being free of weariness and fatigue.

92. Section: Purifying Dharmas Five Through Eight — Tireless Seeking, Mastering Treatises, Understanding the World, Cultivating Conscientiousness

The treatise continues through the ten purifying dharmas. The fifth is being free of weariness: in order to benefit sentient beings, tirelessly and diligently seeking worldly and world-transcending dharmas from others, never once growing weary or fatigued — since beings are limitless, the dharma-doors one must learn are likewise limitless, and only sustained, uncomplaining effort can master them. The sixth is mastering treatises: because of this very freedom from weariness, attaining a pure wisdom of every treatise, both worldly and world-transcending, understood thoroughly through subsequently attained wisdom yet never grasped at. The seventh is understanding the world: because of skillfully knowing these treatises, understanding worldly affairs and human circumstance so thoroughly that, for beings of inferior, middling, or superior capacity alike, one is able to cultivate the right practice fittingly and appropriately for each, well understanding the world's own ways. And the eighth is cultivating conscientiousness: precisely within this same correct applied effort, cultivating a sense of shame and conscientiousness according to what is fitting in time, place, and measure — the commentary here cross-referencing the Basic Section's own chapter on the hearers' stage, on abiding with correct understanding, for the precise meaning of acting rightly as to matter, place, time, measure, principle, and kind.

Chinese Source

戌五、無有厭倦　五者、為欲利益諸有情故，從他勤求世出世法，曾無厭倦。

戌六、善知諸論　六者、無厭倦故，證得一切論智清淨，善知諸論。

戌七、善解世間　七者、善知論故，於劣中勝諸有情所，如應如宜而修正行，善解世間。

戌八、修習慚愧　八者、即於如是正加行中，依應時分量等正行，而修慚愧。

《披》依應時分量等正行者：聲聞地中正知而住作如是說：若於是事、是處、是時，如量、如理、如其品類所應作者，即於此事、此處、此時，如量、如理、如其品類正知而作。（陵本二十四卷二十頁2066）此說依應時分量等，等取彼所餘相應知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fifth, being free of weariness. Fifth, in order to benefit sentient beings, diligently seeking worldly and world-transcending dharmas from others, never once growing weary.

Sixth, mastering treatises. Sixth, because of freedom from weariness, attaining a pure wisdom of every treatise, mastering treatises.

Seventh, understanding the world. Seventh, because of skillfully knowing treatises, for sentient beings of inferior and superior capacity, cultivating the right practice fittingly and appropriately for each, well understanding the world.

Eighth, cultivating conscientiousness. Eighth, precisely within this correct applied effort, according to what is fitting in time and measure, cultivating shame and conscientiousness.

Commentary: Regarding "according to what is fitting in time and measure" — the chapter on the hearers' stage, on abiding with correct understanding, says: whatever is to be done regarding this matter, this place, this time, according to measure, according to principle, according to its kind, one does exactly this matter, this place, this time, according to measure, according to principle, according to its kind, with correct understanding. (Fascicle 24, page 2066 of the Nanjing edition.) This statement, "according to what is fitting in time and measure," includes the remaining aspects as well, which should be understood accordingly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi traces the logical chain linking these four: tireless seeking of both worldly and world-transcending dharma (since world-transcending truth is never separate from worldly reality) naturally produces mastery of every treatise — astronomy, geography, history, literature, governance, economics, medicine, trade, agriculture, whatever a given age requires — and this mastery in turn produces genuine understanding of the world, enabling a bodhisattva to teach each being flexibly, matched precisely to that being's own capacity, whether inferior, middling, or superior.

He unpacks the six factors the cross-referenced hearers'-stage passage names for acting with correct understanding — matter (exactly what one is doing: sitting in meditation, listening to dharma, receiving a robe and bowl), place (a monastery or elsewhere), time (morning, midday, evening, each suited to different activities), measure (neither excessive nor deficient — teaching a layperson only when genuinely needed, not simply because one feels it might help), principle (according with the undistorted teaching), and kind (fitting the specific category of occasion, a funeral rite differing entirely from a celebratory one) — noting that even a first-ground bodhisattva, aware that complete mastery of all six factors together remains imperfect, continues cultivating a healthy sense of shame and conscientiousness precisely so that wholesome qualities keep increasing rather than stagnating. Having covered purifying dharmas five through eight, he notes, the treatise turns next to the ninth and tenth: firm, sustained resolve, and making offerings to the Tathāgata.

93. Section: Firm Resolve and Offering to the Tathāgata — Closing All Ten Purifying Dharmas With Their Summary

The treatise completes the ten purifying dharmas. The ninth is firm, sustained resolve: within this same correct applied effort, attaining an irreversible, firm, sustaining nature — restraining and controlling any afflicted disposition, enduring every suffering without being shaken by intense fear, courageous in disposition, and, through correct discernment, accomplishing an unwavering strength of mind that holds firm without wavering. The tenth is making offerings to the Tathāgata: with every manner of excellent offering, with reverence, and through right conduct itself, making offerings to the Tathāgata — a first-ground bodhisattva's own pure, firm faith and strong resolute understanding toward the merit, causality, and correct view of offering allowing material offerings to be made with truly vast, reverent visualization, while right conduct itself, the six perfections practiced for self- and other-benefit, constitutes offering through dharma-practice. The treatise then names all ten together in summary — pure faith, compassion, loving-kindness, generous giving, freedom from weariness, mastering treatises, understanding the world, cultivating conscientiousness, firm resolve, and offering to the Tathāgata — closing the entire fourth aspect, the ten dharmas purifying the abode of utmost joy.

Chinese Source

戌九、堅力持性　九者、即於如是正加行中，得無退轉堅力持性。

戌十、供養如來　十者、以諸上妙利養恭敬及與正行，供養如來。

酉三、結略義　是名十種淨修住法。由此能令極歡喜住速得清淨。所謂淨信、悲、慈、惠捨、無有厭倦、善知諸論、善解世間、修習慚愧、堅力持性、供養如來。

Ru-Yi Translation

Ninth, firm, sustained resolve. Ninth, precisely within this correct applied effort, attaining an irreversible, firm, sustaining nature.

Tenth, offering to the Tathāgata. Tenth, with every kind of excellent offering, with reverence, and through right conduct, making offerings to the Tathāgata.

Third, summarizing the brief meaning. These are called the ten dharmas purifying the abode. By these, the abode of utmost joy is able to swiftly attain purity — namely, pure faith, compassion, loving-kindness, generous giving, freedom from weariness, mastering treatises, understanding the world, cultivating conscientiousness, firm resolve, and offering to the Tathāgata.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes an important qualification embedded in his own gloss on cultivating conscientiousness (the eighth dharma): a first-ground bodhisattva has severed only the discriminated grasping at self and dharmas, together with a portion of afflictive craving, but has not yet severed the innate grasping at self and dharmas — hence there remains real, unfinished work, and hence the continuing need for shame and conscientiousness rather than complacency. He treats firm resolve (ninth) as the resulting strength of mind this honest self-assessment produces — not brittle overconfidence, but a settled, unshakeable steadiness able to withstand real fear and real suffering without wavering — and offering to the Tathāgata (tenth) as fittingly placed last, since it draws together both material and dharma-offering into a single culminating act of reverence, quoting the Mahāprajñāpāramitā Sūtra's own declaration that "dharma-offering is called true offering, foremost among every kind of offering."

With all ten dharmas now named in summary, he notes, the fourth of the eight aspects distinguishing the abode of utmost joy reaches its complete conclusion — a first-ground bodhisattva's own concrete daily practice, cultivating faith, compassion, loving-kindness, and giving as the emotional foundation, tireless learning and worldly understanding as the intellectual foundation, and conscientious, firm, offering-minded conduct as the behavioral expression, together swiftly purifying this abode's qualities toward full perfection. The treatise turns next to the fifth aspect: being shown the way toward the remaining abodes still to come.

94. Section: The Fifth Aspect Opens and Closes — Learning the Remaining Nine Abodes' Own Conduct, Attainment, and Result, Becoming a Great Guide

The treatise now opens the fifth of the eight aspects: being shown the way toward the remaining abodes. Bodhisattvas, having received, practiced, and repeatedly cultivated these ten purifying dharmas, go on to seek out, single-mindedly and respectfully from the buddhas and bodhisattvas, every kind of path-quality and path-fault, every joy of spiritual power, and every unbroken path belonging to the remaining nine abodes — from the abode of heightened discipline through the abode of ultimate accomplishment — skillfully grasping their conduct, attainment, and outflowing result; by this, such a bodhisattva naturally advances through every abode, realizes great awakening, and becomes a great guide, leading every caravan of sentient beings across the perilous wilderness road of birth-and-death. The treatise then clarifies the three terms just used: conduct names the applied-effort wisdom by which one enters a given abode; attainment names the very moment of correctly entering it; and outflowing result names the complete, realized fruit once one has fully entered and its benefits are accomplished. With this clarification, the fifth aspect reaches its complete conclusion.

Chinese Source

申五、開曉餘住　酉一、善取其相　又諸菩薩，於此十法受學、隨轉、多修習已，復於餘九增上戒等諸菩薩住，從佛菩薩專精訪求一切種道功德、過失，及神通樂、無失壞道，善取其行、得、等流相，於一切住自然昇進，證大菩提，為大導師，率領一切有情商侶，超度生死曠野嶮道。

《披》一切種道功德過失等者：隨諸地中所有方便，是名一切種道。能治諸障，是名功德。彼所治障，是名過失。威力神變，隨欲自在，名神通樂。是道非道如實了知，不墮生死曠野嶮道，是名無失壞道。

酉二、釋行等名　當知此中，諸行能入，說名為行；若正入時，說名為得；入已果利成辦圓證，說名等流。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fifth aspect: being shown the way toward the remaining abodes; first, skillfully grasping their mark. Furthermore, bodhisattvas, having received, practiced, and repeatedly cultivated these ten dharmas, go on, toward the remaining nine abodes from heightened discipline onward, to seek out from the buddhas and bodhisattvas, single-mindedly and respectfully, every kind of path-quality, path-fault, joy of spiritual power, and unbroken path, skillfully grasping their conduct, attainment, and outflowing-result mark. In every abode, one naturally advances, realizes great awakening, and becomes a great guide, leading every caravan of sentient beings across the perilous wilderness road of birth-and-death.

Commentary: Regarding "every kind of path-quality, path-fault" and the like — the skillful means present in each ground is called every kind of path. Being able to remedy every obstruction is called quality. The very obstruction being remedied is called fault. Spiritual power and transformation, entirely at will, is called joy of spiritual power. Knowing truly what is the path and what is not the path, and so not falling into the perilous wilderness road of birth-and-death, is called the unbroken path.

Second, explaining the terms conduct and the rest. One should understand that within this, the applied effort by which one is able to enter is called conduct; the very moment of correctly entering is called attainment; the complete, realized fruit once one has entered is called outflowing result.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes the striking phrase "naturally advances" (自然昇進) here — a first-ground bodhisattva, having thoroughly mastered utmost joy's own conduct through the previous four aspects, now progresses through the remaining nine abodes with a kind of spontaneous momentum rather than through effortful striving alone, echoing the treatise's earlier remark that resolute-practice's own effortful deliberation gives way, from the first ground onward, to accomplishment that increasingly accords with one's own already-purified nature. He treats the image of "great guide leading a caravan across the perilous wilderness of birth-and-death" as deliberately vivid — sentient beings, like merchants risking a dangerous desert crossing, need someone who has already walked the entire route and knows every hazard, exactly what a bodhisattva progressing steadily through all ten grounds is being prepared to become.

He explains the threefold terminology — conduct, attainment, outflowing result — as a template the treatise will reuse whenever describing progress through any stage: conduct is the causal effort of applied practice that carries one toward a goal, attainment is the decisive moment of actually arriving there, and outflowing result is the settled, complete fruition that follows once one has fully arrived, the effects of that attainment now fully working themselves out. With this clarification, he notes, the fifth aspect closes complete, and the treatise turns next to the sixth of the eight aspects: cultivating and purifying wholesome roots, opening with two causes for actually beholding the buddhas in this very life.

95. Section: The Sixth Aspect Opens — Cultivating Wholesome Roots, Beginning With Two Causes for Beholding the Buddhas in This Very Life

The treatise now opens the sixth of the eight aspects: cultivating and purifying wholesome roots, organized first around pure conditions of three kinds — making offerings to the three jewels, bringing sentient beings to maturity, and dedicating merit toward awakening. Turning first to offerings to the three jewels, and within that first to the buddhas, the treatise poses the question of actually beholding buddhas in this present life, naming two causes that make this possible for a bodhisattva dwelling in this abode.

Chinese Source

申六、修治善根　酉一、舉淨緣　戌一、明一切　亥一、供養三寶　天一、於諸佛　地一、現見因緣　玄一、標　又諸菩薩住此住中，由二因緣現見諸佛。

Ru-Yi Translation

Sixth aspect: cultivating and purifying wholesome roots; first, presenting pure conditions; first, clarifying every case; first, making offerings to the three jewels; first, toward the buddhas; first, the cause of beholding them now; first, naming them. Furthermore, bodhisattvas dwelling in this abode, by two causes, behold the buddhas in this very life.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi previews the elaborate structure this sixth aspect will unfold across many coming sections: three pure conditions in total — offerings to the three jewels (itself subdivided into the buddhas, the sangha, and the true dharma), bringing sentient beings to maturity, and dedicating merit toward awakening — with the very first subsection, offerings to the buddhas, itself opening with the specific question of how a first-ground bodhisattva can actually behold living buddhas within this present life, rather than merely through faith or scriptural report.

He notes this reflects a distinctly Mahāyāna concern already anticipated in the treatise's own earlier account of the sixth of the ten great vows — journeying to buddha-lands to behold the tathāgatas directly — utmost joy being the very ground at which such direct encounter becomes genuinely possible rather than merely aspirational, since a first-ground bodhisattva has already realized the same suchness the buddhas themselves have fully realized. Having named that two such causes exist, he notes, the treatise turns next to explaining each of the two in turn.

96. Section: The Two Causes Explained — Rough Pure Faith, and the Power of a Correct Vow

The treatise now explains each of the two causes for beholding buddhas in this very life. The first is rough pure faith: whether through hearing what is proclaimed in the bodhisattva canon, or through an excellent understanding arisen from within, a bodhisattva believes that throughout the ten directions, in world-systems bearing every kind of different name, there dwell buddha-tathāgatas bearing every kind of different name, and, with a mind accompanied by rough pure faith, seeks to behold them directly — and, having sought in this way, genuinely has this wish fulfilled exactly as sought. This should be understood as the first cause. The second is the power of a correct vow: further arousing this correct vow in the mind — "wherever, among all these various world-systems, a buddha appears, I shall go and be born there" — and, having made this vow, genuinely has it fulfilled exactly as vowed. This should be understood as the second cause.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩住此住中，由二因緣現見諸佛。（十地經說有二種方便能見佛，一是以神通力見佛色身，一是以佛力見佛法身。）

黃一、由麤淨信　或由聽聞菩薩藏說，或由內心發起勝解，信有十方種種異名諸世界中，種種異名諸佛如來，由麤淨信俱行之心，求欲現見。如是求已，如實稱遂。當知是名第一因緣。

黃二、由正願力　又心發起如是正願：隨於彼彼諸世界中，有佛出現，我當往生。如是願已，如實稱遂。當知是名第二因緣。

Ru-Yi Translation

Furthermore, bodhisattvas dwelling in this abode, by two causes, behold the buddhas in this very life.

First, rough pure faith. Whether through hearing what is proclaimed in the bodhisattva canon, or through an excellent understanding arisen from within, believing that throughout the ten directions, in world-systems bearing every kind of different name, there dwell buddha-tathāgatas bearing every kind of different name, with a mind accompanied by rough pure faith, one seeks to behold them directly. Having sought in this way, this is genuinely fulfilled exactly as sought. One should understand this as the first cause.

Second, the power of a correct vow. Furthermore, arousing in the mind this correct vow: wherever, among these various world-systems, a buddha appears, I shall go and be born there. Having made this vow, this is genuinely fulfilled exactly as vowed. One should understand this as the second cause.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi cross-references the Ten Grounds Sutra's own two methods for beholding a buddha: beholding the buddha's own form-body through spiritual power (matching this treatise's first cause, rough pure faith bringing a buddha vividly present within meditative absorption), and beholding the buddha's own dharma-body through the buddha's own empowering grace (matching the second cause, vowing rebirth in whatever world a buddha appears and, through diligent cultivation of wholesome roots, actually arriving there to hear the dharma taught directly).

He explains the word "rough" carefully: a first-ground bodhisattva's own faith, though already far surpassing an ordinary being's, remains comparatively coarse next to the faith of bodhisattvas at the second ground and beyond — hence "rough pure faith" rather than simply "pure faith," a modest, precise qualification rather than any diminishment of genuine attainment. He notes that both causes converge on the same result — direct encounter with living buddhas, whether the buddha comes vividly to mind through faith-empowered absorption, or the bodhisattva goes to the buddha through vow-empowered rebirth — utmost joy being the ground where both avenues genuinely open. Having explained both causes for beholding buddhas, he notes, the treatise turns next to what a bodhisattva does once such buddhas are actually beheld: making offerings according to one's own capacity.

97. Section: Offerings According to Capacity, Closing Out Offerings to All Three Jewels

The treatise now explains what follows once buddhas are actually beheld: bodhisattvas, having thus beheld the buddhas through rough pure faith and through the power of a correct vow, having beheld them, arouse every kind of reverent offering according to their own power and capacity, respectfully presenting every manner of excellent, joyful offering-substance — the commentary here cross-referencing the Basic Section's own tenfold list of offerings from fascicle 44: offering relics, offering at a stūpa, offering in the buddha's actual presence, offering in the buddha's absence, offering performed oneself, offering taught to others, offering of wealth and reverence, vast offering, undefiled offering, and offering through right conduct. The treatise then turns to the remaining two jewels: toward the sangha, likewise offering with reverence — the robes, food, bedding, and medicine given to renunciates cultivating this same wholesome root; and toward the true dharma, hearing the true dharma taught in the buddha's presence, receiving and upholding it without distortion, and diligently practicing dharma in accordance with dharma. With this, offerings to all three jewels — buddha, sangha, and dharma — are complete.

Chinese Source

地二、隨興供養　菩薩如是由麤淨信現見諸佛，由正願力現見諸佛；既得見已，隨力隨能興一切種恭敬供養，奉施種種上妙樂具。

天二、於僧眾　及於僧眾恭敬供養。

天三、於正法　於如來所聽聞正法，無倒受持，精進修行法隨法行。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, offering according to capacity. Bodhisattvas, thus beholding the buddhas through rough pure faith and through the power of a correct vow, having beheld them, arouse every kind of reverent offering according to their own power and capacity, respectfully presenting every manner of excellent, joyful offering-substance.

Second, toward the sangha. And toward the sangha, offering with reverence.

Third, toward the true dharma. In the buddha's presence, hearing the true dharma, receiving and upholding it without distortion, diligently practicing dharma in accordance with dharma.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi supplies the full tenfold list of offering the commentary cross-references from fascicle 44's own chapter, asking how a bodhisattva makes offerings to the tathāgata: relic-offering, stūpa-offering, offering in actual presence, offering in absence, offering performed personally, offering taught to and performed by others, offering of wealth and reverence, vast offering, undefiled offering, and offering through right conduct — a comprehensive taxonomy covering every conceivable circumstance and motive for veneration.

He explains "dharma in accordance with dharma" precisely: on one reading, the first "dharma" is nirvana and the second, "in accordance with," is the eightfold noble path leading to it; on another, the first "dharma" is the sutras, vinaya, and treatises collectively, and the second is practicing in accordance with them; in the specifically Mahāyāna sense, given the treatise's own concern with the great, abiding-nowhere nirvana, "in accordance with dharma" points especially to the six perfections. Having now completed offerings to all three jewels — buddha, sangha, and dharma — as the first of three pure conditions cultivating wholesome roots, he notes, the treatise turns next to the second pure condition: maturing sentient beings through the four means of attraction.

98. Section: Maturing Sentient Beings and Dedicating All Merit — Closing All Three Pure Conditions

The treatise now completes the second and third of the three pure conditions cultivating wholesome roots. The second is maturing sentient beings: using the four means of attraction — giving, kind speech, beneficial conduct, and cooperation — to bring sentient beings' own faculties of faith, diligence, mindfulness, concentration, and wisdom to maturity, this practice likewise cultivating the bodhisattva's own wholesome roots and furthering entry into the ultimate truth. The third is dedicating merit toward awakening: every wholesome root accumulated — from reverent offerings to the three jewels, to hearing and upholding the true dharma without distortion, to maturing sentient beings through the four means of attraction — is entirely dedicated toward unsurpassed awakening, this dedication itself corresponding to the quiescent mark of the unattainable, a vast offering that causes the very merit being dedicated to grow ever greater still. With this, all three pure conditions — offerings to the three jewels, maturing sentient beings, and dedicating merit toward awakening — are complete.

Chinese Source

亥二、成熟有情　以四攝事成熟有情。

亥三、迴向菩提　一切善根悉皆迴向無上菩提。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, maturing sentient beings. Using the four means of attraction, maturing sentient beings.

Third, dedicating toward awakening. Every wholesome root is entirely dedicated toward unsurpassed awakening.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats the pairing of "maturing sentient beings" with "offerings to the three jewels" as deliberately symmetrical — the first pure condition is entirely self-benefiting (venerating the objects of one's own faith), the second entirely other-benefiting (bringing others' own faculties to fruition through the four familiar means of attraction), and the third, dedication, gathers both together and directs their combined merit toward the single, shared goal of unsurpassed awakening, itself performed in a manner "corresponding to the quiescent mark" — that is, without any grasping at a self who dedicates, a merit that is dedicated, or an awakening toward which it is dedicated, this very absence of grasping being precisely what makes the dedication "vast" and self-amplifying rather than finite and self-limiting.

With all three pure conditions now complete, he notes, this closes the first major subsection of the sixth aspect — cultivating wholesome roots — and the treatise turns next to a brief summary of their combined effect across an almost unimaginable span of time, followed by a vivid simile likening this entire process to a skilled craftsman smelting gold ore.

99. Section: The Summary and the Simile — Ore Refined by Fire, Closing the Entire Sixth Aspect

The treatise now draws these three pure conditions together into a summary, and illustrates their combined effect with a simile, closing the entire sixth aspect. By these three pure conditions, a bodhisattva's own wholesome roots grow doubly bright and pure: through offering to and being embraced by the buddha, sangha, and dharma; through maturing sentient beings with the four means of attraction; and through dedicating every wholesome root toward awakening — continuing in this way across immeasurable koṭis of nayutas of hundreds of thousands of great eons, vast units of time borrowed from Indian numerical convention to convey genuinely unimaginable duration. The treatise then offers its simile: just as a clever, skillful worldly craftsman places ore containing gold into a fire, and, the more it is smelted again and again, the more bright and pure it becomes, so too a bodhisattva of pure, excellent aspiration, through these same three pure conditions, has all such wholesome roots become ever more bright and pure in just this way. With this simile, the entire sixth aspect — cultivating and purifying wholesome roots — reaches its complete conclusion.

Chinese Source

戌二、出略義　由是三種清淨因緣，彼諸善根倍復明淨。謂於佛僧法供養攝受故，以四種攝事成熟有情故，以一切善根迴向菩提故。如是乃至無量俱胝那庾多百千大劫。

酉二、以喻顯　譬如世間黠慧工匠，以鑛性金置於火中，如如燒鍊，如是如是轉得明淨。如是淨勝意樂菩薩所有善根，由是三種清淨因緣，轉復明淨，當知亦爾。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, presenting the brief meaning. By these three pure conditions, one's own wholesome roots grow doubly bright and pure — namely, through offering to and being embraced by the buddha, sangha, and dharma; through maturing sentient beings with the four means of attraction; and through dedicating every wholesome root toward awakening. This continues on, even to immeasurable koṭis of nayutas of hundreds of thousands of great eons.

Second, illustrating with a simile. Just as, in the world, a clever, skillful craftsman places ore containing gold into a fire, and the more it is smelted again and again, the more bright and pure it becomes — so too, a bodhisattva of pure, excellent aspiration, whatever wholesome roots are possessed, by these three pure conditions, grow ever more bright and pure in just this way. One should understand it is just so.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi supplies the Indian numerical units named here: ten billion is called a lakṣa, ten lakṣa a koṭi, ten koṭi a nayuta — the compounded phrase "koṭis of nayutas of hundreds of thousands of great eons" conveying, deliberately, a span of time no listener could concretely picture, precisely the point, since a first-ground bodhisattva's own path to full buddhahood genuinely spans such immeasurable duration.

He treats the smelting simile as one of the treatise's most memorable images: the craftsman represents the bodhisattva's own skillful practice, the ore-bearing rock represents wholesome roots such as faith already present but still mixed with impurity, and the fire itself represents precisely these three pure conditions — offering, maturing beings, and dedication — repeated smelting causing the ore's own external dross to fall away and its inner gold to shine forth ever brighter, exactly as repeated practice causes a bodhisattva's own afflictive residue to fall away and inherent wisdom to shine forth ever more purely. With this simile, he notes, the sixth aspect — cultivating wholesome roots — reaches its complete conclusion. The treatise turns next to the seventh of the eight aspects: how a bodhisattva at utmost joy takes rebirth, most often as a wheel-turning king.

100. Section: The Seventh Aspect Opens and Closes — Rebirth as a Wheel-Turning King, and Constant, Correct Vows

The treatise now opens and completes the seventh of the eight aspects: how a bodhisattva at utmost joy takes rebirth. Dwelling in this abode, in whatever place of rebirth, such a bodhisattva most often becomes a wheel-turning king — specifically an iron-wheel king ruling Jambudvīpa, the southern continent — attaining great sovereignty, entirely free of any stain of miserliness, overawing sentient beings with majestic virtue while subduing their own miserly stinginess. Turning to what such a bodhisattva does once reborn as this king, the treatise explains that in every undertaking belonging to the four means of attraction, the mind never departs from the three jewels, always according with realized suchness, and thereby accomplishing an intention fixed on every kind of pure, unsurpassed awakening — abiding in great reverence, free of every deluded conceptual construction, dedicating everything done toward unsurpassed awakening. Such a bodhisattva constantly makes this vow: "I shall become most honored among all sentient beings, becoming the very support on which every sentient being's own highest benefit depends."

Chinese Source

申七、受生　酉一、多作輪王　又住於此，在在生處多作輪王，王贍部洲，得大自在。遠離一切所有慳垢，威被有情，調伏慳吝。

酉二、恆發正願　諸四攝事所作業中，一切不離佛法僧寶，證一切種菩提作意。

《披》諸四攝事所作業中等者：四種攝事方便所攝身語意業，是名諸四攝事之所作業。即於如是一切所作業中，念佛法僧，住大恭敬，除諸妄想，依此一切所作皆願迴向無上菩提，是名一切不離佛法僧寶，證一切種菩提作意。

恆發願言：我當一切有情中尊，作諸有情一切義利所依止處。

Ru-Yi Translation

Seventh aspect: taking rebirth; first, most often becoming a wheel-turning king. Furthermore, dwelling in this abode, in whatever place of rebirth, one most often becomes a wheel-turning king, ruling Jambudvīpa, attaining great sovereignty. Entirely free of every stain of miserliness, overawing sentient beings with majesty, subduing their own miserly stinginess.

Second, constantly making correct vows. In every undertaking belonging to the four means of attraction, never departing from the three jewels, accomplishing an intention fixed on every kind of awakening.

Commentary: Regarding "in every undertaking belonging to the four means of attraction" and the like — the bodily, verbal, and mental karma subsumed within the skillful means of the four means of attraction is called every undertaking belonging to the four means of attraction. Within all such undertakings, being mindful of the buddha, dharma, and sangha, abiding in great reverence, eliminating every deluded conceptual construction, dedicating everything done toward unsurpassed awakening — this is called never departing from the three jewels, accomplishing an intention fixed on every kind of awakening.

Constantly making this vow: I shall become most honored among all sentient beings, becoming the very support on which every sentient being's own highest benefit depends.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the classical fourfold hierarchy of wheel-turning kings this passage presupposes: a gold-wheel king rules all four continents, a silver-wheel king rules three, a copper-wheel king rules two, and an iron-wheel king rules only Jambudvīpa alone, the single continent human beings actually inhabit — a first-ground bodhisattva, though capable of far more, most often takes rebirth specifically at this most modest tier, ruling as a householder-king rather than as a renunciate, precisely so as to practice the bodhisattva path from within lay life, wielding worldly authority for the benefit of beings rather than for personal enjoyment.

He treats the sustained integration this passage describes — every act of giving, kind speech, beneficial conduct, and cooperation as king simultaneously remaining mindful of the three jewels, free of delusion, and dedicated toward awakening — as itself a mark of utmost joy's own genuine attainment: an ordinary ruler's generosity and diplomacy remain worldly, self-interested acts, whereas this same conduct, performed by a first-ground bodhisattva-king, becomes simultaneously an act of governance and an act of bodhisattva practice, the two no longer separable. With the constant vow to become the very support sentient beings depend on for their highest benefit, he notes, the entire seventh aspect — taking rebirth — reaches its complete conclusion. The treatise turns next to the eighth and final aspect distinguishing the abode of utmost joy: the majestic power such a bodhisattva now possesses, beginning with the power arising from diligence itself.

101. Section: The Eighth and Final Aspect Opens — Majestic Power Through Diligence, Renouncing the Throne to Attain a Hundred Samādhis

The treatise now opens the eighth and final aspect distinguishing the abode of utmost joy: majestic power, beginning with the power arising from diligence. Should such a bodhisattva-king delight in arousing this very diligence, relinquishing every family member, every treasure, and the throne itself, taking refuge in the buddha's own noble teaching and going forth with pure faith into monastic life, then in a single instant, in the mere flash of a moment, such a bodhisattva is able to realize a full hundred bodhisattva samādhis; with a pure divine eye, is able to behold a hundred tathāgatas across every kind of buddha-land; and, regarding these very buddhas' own miraculously sustaining and bodhisattvas' own sustaining power, is able to understand them completely — the treatise beginning here a list of what the text identifies as twelve such attainments accomplished in that same single instant.

Chinese Source

申八、威力　酉一、由精進力　若樂發起如是精進，棄捨一切家屬、財位，歸佛聖教，淨信出家。一剎那頃，瞬息須臾能證菩薩百三摩地。以淨天眼，能於種種諸佛國土見百如來。又即於彼變化住持、菩薩住持皆能解了。

Ru-Yi Translation

Eighth aspect: majestic power; first, the power arising from diligence. Should one delight in arousing this very diligence, relinquishing every family member and every treasure and throne, taking refuge in the buddha's noble teaching, going forth with pure faith into monastic life — in a single instant, the mere flash of a moment, one is able to realize a hundred bodhisattva samādhis. With a pure divine eye, one is able to behold a hundred tathāgatas across every kind of buddha-land. And regarding these very buddhas' own miraculous sustaining power and bodhisattvas' own sustaining power, one is able to understand them completely.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this opening as a dramatic narrative turn: the very wheel-turning king just described in the seventh aspect, ruling Jambudvīpa in great worldly splendor, now renounces every bit of that same splendor — family, wealth, throne entirely relinquished — the moment genuine diligence for the path itself arises, entering monastic life out of pure faith rather than worldly disillusionment. He emphasizes the speed the text insists upon: not gradually, but in a single instant, the mere flash of a moment, such renunciation yields the first two of what the treatise will enumerate as twelve total attainments — a hundred samādhis realized, and a hundred buddhas beheld directly across a hundred buddha-lands through a purified divine eye, together with complete understanding of both a buddha's own miraculous sustaining power and a bodhisattva's own sustaining power.

He notes that this vivid image — a supreme worldly ruler simply walking away from the throne toward monastic diligence, and immediately reaping attainments no ordinary practitioner could achieve in many lifetimes — dramatizes just how much spiritual capital a first-ground bodhisattva has already accumulated through the preceding seven aspects; the wheel-turning kingship was never the point, only a temporary, skillful vehicle for benefiting beings while awaiting the moment such renunciation and diligence would bear their full fruit. Having introduced the first two of twelve total attainments, he notes, the treatise turns next to enumerating the remaining ten, completing this final, capstone aspect of the abode of utmost joy.

102. Section: The Remaining Ten Attainments — Shaking a Hundred Worlds, a Hundred Bodies, Each With a Hundred Bodhisattva Retinues

The treatise now completes the full twelve attainments accomplished in that same single instant of renunciation and diligence. With spiritual power, such a bodhisattva is able to shake a hundred world-systems, and the body itself is likewise able to journey there; is able to emit great light, illuminating all around, universally causing others to see; is able to transform into a hundred kinds of appearance, bringing a hundred kinds of beings to maturity; if wishing to prolong life, is able to remain in the world for a hundred eons; regarding matters spanning a hundred eons both past and future, wisdom and vision are able to penetrate them; and, within the dharma-gateways of the aggregates, elements, and sense-fields, is able to correctly discern a hundred such dharma-gateways. Finally, such a bodhisattva is able to manifest as a hundred bodies, and in each and every one of those hundred bodies, is able to display a retinue of a hundred bodhisattvas surrounding it. With this, the twelve attainments arising from the power of diligence — a hundred samādhis, seeing a hundred buddhas, understanding the sustaining power of both buddhas and bodhisattvas, shaking a hundred worlds, journeying to a hundred worlds, illuminating a hundred worlds, transforming into a hundred kinds to mature a hundred kinds of beings, remaining a hundred eons, penetrating a hundred eons past and future, discerning a hundred dharma-gateways, manifesting a hundred bodies, and displaying a hundred-fold retinue around each — are complete.

Chinese Source

以神通力動百世界，身亦能往。放大光明周匝遍照，普令他見。化為百類，成熟百種所化有情。若欲留命，能住百劫。於前後際各百劫事，智見能入。蘊、界、處等諸法門中，於百法門能正思擇。化作百身，身身皆能現百菩薩眷屬圍繞。

《披》變化住持菩薩住持者：謂變化土、受用土如次應知。

Ru-Yi Translation

With spiritual power, shaking a hundred world-systems, the body also able to go there. Emitting great light, illuminating all around, universally causing others to see. Transforming into a hundred kinds, bringing a hundred kinds of beings to be transformed to maturity. If wishing to prolong life, able to remain for a hundred eons. Regarding matters of a hundred eons in both past and future, wisdom and vision are able to penetrate them. Within the dharma-gateways of the aggregates, elements, sense-fields, and the rest, able to correctly discern a hundred such dharma-gateways. Transforming into a hundred bodies, each and every body able to display a retinue of a hundred bodhisattvas surrounding it.

Commentary: Regarding "the sustaining power of transformation, the sustaining power of bodhisattvas" — this means the transformation-land and the land of actual enjoyment, respectively, which should be understood in this order.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi numbers all twelve attainments explicitly for clarity: first, realizing a hundred samādhis; second, seeing a hundred tathāgatas (a hundred buddhas across a hundred great trichiliocosms, each such world-system home to exactly one buddha); third, understanding the transformation-land and enjoyment-land such buddhas maintain (the commentary specifying that "transformation-land" refers to the shared land where ordinary beings and noble ones dwell together, appropriate to a buddha's emanated body, while "enjoyment-land" refers to the land of true reward, appropriate to a buddha's own dharma-body and responsive body — mind purified, land purified, a land's own purity or defilement ultimately manifesting mind alone); fourth, shaking a hundred worlds; fifth, the body itself journeying to a hundred worlds via spiritual feet; sixth, light illuminating a hundred worlds so all beings there can see the bodhisattva; seventh, transforming into a hundred kinds of appearance to mature a hundred kinds of beings' own wholesome roots; eighth, remaining in the world for a hundred eons at will, sovereign over one's own lifespan; ninth, wisdom penetrating without obstruction the affairs of a hundred eons past, present, and future alike; tenth, correctly discerning a hundred dharma-gateways among the five aggregates, twelve sense-fields, and eighteen elements, each a different gateway into ultimate truth; eleventh, manifesting a hundred bodies; and twelfth, each of those hundred bodies itself displaying a hundred bodhisattvas as its own surrounding retinue.

He treats this entire twelvefold list as concrete, almost dazzlingly literal evidence for just how much a single instant of monastic renunciation accomplishes once genuine diligence and prior accumulated merit converge — every single attainment measured in the same unit, a hundred, driving home that this is merely the beginning tier of spiritual power, destined only to multiply further at each successive ground. With all twelve attainments arising from the power of diligence now complete, he notes, the treatise turns next to a second source of majestic power distinct from diligence: the power arising from a correct vow itself.

103. Section: Immeasurable Power From Vow-Force Alone — Closing the Eighth and Final Aspect Entirely

The treatise now explains the second source of majestic power: the power arising from a correct vow. From this point onward, one should understand that bodhisattvas, purely by the power of their own vow, are able to accomplish immeasurable majestic, spiritually transformative power — bodhisattvas abiding in this very abode of utmost joy, through the heightened power of their assembled vows, are able to draw forth countless kinds of transformation performed through immeasurable, excellent, correct vows; and by these correct vows, continuing even across koṭis of nayutas of hundreds of thousands of great eons, the resulting transformations become genuinely impossible to number. The commentary clarifies that spiritual transformations arising from the sovereign power of one's own vow far exceed, in number, everything just enumerated through the power of diligence — hence "immeasurable." With this, the entire eighth and final aspect distinguishing the abode of utmost joy — majestic power, whether through diligence or through vow — reaches its complete conclusion.

Chinese Source

酉二、由正願力　自茲以去，是諸菩薩由願力故，當知無量威力神變，安住如是極歡喜住諸菩薩眾願力增上，能引無量殊勝正願所作神變。如是正願，乃至俱胝那庾多百千大劫，不易可數。

《披》由願力故當知無量威力神變者：謂諸菩薩，若由願力自在增上，所作神變，過於前數不可數知，故言無量。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, the power arising from a correct vow. From this point onward, these bodhisattvas, by the power of their vow, should be understood to accomplish immeasurable majestic, spiritually transformative power. Bodhisattvas abiding in this abode of utmost joy, through the heightened power of their vows, are able to draw forth immeasurable, excellent, correct vows' own transformations. Such correct vows, continuing even across koṭis of nayutas of hundreds of thousands of great eons, become genuinely difficult to number.

Commentary: Regarding "by the power of their vow, immeasurable majestic, spiritually transformative power should be understood" — bodhisattvas, if by the sovereign, heightened power of their own vow, produce transformations that, in number, exceed everything enumerated before and cannot be counted, hence it is said to be immeasurable.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi contrasts these two sources of power directly: diligence-power yields a precisely enumerated, if already extraordinary, twelvefold set of attainments, each measured in hundreds — a concrete, countable achievement; vow-power, by contrast, yields transformations the text itself declares to be beyond counting, since a bodhisattva's own vow, as already explained several milestones earlier, is itself endless, modeled on the Avataṃsaka's declaration that the vow only ends when empty space, sentient beings, their karma, and their afflictions are all exhausted — an endless cause naturally producing an endless, unnumbered effect.

With majestic power now explained through both diligence and vow, he notes, the eighth and final aspect distinguishing the abode of utmost joy reaches its complete conclusion — and with it, all eight aspects together (well-determined vow, arising bodhicitta, giving rise to a well-determined vow, purifying dharmas, being shown the remaining abodes, cultivating wholesome roots, taking rebirth, and majestic power) that have occupied the treatise since the abode of utmost joy first opened many milestones ago. The treatise turns next to a concluding summary drawing all eight aspects together under their own names.

104. Section: The Summary Names All Eight Aspects Together — Closing the Brief Answer for the Abode of Utmost Joy

The treatise now closes the brief answer covering the entire abode of utmost joy with a concluding summary, naming all eight aspects together by their own titles: this, in brief, is the bodhisattva's abode of utmost joy — namely, because of a well-determined vow; because of the fourfold mark of arising bodhicitta; because of arousing diligence and giving rise to a correct vow; because of dharmas purifying the abode; because of being shown the way toward the remaining abodes; because of cultivating wholesome roots; because of taking rebirth; and because of majestic power. With every one of these eight aspects now named together, the entire brief answer to the original question — what conduct, state, and mark characterizes a bodhisattva at the abode of utmost joy — reaches its complete conclusion.

Chinese Source

未二、結略義　當知是名略說菩薩極歡喜住。謂善決定故，四相發心故，發起精進引發正願故，淨修住法故，開曉餘住故，修治善根故，受生故，威力故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, closing the brief meaning. One should understand this, in brief, as the bodhisattva's abode of utmost joy — namely, because of a well-determined vow; because of the fourfold mark of arising bodhicitta; because of arousing diligence and giving rise to a correct vow; because of dharmas purifying the abode; because of being shown the way toward the remaining abodes; because of cultivating wholesome roots; because of taking rebirth; because of majestic power.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks back through all eight aspects one final time as a capstone review: well-determined vow (the six marks — new well-determination, inwardly realized cultivated nature, the bodhisattva's great vow, peerless, uncommon, unchanging-inexhaustible — mapped onto the Ten Grounds Sutra Commentary's own six well-determinations); the fourfold arising of bodhicitta (cause, object of concern, state and mark, benefit); giving rise to a correct vow (the ten great vows, from offering to buddhas through swiftly realizing awakening, plus the endless further vow); dharmas purifying the abode (all ten, from pure faith through offering to the Tathāgata); being shown the way toward the remaining abodes; cultivating wholesome roots (the three pure conditions and the gold-smelting simile); taking rebirth (most often as an iron-wheel king); and majestic power (twelve attainments from diligence, immeasurable transformations from vow).

He treats this summary as confirming just how exhaustively the treatise has now treated a single ground — the first of ten — suggesting, by simple proportion, how much material the remaining nine grounds might contain if treated with anything like comparable thoroughness (though, as the very next section will explain, the treatise itself chooses brevity from here, referring readers elsewhere for full detail). With the brief answer now fully closed, he notes, the treatise turns to its second and final major division answering the opening question: a broader pointer toward where fuller detail on all ten grounds may be found.

105. Section: Pointing to the Ten Grounds Sutra for Full Detail, and Explaining Why "Ground" and "Abode" Are Both Used — Closing the Entire Abode of Utmost Joy

The treatise now closes its entire treatment of the abode of utmost joy with a final, broader pointer and an etymological clarification. Should one wish to know the fuller, more extensive account, the treatise directs the reader to the Ten Grounds Sutra's own chapter on the ground of utmost joy — noting that what the Ten Grounds Sutra expounds at length as the bodhisattva's ten grounds is exactly what this text, belonging to the bodhisattva canon's own mātṛkā, the root-matrix of wisdom, expounds in brief as the bodhisattva's thirteen abodes, the same sequence running from utmost joy through the abode of ultimate accomplishment. Turning to a final clarification, the treatise explains why two different terms — ground and abode — are both used for this same reality: because it is able to hold and sustain everything belonging to a bodhisattva's own meaning and merit, it is called a ground; and because it serves as the very dwelling-place a bodhisattva actually enjoys and makes use of, it is called an abode. With this etymological note, the entire second of the eleven-part deep-dive — the abode of utmost joy in its complete eightfold analysis — reaches its full and final conclusion.

Chinese Source

午二、廣指　未一、指配所說　若廣宣說，如十地經極喜地說。彼十地經廣所宣說菩薩十地，即是此中菩薩藏攝摩怛理迦略所宣說菩薩十住。如其次第，從極歡喜住，乃至最上成滿菩薩住。

未二、釋地住名　應知此中，由能攝持菩薩義故，說名為地；能為受用居處義故，說名為住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, the broader pointer; first, pointing to the corresponding account. Should one wish to hear it expounded at length, it is exactly as the Ten Grounds Sutra expounds it in its chapter on the ground of utmost joy. What the Ten Grounds Sutra expounds at length as the bodhisattva's ten grounds is exactly what this text, subsumed within the bodhisattva canon's own root-matrix, expounds in brief as the bodhisattva's ten abodes. In their proper sequence, from the abode of utmost joy through the abode of ultimate accomplishment.

Second, explaining the terms ground and abode. One should understand that within this, because it is able to hold and sustain what belongs to a bodhisattva's own meaning, it is called a ground; because it serves as the dwelling-place one actually enjoys and makes use of, it is called an abode.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the significance of "mātṛkā," transliterated and glossed in the commentary tradition as "root-matrix" or simply "treatise" — a mātṛkā is called the mother of wisdom, because deeply hearing, reflecting on, and studying it gives rise to immeasurable, boundless wisdom in turn; this treatise, the Yogācārabhūmi-śāstra, is precisely such a root-matrix, here explicitly choosing brevity over the Ten Grounds Sutra's own much fuller exposition of the same ten grounds, while directing any reader who wants the fuller account to go there directly.

He treats the closing etymological note — ground (地) because it holds and sustains a bodhisattva's own merit the way earth holds and sustains all growing things, abode (住) because it names the actual dwelling a bodhisattva inhabits and makes genuine use of — as the treatise's own tidy explanation for why it freely uses both terms interchangeably throughout this entire chapter, "grounds" when following the Ten Grounds Sutra's own terminology and "abodes" when following this text's own thirteen-stage framework, the two words pointing at exactly the same reality from two complementary angles.

With this etymological clarification, he notes, the entire second part of the eleven-part granular analysis — the abode of utmost joy, examined across all eight aspects from the well-determined vow through majestic power — reaches its full and complete conclusion, a session-spanning achievement matching in scale the earlier completion of the entire eighteen-chapter initial yoga foundation. Fascicle 47's own source material is now fully exhausted at this point; the treatise's continued examination of the third of the eleven-part analysis — the abode of stainlessness, the second ground — together with the remaining eight abodes, will continue in fascicle 48.
